GM to pay $220 million after selling customer Smart Driver data
2026-10-30

GM to pay $220 million after selling customer Smart Driver data

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
General Motors marketed its Smart Driver system as a way for parents to monitor teen drivers or for adults to track their driving behavior. But in 2024, it emerged that GM had been sharing that data with brokers. Those brokers then sold the data to insurance companies, and some drivers found that their insurance became more expensive as a result. To make matters worse, many of those drivers claimed they had no knowledge of ever agreeing to share such data for such purposes. Last year, GM and its OnStar subsidiary entered into a settlement with the FTC, prohibiting them from sharing some kinds of driver data with third parties. The settlement was finalized this January. That agreement banned the two companies from sharing geolocation data or driver behavior data for five years. At the time, GM also discontinued Smart Driver across its entire range. Now the automaker has reached terms with the District of Columbia and 35 states after their attorneys general joined together to sue GM as well. GM told Reuters in a statement that “[s]afer, more helpful and personalized experiences are enabled by connected vehicle data, and GM is committed to being clear about what information is used, how ⁠it’s protected, and the choices customers have about what they share.” According to TechCrunch, the settlement with the states is notable in that it doesn’t take GM’s word that it’s complying with the privacy policy—a third party will be required to make that determination.

Hackers steal $6.3 billion from Binance in record crypto heist
2026-10-30

Hackers steal $6.3 billion from Binance in record crypto heist

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Binance has suspended all withdrawals after the world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange fell victim to what appears to be the biggest heist in history. The exchange put the “estimated total impact” of the hack at about $6.3 billion and said around $3 billion of affected assets, mainly stablecoins that act as cash for the crypto market, had been frozen. But analysts at Elliptic said the attacker had changed about $3.3 billion into digital assets that could not be frozen and was working to cash those out as traditional currency. An “initial investigation” suggested hackers had been able to compromise an engineer’s laptop and withdraw funds from the platform, Binance said, adding on Friday morning that further investigation “has uncovered additional points of breach.” The company is working with law enforcement. Blockchain intelligence firm TRM Labs linked the hack with a North Korean unit known as TraderTraitor, saying that the sums involved would make it “the largest crypto theft on record.” Last year, North Korean hackers stole crypto worth $1.5 billion in an attack on exchange Bybit. Peter Ward, of the Sejong Institute in Seoul, said North Korea had considerable expertise and offered “near-total protection to its hackers.” “Things may get a lot worse going forward,” he said. Binance said on Friday that it had “already reserved over $6.34 billion of company funds to cover this incident,” with $5 billion of the funds “already held in the exact token assets that were impacted.” The company said it was actively buying more tokens in order to cover the remaining amount. “This step can take longer because some tokens may have low liquidity,” and settlement “also takes time,” it said. The hack is a major blow to Binance, which was co-founded in 2017 by Changpeng Zhao and holds about $140 billion of digital assets. In 2023, the company pleaded guilty to US criminal charges related to breach of anti-money laundering laws and paid a $4.3 billion fine. Zhao, known as CZ, pleaded guilty to criminal charges relating to inadequate money laundering controls. He stepped down as chief executive and was pardoned last year by US President Donald Trump. Bitcoin fell about 4 percent in the hours after the hack emerged late on Thursday before regaining ground to trade above $78,000. The crypto market is in the grip of a prolonged downturn after a sharp sell-off late last year wiped more than $1tn from the combined capitalization of about 18,000 digital tokens. Richard Teng, co-chief executive of Binance, told customers that their “money is safe, and we’re covering every loss” from a reserve fund as well as “company funds.” Binance’s reserve fund holds about $1 billion worth of bitcoin, according to a company update from mid-October. Zhao said this leaves roughly $2 billion that the company still needs to cover, adding that the company owns shares in Elon Musk’s SpaceX worth more than $1 billion. “Remaining gap is (still painful, but) manageable,” he said in a post on X. Binance co-chief executive Yi He said on Friday that the company’s annual revenue “is sufficient to cover the losses,” without giving further details. CZ said earlier this year that the company’s annual revenue is about $5 billion. Crypto venues have become lucrative targets for hackers, with notable heists including $280 million stolen in April from the coffers of unregulated crypto exchange Drift. Unlike traditional exchanges, crypto venues sit on big piles of customer assets. Some institutional investors in recent years have sought third-party custody arrangements in order to keep their funds elsewhere and mitigate the risks of their assets being stolen directly off the exchange. Binance works with Swiss banks Sygnum and Flow, the FT previously reported. Abu Dhabi’s Financial Services Regulatory Authority, which regulates Binance’s main global crypto exchange, said in a statement on Friday that it was aware of the “security incident involving unauthorized withdrawals of assets” from the company’s exchange and was “engaging closely with Binance.” Binance was ordered to wind down its EU operations in July after it failed to secure a license under the bloc’s new regulatory framework. The exchange, which became regulated in Abu Dhabi in December 2025, had already agreed to block UK customers from signing up for the exchange after being barred by Britain’s financial watchdog in 2021. In the US, Binance operates under a separate entity from the rest of its global operations that is registered with the Financial Crimes Enforcement Network and authorized to operate as a money transmitter in 32 states. Last month, nearly $390 million was stolen from crypto exchange Bitget, which reimbursed customers from its so-called User Protection Fund. North Korean hackers stole about $2 billion in cryptocurrency in 2025, a 50 percent increase from the year before, according to data from Chainalysis. Binance said it found no evidence that unique user codes, known as “private keys,” had been breached. In a post on X early on Friday, Yi He said the exchange expected to allow withdrawals again “in the next 48 hours.” Binance, which has no official headquarters, last year became regulated in Abu Dhabi, where Zhao and He, who are romantic partners, are based. In a sign of the potential repercussions from the heist, Paolo Ardoino, chief executive of Tether, said his company had frozen about $1.5 billion of USDT, its $180 billion dollar-linked stablecoin. Jeremy Allaire, head of Circle, the second-biggest stablecoin issuer, said $800 million of its USDC coin had been frozen amid “reports of a potential exploit” at Binance. Crypto companies can freeze the wallets in which stolen funds are moved or held. Analytics firm Elliptic said it had verified $2.8 billion of frozen crypto—mostly USDT and USDC—but that the hackers had moved the remainder into assets that could not be frozen, such as the ETH crypto token. “This is almost certainly the largest theft of any kind in history,” Elliptic said. Additional reporting by Daniel Tudor in Seoul © 2026 The Financial Times Ltd. All rights reserved. Not to be redistributed, copied, or modified in any way.

2027 Nissan Rogue Hybrid: Drives like an EV, but there's no plug involved
2026-10-30

2027 Nissan Rogue Hybrid: Drives like an EV, but there's no plug involved

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Nissan has a new version of its best-selling Rogue SUV, and it’s launching the model here with the latest version of its e-Power hybrid system, which Ars tested back in February. Most of the hybrid cars you can buy today are parallel hybrids, which means that they have an internal combustion engine and an electric motor, both of which drive some or all of the wheels—everything works in parallel. But there’s another approach. Instead of connecting the engine to the wheels via a transmission, it can act as a generator and charge the traction battery, which in turn powers the electric motor. Since the engine and motor work in series, this is known as a series hybrid. That’s mostly how the Chevrolet Volt worked. As does Honda’s hybrid CR-V. Well, mostly; engineers worked out it was more efficient for those cars’ gasoline engines to directly drive the wheels at highway speeds, something that to this day makes a certain subset of hybrid car enthusiast irrationally angry. If you know anyone like that in your life, send them to wherever the nearest Nissan dealership is, because the 2027 Rogue Hybrid is exactly what they’ve been asking for. Under the hood, with its crisp shut line and slim horizontal lights that give me something of a Range Rover vibe, there’s a 1.5 L, three-cylinder turbocharged direct-injection engine that generates 148 hp (110 kW). It only revs to 5,200 rpm because it’s optimized to charge the battery, not cope with the various loads of an engine connected to a gearbox and the wheels. That also allows the engine to operate at a rather impressive 42 percent thermal efficiency—we would need to look at Formula 1 engines to find higher. This charges a 1.67 kWh lithium-ion traction battery that has been optimized for its charge and discharge ability, which in turn powers a pair of electric motors. At the front axle sits a 202 hp (150 kW), 243 lb-ft (330 Nm) permanent synchronous magnet motor, at the rear another PSM providing 134 hp (100 kW) and 144 lb-ft (195 Nm), although given the battery’s limitations, combined power output is 225 hp (168 kW). And as you would expect, the motors will regeneratively brake under deceleration to return as much energy to the battery for reuse as possible. Although this is the first time Nissan has offered the e-Power hybrid system here in the US, it has been on sale in Japan for a decade now, with more than 1.4 million cars on the road in that time. Those versions weren’t considered quite sufficient for our roads, so Nissan retuned the system for the Rogue Hybrid to be able to cope with harder acceleration and bigger vehicles. From behind the wheel, you could very easily forget this wasn’t just a new EV—a replacement for the gone-too-soon Ariya, perhaps. Throttle tip-in is immediate, like any good electric car, and there’s enough torque to get you on your way without any fuss, even if a 0–60 mph (97 km/h) time of 6.4 seconds isn’t particularly fast by 2026 standards. Nissan has had plenty of expertise in calibrating one-pedal driving setups, and when you access the Rogue Hybrid’s by pushing a button on the center console, you’ll find it’s no different here. So keen was Nissan to demonstrate the smoothness with which the powertrain decelerates under regen braking that it put us through a mini school-run obstacle course, complete with a fishbowl full of water mounted to the cupholder. Nary a drop was spilled, versus the same test in what might be the Rogue Hybrid’s greatest rival, Toyota’s hybrid RAV4. (The RAV4, with no one-pedal driving, requires you to use the brake pedal to slow and stop.) Acceleration is good at highway speeds, and cruising is quiet—on a wet and windy day it was the gusts we heard, rather than wind noise generated by the car. Speaking of generators, you don’t really hear the engine running much either. It never revs particularly high and shuts off whenever possible—with music playing on the infotainment system, the occupants might not even notice. The ride absorbed bumps on the road well, but it didn’t absorb sharp transient shocks as well as it did those of longer duration. A combination of low-rolling resistance tires and Californian roads that were seeing their first rain in some time conspired to create rather a lot of understeer as I entered a roundabout with perhaps a touch more vigor than normal. That said, I rather liked the Rogue Hybrid’s steering feel, and together with the smooth power delivery from the two electric motors and the easy one-pedal driving mode, my time behind the wheel was most pleasant. The Rogue Hybrid SR we drove is rated at 41 mpg (5.7 L/100 km) in the city, 36 mpg (6.5 L/100 km) highway, and a combined 38 mpg (6.2 L/100 km), not quite as good as the RAV4 hybrid but still competitive with the segment. The Rogue Hybrid provided a commendable amount of storage space, and I was glad to see so many physical controls. As our car was fitted with the Tech package, it included a larger 14.3-inch touchscreen infotainment system running Android Automotive OS with google automotive services. Wireless Android Auto and Apple CarPlay are available with both this and the standard 12.3-inch system, and I was surprised to discover that the Rogue Hybrid would show Google Maps navigation from an iPhone through CarPlay on the main instrument display in front of the driver—usually only Apple Maps (and Waze iirc) appear on this screen, with Google Maps relegated to the infotainment display. Seat comfort has long been a Nissan obsession, and its work on its “Zero Gravity” seats has paid off, as a day of driving did not result in a fatigued driver. The stitching and design of the seats was inspired by cozy puffer jackets, although I think the charcoal interior would be a better idea for life with a family or animals than the light gray of our test car. Initially, Nissan will just have a single flavor of Rogue Hybrid for sale, the SR with the Tech Package, which will cost $41,490. In January, it will add the $35,490 SV and the $43,490 Platinum trims to the mix, prices that are benchmarked against the aforementioned Toyota. And for those who think you’ll only be able to find the most expensive ones at the dealership, Nissan says it expects the mix to be 30 percent SV, 50 percent SR, 20 percent Platinum.

Review: You Can See Everything blurs the view of Elizabeth Holmes' deceit
2026-10-30

Review: You Can See Everything blurs the view of Elizabeth Holmes' deceit

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
In 2015, biomedical entrepreneur Elizabeth Holmes was Silicon Valley’s golden girl, lauded as the youngest and richest self-made female billionaire thanks to the company she founded, Theranos, which was valued at $9 billion at the height of its success. Then it all came crashing down when accusations of fraud surfaced about the company’s signature blood-testing device, culminating in a sensational trial. Holmes was convicted on four counts and sentenced to 11 years in prison. Holmes is back in the headlines thanks to a new film, You Can See Everything, which depicts the final days before Holmes, having exhausted her appeals, went to prison. Co-directed by Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim, it’s a surreal, absurdist piece that has largely been received with gushing enthusiasm and no small amount of hype, with some declaring it the best film of the year. A few contrarian voices have emerged over the past week with much-needed reality checks, but there’s no denying the film is destined to be an awards-season darling. Pardon me for joining the buzzkills, but I cannot in good conscience jump on the You Can See Everything bandwagon. Sure, it has its moments, and the second half is highly entertaining, but there’s nothing here to warrant all the breathless hyperbole. For all their much-touted access, Oppenheim and Fielder have produced a surprisingly shallow film that runs in circles, yielding very little fresh or novel insight—into Holmes, personally, or much else. I get that manufacturing ambiguity is the much-ballyhooed point. I get that much of the film’s appeal lies in how much fun people have afterward vigorously debating their interpretations of key scenes. But there’s no real mystery here. Holmes is still spouting all the same talking points and self-justifications, still playing the victim, dodging accountability, and affixing blame on anyone except herself, just as she did all the way through her trial. The only mystery is why a convicted fraudster is still deemed worthy of our attention. tl;dr: Elizabeth Holmes is back on her bulls—t, and some people are falling for the act all over again. (Spoilers below the first gallery. We’ll give you a heads-up when we get there.) There is already an excellent HBO documentary about Holmes and Theranos by Oscar-winning director Alex Gibney: The Inventor: Out for Blood in Silicon Valley, a companion piece to journalist John Carreyrou’s 2018 book, Bad Blood. (I reviewed Gibney’s latest, Musk, earlier this month.) Holmes was also the focus of the 2022 Hulu fictionalized miniseries (and associated podcast) The Dropout, starring Amanda Seyfried as Holmes. None showed Holmes how she clearly wished to be perceived, so she reached out to A24 about making a documentary to tell her side of the story. You will be shocked to hear that Holmes believes she is innocent and thought all she needed to do was “speak her truth” in order to convince everyone else, too. It didn’t work on a jury of her peers—who deemed her the least credible witness of the entire trial—but apparently it worked on Fielder. At least he’s in good company: Holmes fooled a lot of smart, savvy people for years before a pair of whistleblowers (Tyler Shultz and Erika Cheung) exposed her lies. Fielder, for those unfamiliar with his work, is a comedian, best known for his Comedy Central series (currently streaming on HBO) Nathan for You. Fielder played a fictionalized version of himself as a recent business graduate who “consulted” for struggling local businesses in LA. The gimmick: Fielder’s advice, offered in his trademark deadpan style, was deliberately weird, such as suggesting an ice cream store offer a poop flavor to generate headlines and draw customers. In another episode, he faked a video, which actually went viral, of a pig rescuing a goat at the local petting zoo. Fielder was also an executive producer on the (excellent) HBO series How To With John Wilson before creating the docu-comedy, The Rehearsal, in which another heightened reality version of Fielder helps people prepare for difficult conversations by re-creating the conditions and environments in which those conversations will take place, frequently building extravagant sets to do so. If cringe humor is your thing (think Sasha Baron Cohen, with whom Fielder has also worked), then you’ll likely find Fielder’s schtick appealing—and you’ll be far more likely to love You Can See Everything. Still, at first glance, he was a puzzling choice to co-direct this film, as was Oppenheim, who calls himself a “docu-fantasist.” The two men met at some point, became friends, and wanted to work on a project together; Holmes and current partner Billy Evans were Fielder fans. Thus, You Can See Everything was born. Fielder and Oppenheim don’t spend much time rehashing the Theranos debacle and Holmes’ subsequent trial and conviction, which is fine; that has been covered exhaustively elsewhere, including the Gibney documentary, and it’s not what they were interested in capturing. They’re more keen on the vibes than the facts. Holmes and Evans invited Fielder and the film crew to stay with them for the 34 days before her imprisonment, and given the strangeness of their initial interactions, Fielder became a major on-camera presence in the film. The first half of the film is on the dull side, featuring lots of footage of Fielder and Holmes staring at each other, unblinking, in an attempt to convince each other of their sincerity. (Jimmy Fallon and Jamie Lee Curtis hilariously spoofed this dynamic in a cold open for The Tonight Show.) There is a tiresome segment quibbling over the relative meanings of the words “can” and “could.” Another segment dissects whether or not Holmes is capable of understanding the humor behind the classic “Who’s on First” sketch. Fielder’s deadpan comedic style is used to good effect. Any laughs in that first half come from his reaction shots, such as his expression when Holmes insists, blue eyes extra wide, that she has never once lied in her entire life. And it’s just innately funny seeing him pop up again and again at the edges of the frame, like an awkwardly diffident stalker. Much has been made of the scene where Evans insists Fielder film him working out at the gym—including a post-workout shower scene with Evans faux-nonchalantly displaying side-frontal nudity. It’s nothing we haven’t seen before; HBO flashes its viewers all the time. Still, I can never unsee Evans’ flaccid junk, so thanks for that image being burned into my retinas, guys. But it fits the sleight-of-hand theme Fielder and Oppenheimer develop throughout the film. On the one hand, we are literally seeing everything. On the other, it is only the illusion of transparency, because Evans is so relentlessly squirrely and evasive—when not outright combative—about answering Fielder’s questions. Frankly, Holmes and Evans just aren’t sufficiently interesting to carry a three-hour film, which is why Amanda Seyfried is such a breath of fresh air when she turns up for the second half to help Fielder and Oppenheim work around the physical absence of their primary subject. Seyfried plays Holmes in several carefully staged re-enactments—a fascinating window into the actor’s process—but she also becomes Fielder’s sounding board and (at times) co-conspirator. The film comes to life when she appears, akin to tossing a firecracker into a lackluster dinner party. Seyfried, for instance, goads Evans into revealing that he has power of attorney and manages most activity on Holmes’ X account. She challenges Fielder about being reluctant to include footage that might show Holmes in a negative light because he’s come to care about her. And the film doesn’t let Seyfried off the hook either, forcing her to confront how her portrayal in The Dropout might have been painful for Holmes, despite clearly being fictionalized to allow for taking a few liberties with the facts. Ars Technica senior editor Sam Axon,  who loved the film, describes it as “an absurdist reality show” or a “post-postmodern comedy rather than an interesting documentary” that is “more for fans of Nathan Fielder than for people interested in Theranos.” Fair enough. But it has been marketed as a documentary. That label carries certain expectations. Yes, Fielder and Oppenheim are deliberately (and skillfully) thwarting those expectations, but to what end? Savvy consumers already know that documentaries frame their subjects in specific ways. Gibney, for one, unapologetically makes documentaries that espouse his personal point of view, rejecting the so-called “view from nowhere”—i.e., the illusion of objectivity. And reality TV has been around for decades. We know it’s innately performative, and that cameras subtly alter human behavior, blurring the boundary between truth and fiction. This is not a novel insight. Fielder makes a show of approaching his subject with an open mind in order to be more “objective,” but there are plenty of details that Fielder omits to better shape his preferred narrative. For instance, Evans claimed they took a private jet to the Texas federal prison, whereas the film shows them driving there. More significantly, it’s clear Fielder deeply dislikes Evans (by the end of filming, the feeling was mutual), which is why he deliberately features moments that portray Evans as a controlling, manipulative, and almost comically vain Silicon Valley bro. By comparison, Holmes is treated with kid gloves. This is not the smart, savvy, high-powered businesswoman who graced magazine covers and wowed tech reporters with her rosy vision of a diagnostic future free of anxiety-inducing blood draws. Even whistleblower Tyler Shultz has said this version of Holmes seems “broken.” But she’s still a very good liar. Shultz points out in an article for STAT News that Holmes and Evans were living in the $125 million Green Gables estate just prior to filming. They moved to a smaller, average-looking rented condo on the beach for the duration of the shoot (which still rents for around $28,000 a month)—a strategy Holmes employed before when being profiled by journalists. And the disassembled Theranos blood testing device Holmes displays for the cameras—in the scene that triggered the “can” vs. “could” discussion—is not the correct device, per Shultz: That was not the device I worked with 12 hours a day, not the device we hid from regulators, not the device that constantly failed quality controls, not the device we used to knowingly give patients inaccurate medical results. The device she showed Fielder on the kitchen table was not the reason she went to prison. Elizabeth showed him a future version of the Theranos product that was never completed and never used in order to lead Fielder down this BS semantic rabbit hole and to trick him into litigating the entirely wrong thing. Did Fielder catch the misdirection? Was he taken in by the subterfuge, like so many before him? Or is he willfully ignoring those aspects because they don’t suit his purposes? I’ll take Gibney’s transparent subjectivity any day over Fielder’s ambiguous gamesmanship. You Can See Everything is obsessed with whether Holmes is lying, believes her own lies, or even understands what lying entails. Perhaps it’s not that black and white. I’m willing to grant that Holmes’ tears at having to leave her newborn child to serve her sentence are genuine; any mother would be heartbroken. But it also serves her broader purpose of crafting a more sympathetic narrative. The portrait that emerges is one of Holmes as the victim of yet another controlling, abusive man. It’s her entire legal defense during her trial, re-created here for Fielder’s (and our) benefit, with Evans taking on the scapegoat role of Holmes’ former business and romantic partner, Ramesh “Sunny” Balwani, currently serving a 12-year sentence for his part in the Theranos debacle. Fielder has built his entire brand around the unreliable narrator trope, while Oppenheim has been accused of misleading his subjects while making a 2017 short for The New York Times, so it’s fair to say both men might have a complicated relationship with the truth. Perhaps Holmes got the “documentarians” she deserved, and this is a match made in heaven. One can’t rule out the possibility that she and Evans were in on the game from the start. The entire film is an elaborate performance piece, manufactured for our post-truth consumption: ‘Haha, look at these people trying to out-manipulate each other for the cameras, like contestants on the latest reality show.’ Are you not entertained? The cynical nihilism of it all is exhausting, and ultimately the joke is on us. Facts matter. Accountability matters. Even in a world awash in deep fakes and misinformation, there are still some truths that can be known, no matter how doggedly Fielder and Oppenheim try to muddy the waters. They want us to believe that Elizabeth Holmes is “unknowable and everything is ambiguous,” as Carreyrou—who, unlike me, loved the film and thinks it should win the Oscar for best picture—recently wrote on X. “She went after Tyler Shultz and Erika Cheung with everything she had. I was threatened with litigation on a daily basis for months on end. It was a ruthless cover-up of a breathtaking fraud, and she was at the controls. Just a little context for those who have forgotten or didn’t follow the original story.” In the grand scheme of things, nobody cares what the science-and-culture writer at Ars Technica thinks about this film. I am firmly in the minority with my assessment, and I’m good with that. Oppenheim and Fielder will rake in box-office millions and collect a bunch of prestigious awards, including a possible Oscar. Holmes will serve her time—in fact, she’ll be eligible for a halfway house next year—and Evans is already hard at work raising capital for a new biomedical startup. Holmes will once again try to rehabilitate her image by spinning some new variation on how she did nothing wrong, the device really does work, and she is the victim here. It’s the only story she knows how to tell. But we don’t have to keep falling for it. You Can See Everything is now playing in theaters.

Rocket Report: Winter is coming for Themis; Cowboy Space adds iron to the fire
2026-10-30

Rocket Report: Winter is coming for Themis; Cowboy Space adds iron to the fire

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Welcome to Edition 9.17 of the Rocket Report! It’s been a while since an American company announced plans for a medium-lift new launch vehicle, but that’s what happened this week when a startup named Cowboy Space revealed specs for its Big Iron rocket, named for the classic country-western song by Marty Robbins. I’m a huge fan of the name. Maybe Cowboy Space is striking while the iron is hot as the space industry frets over demand for launch outpacing supply. However, Cowboy Space lags far behind so many other launch providers nearing the launch pad with their medium-class rockets, and the company didn’t have much hardware to show at this week’s big reveal. In other words, don’t mistake style for substance. But we’re rocket fans at Ars, and we wish them luck. As always, we welcome reader submissions, and if you don’t want to miss an issue, please subscribe using the box below (the form will not appear on AMP-enabled versions of the site). Each report will include information on small-, medium-, and heavy-lift rockets as well as a quick look ahead at the next three launches on the calendar. Virgin’s new rocket plane reaches home port. The first of Virgin Galactic’s new Delta-class rocket planes left its Arizona factory Wednesday for a trip to Spaceport America in neighboring New Mexico, where it will undergo preparations for a test campaign culminating in suborbital flights to the edge of space, Ars reports. Virgin Galactic expects the craft’s first commercial spaceflight to take place “early” in the second quarter of 2027, but like every other projected date in aerospace, this should be taken with a grain of salt. Virgin Galactic halted commercial flights of its previous vehicle, VSS Unity, in 2024. More to do … The company also revealed the rocket plane’s name: Apeiron. Engineers and technicians at Spaceport America will complete work on the vehicle’s avionics equipment and flight software. Following additional ground tests, Apeiron will be mated again to its carrier aircraft for captive carry tests, followed by a drop test and ultimately a powered test flight above 80 km. With Blue Origin’s retreat from the suborbital spaceflight market and SpaceX’s planned retirement of the orbital-class Dragon spacecraft, Virgin Galactic is the only company with a realistic chance of flying paying commercial passengers to space anytime soon. ESA’s reusable rocket testbed seems cursed. The European Space Agency’s Director of Space Transportation, Géraldine Naja, said on October 22 that she was uncertain whether the agency would be able to complete the first flight of its Themis reusable rocket demonstrator before the end of the year, European Spaceflight reports. The single-stage rocket hopper has completed two countdown rehearsals at Esrange Space Center in Sweden, but the harsh Scandinavian winter may prevent Themis from taking flight this year. Missed schedules … ESA Director General Josef Aschbacher said in January that the Themis rocket’s first hop test would happen in the first quarter of 2026. Delays are nothing new for Themis. Officials originally expected the rocket’s first low-altitude hop test to occur in 2022. The rocket is powered by a reusable methane-fueled Prometheus engine. ArianeGroup, the company behind Europe’s Ariane rocket family, is the main contractor on Themis, which is supposed to test technologies for an eventual reusable replacement for the expendable Ariane 6. Cowboy Space unveils new rocket. Cowboy Space on Monday released the first details about the launcher that it plans to loft before the end of 2028, Aviation Week & Space Technology reports. The rocket, named Big Iron, will be 282 feet (86 meters) tall and about 15 feet (4.7 meters) in diameter. Cowboy Space is developing a methane-fueled engine named Clydesdale. Nine of them will power the first stage, and the second stage will fly a single Clydesdale. Big Iron’s payload capacity will be up to 18 metric tons (39,700 pounds) to low-Earth orbit. Let the horses run … Baiju Bhatt, the billionaire co-founder of Robinhood, started his space company, originally named Aetherflux, in 2024. He rebranded the company as Cowboy Space earlier this year. Bhatt is chasing the market for space-based AI led by SpaceX. After launching into orbit, the upper stage of the Big Iron rocket will form the core of Cowboy Space’s data center spacecraft, with five racks of GPUs, six inter-satellite laser links, and solar arrays stretching the length of one-and-a-half football fields. More good news for Stoke Space. The Pentagon’s Office of Strategic Capital announced Monday a conditional commitment to provide a $150 million loan to Stoke Space for construction of a second launch pad at Cape Canaveral Space Force Station, Florida, GeekWire reports. Stoke Space is well along in construction of its first launch pad at Cape Canaveral for its reusable medium-lift Nova rocket. The second pad would accommodate an upgraded Nova Block 2 vehicle for heavier payloads. Swimming in cash … The $150 million government loan is subject to financial, legal, technical, and due diligence requirements. It follows Stoke Space’s $1 billion Series E fundraising round in September, bringing the company’s total amount of capital raised to $2.3 billion. Stoke aims to launch its Nova Pathfinder rocket next year, followed by Block 2 in 2029. The company’s reusable upper stage, the most innovative segment of the rocket, is now undergoing qualification testing at Moses Lake, Washington. Industry execs forecast launch crunch. The US government may have moved too quickly to conclude that the commercial launch market could support its growing military needs, industry executives warned at a conference this week, Space News reports. The Space Force’s launch strategy, which is designed to broaden competition by tapping commercially developed rockets, has expanded the number of companies eligible to compete for military launches. But executives speaking at the 2026 Silicon Valley Space Week conference questioned whether enough capacity will be available as national security demand rises and SpaceX begins a long-planned transition from its workhorse Falcon 9 to its next-generation Starship rocket. Pertinent quotes … An executive at Stoke Space, one of the companies seeking to break into the military launch business, said it “may have been a strategic error” for the government to assume the launch problem had been solved. Devin Papandrew, Stoke’s vice president of business development, said the government should consider additional funding, along with relief from regulatory and infrastructure constraints. The head of government relations at United Launch Alliance, the military’s leading launch provider after SpaceX, said the launch supply crunch should ease as new rockets enter service. When might that happen? “2030-ish is the best I would hope for right now,” Hunt said. SpaceX no longer needs NASA. In a comprehensive feature story this week, Ars reports on the fraying relationship between SpaceX and NASA. For the last decade and a half, NASA has relied upon Elon Musk’s space company to resupply the International Space Station, end US reliance on Russia for crew transport, and develop a critical piece of the puzzle for beating China back to the Moon. But Musk is now all-in on AI, and SpaceX’s clear priority today is focusing on how to rapidly deploy compute on Earth and in space. The signs are there … SpaceX continues to work on a human-rated lander for NASA’s Artemis Moon program, but there are signs of a partial breakup looming. SpaceX wants to end flights of the Crew Dragon spacecraft by 2030, and is already winding down its remarkably successful Falcon 9 rocket in favor of the company’s much larger but relatively unproven Starship. And Starship’s launch capacity will be filled by SpaceX’s internal demand for Starlink and Starmind, the company’s orbital data center constellation. SpaceX now seems increasingly disinterested in the US government for most contracts outside of data services, such as Starshield for military communications and observations. ULA to develop upgraded Centaur for SLS. During a lengthy news conference on October 23, NASA provided a detailed overview of how it plans to return astronauts to the lunar surface as soon as 2028 as part of its Artemis program, Ars reports. The changes include revised architectures for getting astronauts to the lunar surface on landers provided by SpaceX and Blue Origin. On the launch front, NASA confirmed it will contract with United Launch Alliance to develop an upgraded version of its Centaur V upper stage to fly on the Space Launch System rocket. LLO or bust … The “extended” Centaur V is a larger version of the upper stage that flies on ULA’s Vulcan rocket. Incorporating the extended Centaur onto the SLS rocket will allow the Orion spacecraft to reach low-lunar orbit, a capability it does not have with the current SLS upper stage. In fact, NASA officials said the extended Centaur will be higher-performing than the costly Exploration Upper Stage the agency originally planned to develop as an SLS enhancement. “Getting Orion to low-lunar orbit means solving propulsion and spacecraft integration problems that were left unaddressed for far too long,” NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman said. Vulcan’s return-to-flight mission has a launch date. United Launch Alliance announced Wednesday it plans to return its Vulcan rocket to service on November 13, nine months after a solid rocket booster anomaly threw the company’s 2026 launch schedule into oblivion, Ars reports. The return-to-flight mission will launch 40 satellites for the Amazon Leo broadband constellation, the first of 38 Vulcan flights Amazon has booked to deploy the bulk of its network. The mission will lift off from Cape Canaveral Space Force Station, Florida, and ULA said the launch date is pending range approval. An important mission … The launch is critical for ULA after its new flagship rocket suffered nozzle failures on its solid rocket boosters on two of its first four missions, most recently during a flight in February. In both cases, the rocket overcame the nozzle failure and completed its mission, but officials at the US Space Force, ULA’s primary customer, lost confidence in Vulcan. It’s also important for the Space Force and Amazon, which have staked much of their future satellite fleets on the success of the Vulcan rocket. Space Force says America needs a new spaceport. US Space Force leaders want a new spaceport up and running by 2030, adding a third heavy-lift launch site to relieve pressure on bases in Florida and California that have launched rockets into orbit for nearly 70 years, Ars reports. The obvious problem with that ambition is that 2030 is four years away, and Pentagon officials have not determined where the new spaceport should go or how to acquire land for it. All options on the table … “We need this capability by 2030, but I’m also a pragmatist,” said Lt. Gen. Philip Garrant, head of Space Systems Command. “That is not an inconsequential demand to build a spaceport when you factor in orbital regimes, safety, access to commodities, the Americans that live near it. In many ways, it’s much like an airport, but even louder. So there’s a lot of consideration.” Officials haven’t decided where to put a new spaceport. Garrant later said all options are on the table: a public-private partnership, a new military base, state land, or commercial land. October 30: GSLV Mk.II | NVS-03 | Satish Dhawan Space Center, India | 18:21 UTC October 31: Falcon 9 | Starlink 15-18 | Vandenberg Space Force Base, California | 03:48 UTC November 2: Electron | QPS-SAR-20 | Māhia Peninsula, New Zealand | 08:45 UTC

Reddit is moving to “a logged-in-only experience,” CEO says
2026-10-29

Reddit is moving to “a logged-in-only experience,” CEO says

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
In the future, you won’t be able to use Reddit without logging in. Reddit CEO Steve Huffman confirmed the impending change in a letter (PDF) to shareholders today about Q3 performance. He said that Reddit is shifting “to a logged-in-only experience,” adding: Moving to primarily logged-in will ensure our users have the best Reddit experience possible, and can find the content and communities most relevant to them. It is better for our business because we will have more signals for ads targeting, and being logged-in leads to higher engagement generally. It also allows us to protect access to Reddit’s extremely valuable corpus of data, and helps us better adapt to emerging regulations around the world. Huffman also said that the login requirement will help Reddit better retain new users. Reddit will start testing the login requirement in “select,” non-US markets in Q4. The company will use those tests to decide what a logged-in-only Reddit will look like “globally,” per Huffman. “Pretty much all of the major ad-based platforms have gone through this transition. We are excited and moving quickly,” the CEO added, noting Reddit’s intent to become a “direct daily habit for every Internet user.” The impending requirement comes as Reddit search referral traffic “was down meaningfully in Q3,” Huffman wrote. The largest hit came from logged-out and web traffic, and the overall trend has continued into Q4. “During October, we have seen that search traffic has dropped by more than 1 million referrals on average per week. If this rate of decline continues, current trends would suggest that total DAUqs [or daily active unique users] could fall in Q4, by a low-double-digit percentage from our current base of 130 million DAUqs,” Huffman wrote. Reddit’s average number of DAUqs increased 12 percent year over year (YoY) in Q3. Huffman would like Reddit to become less dependent on search referrals. “Logged-in and app DAUq are our most valuable users, and they are our primary focus,” he wrote. Reddit has been testing a login requirement on desktop since at least August, per user reports. The login requirement is one of many changes that Reddit has made over the past couple of years to grow its user base of its website and mobile apps, and it will also make it easier to track user activity. Earlier this year, Reddit began testing a requirement that people use one of its mobile apps to access its mobile website. It plans to expand testing. “Currently only about 13 percent of eligible mobile web users are seeing the app conversion prompt, so this has room to scale,” Huffman said. As of July, Reddit requires users to be logged in to use Old.Reddit.com. More recently, it blocked access to Old Reddit for people who haven’t logged in within the previous six months. Reddit also effectively shuttered most third-party apps by making its API expensive in 2024. Combined, the changes should make it significantly easier for Reddit to understand who uses Reddit and why, which it says will help manage things like AI scraping and evolving search habits. The changes should also help Reddit understand ad effectiveness and gain additional insight into user activity, while making it more appealing to advertisers and investors. Reddit reported a net income of $283 million (a 74 percent YoY increase) from $895 million in revenue (a 53 percent YoY increase) in Q3. Advance Publications, which owns Ars Technica parent Condé Nast, is the largest shareholder in Reddit.

Gen Z doesn’t think their degrees will land them work anymore, so they’re becoming their own bosses instead—and these college towns are thriving
2026-10-30

Gen Z doesn’t think their degrees will land them work anymore, so they’re becoming their own bosses instead—and these college towns are thriving

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
College degrees are increasingly leaving graduates questioning whether the time and money were worth it as underemployment, unemployment, and an uncertain job market cloud their career prospects. But rather than waiting for the right job opportunity to come along, some young people are taking matters into their own hands—and turning to entrepreneurship. New data shared exclusively with Fortune by GoDaddy finds that microbusiness activity is growing across college communities nationwide, with several smaller university towns emerging as particularly strong entrepreneurial hubs. Stanford, Calif.,—home to Stanford University—has long been an entrepreneurial hotspot and led the ranking with 20.2% growth in microbusiness activity between July 2025 and June 2026. But smaller college communities weren’t far behind: Warrensburg, Mo., home to the University of Central Missouri, saw 16.1% growth, while Newark, Del., home to the University of Delaware, recorded 16% growth. Elon, N.C., and Cortland, N.Y., rounded out the top five, with growth of 15.5% and 14.6%, respectively. “What surprised me is the diversity of schools and towns that made the top 10,” Alexandra Rosen, economist and head of GoDaddy’s Small Business Research Lab, told Fortune. “They range from the more high-tech hubs, with Stanford coming in first place, but also with rural areas like two SUNY schools being in the top 10, showing that entrepreneurship really is growing everywhere.” The study identified college towns by combining GoDaddy’s city-level microbusiness data with U.S. Census and federal education data. To qualify, a community needed a population of at least 10,000, a college or university with at least 5,000 students, and at least 20% of its residents enrolled in college or graduate school. While the GoDaddy data isn’t exclusive to students or recent graduates, it offers a broader snapshot of entrepreneurial activity in and around college communities. A microbusiness could belong to a professor publishing a book, a local resident opening a coffee shop, or a student testing a business idea—and it speaks to the entrepreneurial energy universities can help cultivate in the communities around them. “The optimism of opportunity and making an impact on the world—in college towns, you’re encountering the real world and probably asking, ‘What is your place in that world and what are you going to do with it?’” Rosen said. College towns have long been breeding grounds for innovation, producing some of today’s biggest companies. For example, Mark Zuckerberg launched Facebook (now Meta) while attending Harvard University, and Michael Dell founded what would become Dell Technologies from his dorm room at the University of Texas at Austin. But a new wave of technological change is something of a double-edged sword for aspiring entrepreneurs. On one hand, AI is transforming the job market and raising questions about which entry-level opportunities will remain available to young workers. On the other, AI tools are lowering the barriers to starting a business, making it easier for aspiring founders to develop business plans, build websites, test ideas, and get a venture off the ground without the same resources they might have needed in the past. Overall, Rosen said entrepreneurship offers young people a way to “take the reins or to bet on yourself,” rather than wait for an employer to give them an opportunity. Universities themselves have been a major source of making the process less intimidating. According to business school accreditor AACSB, the number of undergraduate entrepreneurship programs increased by nearly 25% between 2017–18 and 2019–20. “It is inviting more students to feel it more accessible and less scary, and as a more likely outcome or potential path forward,” Rosen said. “It feels less risky because it’s more familiar. Everybody knows somebody that has a small business now.” Some of the world’s most prominent business leaders are also urging young people to take a closer look at entrepreneurship, arguing that rapid technological change is creating an unusually fertile environment for aspiring founders. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang called it “absolutely the single greatest time to start a company,” during a conversation earlier this year with Y Combinator CEO Garry Tan. The 63-year-old even said he was “jealous” of the opportunities ahead for young founders. For those intimidated by the changes in the world, he encourages entrepreneurs to approach any challenge with one simple question: “How hard can it be?” OpenAI CEO Sam Altman sees a similar opening for young people willing to take advantage of the AI revolution. Speaking to Stanford students earlier this month, he argued that entering the workforce during a period of rapid change can give ambitious people an advantage. “If you’re heading into the world right now to work on something, I think you want to start your career at a time of the greatest change, when the playing field is most open,” Altman said. “I think people with high potential, drive, ideas, a new way of looking at things, and new skills tend to do relatively well when the ground is shaking the most.” Still, not every billionaire business leader thinks young people should rush to strike out on their own. Amazon founder Jeff Bezos has cautioned against treating well-known college dropouts as blueprints for success. “It is possible to be 18, 19, 20 years old, drop out of college and be a great entrepreneur,” Bezos said on stage at Italian Tech Week 2025. “We have famous examples of that working, Bill Gates, Mark Zuckerberg, etc. But these people are the exception.” Bezos, now the world’s second-richest person with an estimated $355 billion fortune, graduated from Princeton University in 1986 and didn’t launch Amazon until nearly a decade later. That extra time gaining work experience, he said, improved his odds of success. “I started Amazon when I was 30, not when I was 20, and I think that extra 10 years of experience actually improved the odds that Amazon would succeed,” Bezos added. “So that would always be my advice: I finished college, and I think it’s been helpful to me.” Preston Fore is a reporter on Fortune's Success team. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Current price of oil as of October 29, 2026
2026-10-29

Current price of oil as of October 29, 2026

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
By 8:45 a.m. Eastern Time on October 29, 2026, oil had reached $115.40 per barrel, measured using the Brent benchmark. That’s 85 cents less than it cost yesterday morning and about $50.50 above its price a year earlier. Oil prices are inherently unpredictable. While many variables come into play, the basic push and pull of supply and demand is what ultimately matters. In times of heightened concern about recession, war, or other major disruptions, oil can swing suddenly. Each gallon you pay for at the pump bundles together several costs. Crude oil is one piece, but you also pay for refineries, wholesalers, government taxes, and the price markup set by gas stations. Because crude oil usually accounts for more than half of the price per gallon, it tends to move the needle the most. Sharp increases in oil almost always show up quickly at the pump. Declines in the price of oil, on the other hand, often translate into slower, more delayed drops in gas prices—the “rockets and feathers” effect. When an emergency arises, the U.S. has a reserve of crude oil called the Strategic Petroleum Reserve. Its chief function is to secure energy during disasters like sanctions, severe storm damage, or war. It can also help take the edge off brutal price spikes when supply gets hit. It’s not a solution for the long haul. It’s more of an immediate safety net to support consumers and keep crucial sectors of the economy running (think key industries, emergency services, public transportation, and the like). Oil and natural gas are two of the main fuels that keep the world running. A big change in oil prices can end up affecting natural gas. As an example, if oil prices increase, some industries may sub natural gas for certain areas of their operations wherever possible. This can increase demand for natural gas. The oil market typically tracks two benchmarks: Between the two, Brent offers a clearer view of global oil performance because it prices much of the world’s traded crude. It’s also often the preferred gauge for tracking historical oil trends. In fact, the U.S. Energy Information Administration now uses Brent as its primary reference in its Annual Energy Outlook. Looking at the Brent benchmark over multiple decades, you’ll find oil has been anything but stable. It’s seen sharp rises due to factors like wars and supply cuts, along with steep declines tied to global recessions and oversupply (called a “glut”). For example: Bottom line, oil’s historical performance has been anything but smooth. It’s hugely affected by wars, recessions, OPEC whims, evolving energy initiatives and policies, and much more. Looking to stay up-to-date regarding the latest energy developments? Check out our recent coverage: The current price of oil per barrel depends largely on supply and demand, including news about potential future supply and demand (geopolitics, decisions made by OPEC+, etc.). In the U.S., prices also move based on how friendly an administration is to drilling, as it can affect future supply. For example, 2025 saw the Trump administration move to reopen more than 1.5 million acres in the Coastal Plain of the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge for oil and gas leasing, reversing the Biden administration’s policy of limiting oil drilling in the Arctic. The price of oil updates constantly when the “futures” markets are open. A futures market is effectively an auction where people agree to buy or sell oil in the future. As long as people and companies are trading contracts, the oil price is changing. In short, shale is rock that contains oil and natural gas. Think of shale as energy yet to be tapped. The more shale the U.S. accesses, the more energy we’ll have—and the more easily oil prices can keep from spiking as much thanks to a greater supply. When oil is expensive, it tends to make everyday items cost more. This can be related to energy (your heating, gas utilities, etc.), but it’s also due to the logistics involved with making those items accessible to you. Shipping, for example, can affect the price of things at the grocery store, as it’s more expensive to get those products from warehouses and farms onto the shelf. Anthropic’s IPO filing spends 69,800 words on AI’s risks to investors and says little about the risks to the rest of us
2026-10-30

Anthropic’s IPO filing spends 69,800 words on AI’s risks to investors and says little about the risks to the rest of us

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Bhaskar Chakravorti is the dean of global business at The Fletcher School at Tufts University, chair of Digital Planet, and the creator of the Register of AI Risks & Remedies, which is cited in this piece. The modern prospectus evolved as a correction to the manias of the railway age. Its job was to tell the people putting up the money what could go wrong for them. Anthropic’s prospectus does that job with unusual zeal. Even as the AI lab promises the possibility of an age of miracles, it spends 69,800 words, about 30% of its 240,000-word filing, detailing the risks of getting there. Catastrophic risk? Check. Job displacement? Check. Rogue agents? Check. Industry power concentration? Check. All such harms appear in the prospectus as they might become a cost to Anthropic. Even its candor about risks makes the list: Anthropic warns investors that its own public statements about AI risks “may be used against us in litigation.” But the agentic age needs a second correction. The prospectus counts what AI could cost Anthropic’s shareholders. It doesn’t count what Anthropic’s products could cost everyone else, or whether that bill could one day land on those same shareholders. In a research collaboration with Oxfam, I have assembled a Register of AI Risks & Remedies, a living record—with 103 entries of AI risks currently—drawn from investigations, court proceedings, media and technical reports. The Register prioritizes risks by severity, identifying who bore the costs, and readiness of remedies. If you were to think about Anthropic’s prospectus as a document that has framed the risk to capital providers who line up regardless of the warnings in the hopes of eye-watering returns, think of our Register as one that frames the risk to everyone who has already borne AI’s costs, mostly without consent or return. There is a second little appreciated reality about Anthropic that makes things particularly interesting: it is also a Delaware Public Benefit Corporation, and Delaware law requires a periodic statement on how it has served “those materially affected by the corporation’s conduct.” Anthropic’s disclosures on this front describe effort rather than results. It’s worth noting that its charter is to balance “financial interests of shareholders with the benefit of the broader public.” To abide by this charter, I would suggest that the company file a second document: a “Public Risk Statement”, if you will. What could go into this statement that hasn’t already been noted in the 69.8 thousand words of the Risks section of its prospectus? You can run an audit to note what’s in our Register but not in the prospectus and find many answers. Examples? Think about someone in the Netherlands mistakenly flagged by a fraud algorithm, or a Polish doctor whose skills eroded because they over-relied on an algorithm, or a Kenyan content moderator who had the traumatic job of labeling toxic content. Three considerations are key to this second proposed document. The irony is that not only do they highlight the costs borne by third parties, they are eventually relevant to future investors as well. First, the impact on workers and worker productivity. Our Register finds a pattern of overreliance on AI and deskilling, wherever AI acquires authority and the people meant to supervise it lose theirs. Automation bias runs through a wide range of AI-aided decisions. The prospectus mentions overreliance once, in passing, and deskilling not at all. It acknowledges that human oversight can fail under pressure to improve productivity. A Public Risk Statement would have to say what has worked: who checks the AI, and whether they have the time, records and authority to overrule it. AI’s productivity benefits can be oversold when the cost of checking the work is ignored – and shareholders need to be made aware of that. Second, Anthropic is expected to make money because customers experience economic gains from productivity. But if these gains depend on poor oversight or compensation is due to those wrongly harmed, the gains shrink and Anthropic’s sales numbers are overstated. AI has benefited from a hidden subsidy and its invoice could come due. The prospectus does note that the company’s monitoring “does not cover all” AI deployments but it does not specify the entire spectrum of possibilities. Investors need to distinguish revenue built on genuine productivity gains from gains that might get erased in the future. The city of Detroit paid Robert Williams $300,000 after a flawed facial recognition match led to his wrongful arrest. Tenant screener, SafeRent, reached a $2.275 million settlement for discriminatory screening. Closer to home, Anthropic itself reached a $1.5 billion settlement to compensate authors and publishers over pirated books. Civil rights, housing, copyright, each involved costs to AI users and suppliers. Wider use can expose more people to mistakes. As precedents build, demand for compensation may rise. Finally, the Register reveals a surprise, which could accelerate such demands for redress. None of these settlements required a new AI law. For about four out of five of the 103 harms documented, an existing law was on the books and it applied. The gap that exists is between legal protection and the practical means to secure redress, but this gap could narrow as the legal and policy machinery gets moving. Even if Washington does not act, there is AI-related legislation coming from the states. The Transparency Coalition’s 2026 report found legislators in 30 states enacted 115 AI-related laws this year—a nearly 58 percent increase from the previous year. Eight state attorneys-general are suing OpenAI for harming children; despite the federal government’s distaste for taking any action, even the FTC is investigating frontier labs’ safety lapses; elsewhere, the EU is launching AI Act enforcement; and the Financial Stability Board is urging banks to strengthen AI governance. It will be a slow process but AI-related losses will become a cost of doing business when someone has the right and the means to demand payment. A Public Risk Statement would reveal what the company knows about its product’s uses, who could be harmed, who’s responsible, and what affected people can do. Investors will judge how much of the business depends on costs others are carrying. The law doesn’t require this second document, but Anthropic’s chosen purpose does. Also, at some point Anthropic’s shareholders may have to foot the bill for the hidden subsidy of AI development, so it’s only fair that it goes public about this subsidy before the company itself goes public. A 3.4% GDP print hides a ‘haunted’ housing market and a September stall in the economy
2026-10-30

A 3.4% GDP print hides a ‘haunted’ housing market and a September stall in the economy

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
The U.S. economy grew at a 3.4% annualized pace in the third quarter, the Bureau of Economic Analysis said Thursday, up from 2.2% in the second quarter and above the 3.2% median forecast. Consumer spending rose 3.8%, and Goldman Sachs economists called the makeup of the quarter “strong.” Look underneath, though, and much of the growth came from a few places, and some of it had slowed by the end of the quarter. Spending on information-processing equipment, the computers and peripherals going into the AI buildout, jumped 37.4%, and industrial equipment surged 24.7%, according to BEA data broken out by Goldman. But housing investment fell 3.3%. Inventories, which companies build up and then sell down, added 1.6 percentage points to the headline GDP gain, or nearly half of it. And in September, the last month of the quarter, real consumer spending was flat, according to the personal consumption and expenditures report. Goldman expects consumer spending to slow from here as the boost from fiscal stimulus earlier in the year “fades completely.” It already has fourth-quarter growth tracking at 1.8%, with consumer spending slowing to 1.8%. BEA’s own release credits the gain to investment, consumer spending and government spending, partly offset by a decrease in exports. Within investment, it says the increase “primarily reflected increases in private inventory investment and in equipment,” led by information processing equipment. The other economy in the number is housing. David Kelly, chief global strategist at J.P. Morgan Asset Management, opened his weekly note this week by saying “the housing market is haunted.” The National Association of Home Builders’ gauge of buyer traffic fell to 18 in early October, its lowest in almost 15 years, Kelly wrote. He estimates 2026 housing starts at 1.32 million units, the weakest since 2019. The homeownership rate has slipped to 65.2% from 66.0% in 2022, “with a much sharper recent decline for those under age 35.” Goldman expects housing investment to fall 5.0% annualized in the fourth quarter, while business investment, driven by AI, rises 8.2%. Kelly sees “a silver lining” for markets: weak housing restrains growth and inflation “in the face of the AI boom.” For the rest of the country, he called it “only a silver lining in a generally gloomy sky,” with homeownership falling and young people’s plans to “marry, settle down and build a family in their own home” pushed back. BEA’s numbers cut the other way, too. Real final sales to private domestic purchasers, which strips out inventories, trade and government to show what American consumers and businesses actually bought, rose 4.9%, faster than the headline and up from 4.6% in the second quarter. Net trade subtracted 3.3 percentage points, on stronger imports and lower exports, so the headline also hides some domestic demand. Some of those imports are the AI equipment itself; BEA built its information-processing estimate mainly from import data. The labor market is holding up, too. The four-week average of initial jobless claims fell to 197,000, the lowest since 1969, Goldman noted. Core PCE inflation ran 3.12% year over year in September, though Goldman’s estimate of the Dallas Fed’s trimmed-mean measure, which it treats as a better read on the underlying trend, was 2.27%. The Fed held rates steady this week, and Goldman still expects it to raise them another quarter point in December. “That being said, it should be noted that the current rate on a 30-year fixed rate mortgage, at 7.32% last week, is not particularly high by historical standards,” Kelly wrote. “It has actually averaged 7.68% over the past 50 years and 7.60% in the 20 years before the Great Financial Crisis.” Goldman’s global economists think the lift from AI is already easing. “We are passing the peak impulse from the AI boom,” they wrote Thursday, and they expect AI investment as a share of U.S. GDP to shrink in the coming quarters, along with the boost to consumer spending from rising asset prices. Meanwhile, don’t expect a boost from housing, which will remain in a slump. Residential construction was $1.182 trillion in the second quarter, or 3.6% of nominal GDP, according to Kelly. He expects housing starts to fall again, to 1.24 million in 2027, and “lower still in 2028.” Generative AI was used for research assistance and/or transcription of this article. Which Gen Z will show up for the midterms: the socialist rebels or the ones who reelected Trump?
2026-10-29

Which Gen Z will show up for the midterms: the socialist rebels or the ones who reelected Trump?

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
The midterm elections on Nov. 3 threaten to break Republicans’ trifecta, with the House and Senate both in play, if polling suggesting a “blue wave” is to be believed. Democrats are hopeful, with such deep-red states as Texas appearing to be within reach. But two years after Gen Z men broke for President Donald Trump, this vote offers yet another look at Gen Z and its apparently budding appetite for socialism. Some polls make Gen Z’s politics sound downright revolutionary. A June Cato/YouGov survey found 53% of Gen Z hold a favorable view of socialism, against 45% for capitalism. But bank statements tell a different story. Betterment’s 2026 Retail Investor Survey found 52% of Gen Z investors moved money meant for investing into sports betting over the past year, compared with 4% of baby boomers, while an Intuit survey found 43% of Gen Z adults plan to start a business this year. That’s a lot of capitalist behavior from a generation that tells pollsters it’s done with capitalism, and who have another chance to swing a pivotal 2020s election. The other complicating factor is that when you ask what Gen Z means by socialism, the answers sound a lot like the good old-fashioned American Dream: a home, a career, and a shot at getting rich. This vote, then, offers a checkup of sorts from the most disillusioned Americans on whether the American Dream is surviving into the 2020s, or what the new version of it—even a socialist one—might look like. In 1868, about 60 years before “the American Dream” took hold as a concept during the Great Depression, Horatio Alger’s Ragged Dick became a smash-hit novel that summarized the American rags-to-riches story. Dick, a New York City shoeshine boy, works hard and saves his pennies, but his big break comes when he saves a rich man’s son from drowning and is rewarded with a job in the man’s office. Carol Graham, a senior Brookings fellow and University of Maryland professor who has spent two decades measuring hope and despair, thinks the way Americans tell this story is part of the problem. “Even the old famous Horatio Alger story, he had a benefactor,” she told Fortune earlier this month. “And they never talk about that when they tell the story.” For about 30 years after World War II, the government and big employers played the Ragged Dick rich man part for baby boomers and their parents. The GI Bill, federally backed mortgages, and companies that planned to keep you for 40 years vaulted a whole generation into a new level of broadly shared economic prosperity. “The middle class was constructed in the ’40s,” said Misael Galdámez, data manager at the Living Wage Institute, who has been working to understand just what happened to the middle class over the last half-century or so. “So if you want a middle class for the 21st century, it’s also something that needs to be constructed through policy, through employer investment.” That’s roughly what young people mean when they say socialism, according to Graham, who started her career making the case for market reforms in Latin America and isn’t scandalized by the word. Gen Z is describing “progressive attitudes about everything from diversity and inclusion to safety nets to being able to afford housing,” she told me in a follow-up email. Philipp Carlsson-Szlezak, Boston Consulting Group’s global chief economist, put an even finer point on it in a separate statement to Fortune. By a strict definition, he pointed out, not even European economies like Sweden or Norway are socialist, but “capitalist systems” with features including individual ownership, price competition, and decentralized decision making, even as they “choose to divert significant income to soften capitalism’s edges.” That choice doesn’t make them socialist by definition. When Gallup asked the question in 2019, about half of young adults viewed socialism positively, but 83% felt good about “free enterprise.” “Gen Z definitely sees owning a home as less attainable,” Galdámez told Fortune. His group’s living-wage data shows the share of workers earning enough to support a family fell from 47% to roughly 43% between 2021 and 2025. Now it’s building an index of what a middle-class life costs, county by county, and he expects two surprises when it comes out: “how high the level of income needed to actually support what people think of as a middle class life is,” and “how few people actually have it.” People are already adjusting the dream down, he said, and now they’re “less likely to say in order to be middle class, you need to own a home.” Housing costs are the top political issue for voters 18 to 34, a CNBC poll found, and the country is short about 4 million homes, by Realtor.com’s count. Carlsson-Szlezak doesn’t buy it. Millennials are catching up with Gen X’s homeownership rate in their early 40s, he wrote, and could get there sooner by moving to the suburbs. Hold home size constant, and affordability “has not fallen nearly as much” as the headline numbers suggest. “I’m not sure when it became a baseline expectation to own a home at age 24,” he said, noting someone willing to build their career in, say, upstate New York has that option. “I don’t think it was ever feasible in NYC and never will be.” The second promise was a job that turned into a career. MIT labor economist Paul Osterman, whose book was covered by Fortune in September, found young people want that as much as their parents did. “The desire to get a real job and have a career? That’s stable,” he said. The supply is what changed. He counts 35% of the American workforce, more than 55 million people, in what he calls “disposable” jobs, and traces the devolution of middle-class status to the iconic 1981 moment when then-President Ronald Reagan fired over 11,000 air-traffic controllers. Former President Joe Biden swore in 2022 he was “sick and tired of trickle-down economics,” and young men largely pulled the lever for Trump anyway two years later. Now Osterman expects AI to cut “hiring for entry level jobs and so young people will be more affected than others.” A dark theory has increasingly taken hold about the 21st century version of the Alger story: elite overproduction. Peter Turchin, the University of Connecticut professor emeritus who founded the field of cliodynamics, has argued in several books that societies throughout history fall into crisis when they create more credentialed strivers than they have good seats for. This week, he told Fortune the “growing unemployment/underemployment of university graduates and those with advanced degrees” is one of the trends to watch. The New York Fed found 42% of recent graduates underemployed in June, working jobs that don’t need the diploma. Carlsson-Szlezak sees a generation adapting. The mid-20s were always precarious, he wrote: “That’s true today and was true of every prior generation, too.” Getting ahead now takes “more nimbleness, more career-switching and risk-taking, more hustle,” and “Gen Z appears to be doing that.” In January, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said young Americans were warming to socialism because they aren’t invested in the stock market. Their investments are more in things like whether the Jets cover the spread. Gen Z made up 48% of online betting activity among Bank of America’s customers in July, my colleague Cat Gioino reported, and the bettors got back less than 75 cents on the dollar. Gen Z was twice as likely as other Americans to call betting an investment. The legal betting boom also pulled in young, college-educated men who had mostly stayed away from gambling, Cat found, which is about as close to Turchin’s surplus strivers as data gets. Graham said that on a 0-to-10 hope scale, she put the 24-year-old moving retirement money into a sportsbook “closer to 3 or 4 than 8 or 9,” a “whatever” attitude nearer to despair. “For one, it is not a good choice,” she told Fortune. Frustration over a lack of agency, she added, eventually “goes from being a possible motivational factor to a hope eroding factor.” Carlsson-Szlezak points to the market doing what it does, more than a grand theory of hope or despair: “You simply couldn’t bet and speculate as freely and easily before, whether that’s Crypto or Kalshi.” The quieter bets look a lot like their grandparents’. Galdámez said “most of the Gen Z folks that I know are much more into saving for retirement.” A house, a job that pays, a business with your name on it: the wish list is about as bourgeois as it gets. Turchin himself declined to make a conclusion about Gen Z’s socialist tendencies, pointing to economic fundamentals when reached by Fortune, and saying Trump is failing the bourgeois wishlist test. “Trump/MAGA have failed to improve the well-being of common Americans, instead involving [the] USA in a losing war with Iran,” he told Fortune. Other counter-elites could seize the opening, he added, maybe the DSA, but that is more likely to come in the next presidential election, in 2028. Past that, “we are in a highly unpredictable environment, with highly variable possible outcomes. I realize that you would prefer a more definitive forecast, but I don’t have such one for you.” Fortune asked Graham who plays Dick’s benefactor for Gen Z now. Rich kids get one through their parents, she wrote, “and my guess is that there are not too many benefactors just hanging out.” Nick Lichtenberg is business editor and was formerly Fortune's executive editor of global news. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Pope Leo XIV becomes the first pope to vote in a U.S. general election — he’s requested GOP primary ballots 3 times and Democratic twice
2026-10-30

Pope Leo XIV becomes the first pope to vote in a U.S. general election — he’s requested GOP primary ballots 3 times and Democratic twice

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Pope Leo XIV has become the first pope to vote in a U.S. general election. The Vatican confirmed Friday that the Chicago-born pontiff had submitted a mail-in ballot in Chicago ahead of November’s midterm elections, citing his long-standing belief in civic participation. Leo, formerly Robert Prevost, “has exercised his right to vote as a United States citizen” and has done so “whenever he was eligible to do so,” the Holy See press office said. The statement said he had also voted while living in Peru whenever he was eligible. “The Holy Father expresses his belief in the right and the duty of citizens to participate in democratic processes,” it added. Leo’s vote was first reported by the Chicago Sun-Times, which quoted the chief of staff at the Will County clerk’s office as saying the pope, registered at a New Lenox address, requested a mail-in ballot on Oct. 3. It is unclear how Leo returned the ballot. His brother, John Prevost, was in Rome this week accompanying parishioners from St. Jude’s, an Augustinian-run parish in New Lenox, Illinois. The elder Prevost attends Mass there, and Leo gave a talk at the parish in 2024 while still a cardinal. Since 2000, Robert Prevost has voted in five primary elections, requesting Democratic ballots twice and Republican ballots three times, according to records held by the Illinois State Board of Elections. The records do not show which candidates he supported. Illinois voters must declare a party preference to receive a ballot in a primary, when parties choose their nominees for the general election. No such declaration is required in general elections. While registered in heavily Democratic Cook County, Prevost requested Democratic primary ballots in 2008 and 2010. After moving to the more conservative suburban Will County, he requested Republican primary ballots in 2012, 2014 and 2016. The records also show that he submitted ballots in every general election since 2000 except those in 2020 and 2022. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved.
