Ireland joins European Lab for Learning and Intelligent Systems network
2026-10-30

Ireland joins European Lab for Learning and Intelligent Systems network

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Alongside Norway, it is Ireland’s first time being included in the pan-European network. With the launch of ELLIS Unit Ireland, Ireland is set to join the European Laboratory for Learning and Intelligent Systems (ELLIS) network. This is the first time that the country will have been involved and it joins other new units in Austria, France, Germany and fellow first-timer Norway. The network has 49 units across the continent and aims to put forth Europe as a leader at the forefront of AI research. The units operate as localised hubs whereby researchers can work together, share ideas across a range of disciplines and carry out long-term research. The Unit is part of Rinn AI, which is Ireland’s national centre for research and innovation in data science and AI and is funded by Research Ireland. Prof Noel O’Connor, Rinn AI’s director, will lead the ELLIS Unit Ireland, with the purpose being to advance fundamental AI research in areas such as core machine learning, computer vision, natural language processing and information retrieval. The Unit will also work closely with domain experts, policymakers and societal stakeholders, as a means of addressing real-world challenges. There will be a focus on connecting researchers across the country and training the next generation of ML and AI researchers. Commenting on Ireland’s inclusion to the network, O’Connor said, “Joining ELLIS is a significant moment for AI research in Ireland. Through Rinn AI, we have brought together researchers from every university in the country and ELLIS Unit Ireland now connects that community directly to Europe’s leading network in machine learning. “It opens new doors for collaboration, gives our researchers and students a platform on the European stage and helps ensure the AI we develop is shaped by the people it serves.” SiliconRepublic.com recently spoke with prof Paul Townsend, a photonics specialist in the School of Physics at University College Cork, about his plans for the semiconductors research centre that sits within Research Ireland’s new Rinn Network. Spooky science: How your brain conjures up night-time scares
2026-10-30

Spooky science: How your brain conjures up night-time scares

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
University of Bristol’s Prof Michelle Spear looks at how your brain could be responsible for things that go bump in the night, not ghosts. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) It’s three in the morning. You’ve woken from a dead sleep because you’ve heard a thud in the darkness or a floorboard creaking downstairs. Or perhaps you’ve woken because you have the unmistakable sensation you’re not alone. During daylight, these phenomena might be dismissed as the heating, your neighbours or the sounds of an old house settling. But in the middle of the night, they can feel a tad more sinister. Your mind might immediately start reeling, jumping to the worst-case scenario – wondering if you’ve been broken into, or if you’re being haunted by some supernatural force. In reality, the explanation is far more simple. Things really do go bump in the night. But occasionally, the culprit is closer to home than we might imagine. Falling asleep is not the neurological equivalent of switching off a light. The brain moves gradually from wakefulness into sleep, accompanied by changes in sensory processing, muscle activity and awareness of the world around us. But these changes don’t always occur in perfect synchrony. So for brief periods, characteristics of sleep and wakefulness can overlap. The results can be surprisingly spooky. If you’ve ever been on the verge of sleep only to feel that you’ve fallen off a kerb, missed a step or dropped through the bed, you’ve probably experienced a hypnic jerk. Also known as a sleep start, this is a sudden involuntary contraction of one or more muscles as we move into the earliest stages of sleep. It’s an example of how the transition between wakefulness and sleep isn’t always a smooth one. Hypnagogic experiences can also involve vivid sounds, images or sensations. So as you’re on the edge of sleep, you may think you hear footsteps or whispers – or you may even think you see a figure in the room. Exploding head syndrome – perhaps the most alarming name for a harmless sleep condition – is another example of how the boundaries between sleep and wakefulness can become blurred. As someone is falling asleep, or occasionally waking, they suddenly perceive an enormous noise: an explosion, gunshot or slamming door – yet nothing in the outside world has made a sound. Its precise cause remains uncertain. But the phenomenon demonstrates something important: hearing a sound and a sound actually occurring are not quite the same thing. The conscious experience of hearing is constructed by the brain. So neural activity generated without an external sound can still produce an auditory experience that seems entirely real. The brain doesn’t even need to be asleep for this to happen. If you’ve ever thought you’ve heard someone call your name – only to turn around and find no one there – you’ve been tricked by your brain. Another possible explanation for the sounds we think we hear at night involves source monitoring. This is the brain’s ability to distinguish events generated internally from those coming from the outside world. Thoughts, memories and imagined speech are internally generated, yet normally retain the quality of being ours. But occasionally, that attribution goes awry – allowing something generated within the brain to feel as though it came from somewhere else. Around sleep, that boundary becomes particularly interesting. Another common – but frightening – experience that can happen at night is waking up with the sudden feeling that someone else is in the room. This experience is usually the result of sleep paralysis. During rapid eye movement (REM) sleep, when most of our vivid dreaming occurs, the brain temporarily suppresses activity in most muscles. This protective mechanism, known as REM atonia, prevents us from physically acting out our dreams. But during sleep paralysis, our waking awareness returns while this physical paralysis persists. You know where you are, but cannot move. Yet elements of dream perception can remain active. This is why you may have experienced the sensation that someone – or something – is in the room with you, despite clear sensory evidence of the contrary. One clue as to why this happens comes from experiments examining how the brain distinguishes itself from others. In one study, participants controlled a robotic device positioned in front of them, while a second robot, which was placed behind them, reproduced these movements by touching the participants’ backs. But when researchers introduced a slight delay between the movement and the touch, some participants began to feel as though somebody else was touching them. Some even sensed another person standing behind them. While this experiment doesn’t explain every felt presence that can happen during sleep paralysis, it does reveal something remarkable: the distinction between ourselves and others is something the brain has to construct. Usually, movement, touch and our sense of body position agree about what belongs to us. But if that relationship is disrupted, an internally generated sensation can begin to feel like it originated elsewhere. Now picture this happening in a dark bedroom, when you’re partly awake yet still on the edge of dreaming and unable to move. The sensation that somebody else is there becomes rather less mysterious. Perception is not a passive recording of the world. It combines incoming sensory information with expectation and previous experience to construct the most likely explanation. But at the boundary between sleep and waking, this process can become less reliable. We may hear sounds that were never made, experience fragments of dreams intruding our bedroom or interpret sensations arising from our own bodies as the presence of somebody else. Michelle Spear is a professor of anatomy at the University of Bristol. Her research focuses on the architecture of synovial joints, drawing on her experience in histopathology to examine clinical and functional aspects of tissue-based research. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Sign up for the Daily Brief, Silicon Republic’s digest of need-to-know sci-tech news. Silicon Republic
Rinn Semiconductors director’s vision for the sector in Ireland
2026-10-30

Rinn Semiconductors director’s vision for the sector in Ireland

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Prof Paul Townsend tells us about his plans for the semiconductors research centre that sits within Research Ireland’s new Rinn Network. Over the summer, seven new Research Ireland centres were launched to address key research areas deemed pertinent to the country’s scientific, medical, economic and technological development. The centres belong to a national research network called ‘Rinn’ and between them are set to receive €460m in Government investments over the next eight years, supporting 577 research positions, developing more than 800 PhDs and collaborating with 17 research-performing organisations. The seven Rinn research centres – dedicated to the fields of advanced therapies, AI, energy, medical devices, pharma and biopharma, quantum, and semiconductors – began their work in July, with each being led by directors who are experts in their sectors. Rinn Semiconductors is set to receive nearly €71m of Government funding overall and is headed by Prof Paul Townsend, a photonics specialist in the School of Physics at University College Cork (UCC) and with Tyndall National Institute who was born and grew up in the UK. “I studied physics at university, gaining a PhD from the University of Cambridge on the novel properties of polymers that behave as one-dimensional semiconductors,” he tells SiliconRepublic.com. His subsequent work in industry included time spent at British Telecom on quantum cryptography, the then-emerging concept of exploiting quantum physics to secure information. This work, he explains, was ancestral to “the quantum information and computing revolution that we frequently read about” today. “In 2003, I had the opportunity to move to Ireland with my family and colleagues to found a new group at UCC and Tyndall, working on optical fibre communication systems with funding from Science Foundation Ireland,” says Townsend. Tasked then with supporting the development of the wider photonics research ecosystem and engagement with industry in Ireland, this has since become the focus of his time working with Tyndall as its head of photonics research. “My ambition is to take this an exciting further step forward with Rinn Semiconductors,” says Townsend. The centre is set to focus on research and innovation in heterogeneously integrated semiconductor systems, and aims to lead Ireland’s response to the point at which traditional improvements in computer chips diminishes (the end of Moore’s Law scaling) by pioneering modular integrated circuit-based – or chiplet-based – semiconductor design and integration. Chiplet architecture is a method of using multiple smaller modules to build a single larger processor for a more powerful end result. The Rinn Semiconductors team will seek transformative advances in these technologies and to apply them in three main areas: future internet, sustainable energy and the environment, and digital healthcare. “Today, semiconductor chips touch nearly every aspect of our daily lives; they are in phones, computers and medical technology,” notes Townsend. “Almost any problem you can think of – societal or technological – requires the use of semiconductors to provide a solution. “This is also a technology where Ireland plays a leading role. We are home to a world-class semiconductor industry sector – with a 50-year operating history – that employs 20,000 people in more than 100 companies. “The national semiconductor strategy, Silicon Island, aims to build upon this to position Ireland internationally as a hub of semiconductor excellence and to contribute to the EU Chips Act goal of growing the EU’s proportion of global semiconductor manufacturing output.” Key elements of the Government’s Silicon Island strategy, he notes, include strengthening research capacity, supporting start-ups and spin-outs, and building a future-ready talent pipeline through education and skills programmes. “Rinn Semiconductors is extremely well-positioned to make major contributions to the achievement of these goals,” he says. Another “key objective” of the centre, according to Townsend, is “to foster deep collaboration across the seven universities and institutions involved” by leveraging the “world-class” semiconductor fabrication facilities and infrastructure at Tyndall. The centre plans for what it calls “radical collaboration” with and between Dublin City University, Munster Technological University, Trinity College Dublin, Tyndall, UCC, University of Galway and University of Limerick. This, it is hoped, will lead to “a cohesive ‘atom to systems’ value chain in Ireland, connecting everything from basic materials research through to complete technology solutions”, says Townsend. The Rinn Network, in his view, offers the opportunity to leverage coordinated research at scale, which is “critical” to the development of complex technologies such as semiconductors. “The significant scale of the industry collaborative research projects we have established in just the first two months of operation gives me great confidence that this vision can be realised.” As centre director, Townsend is responsible for ensuring that his programme is “suitably prioritised, innovative, impactful, internationally leading in its focus areas and, critically, delivers on its mission and goals”. He is also in charge of budgeting, and overseeing grant compliance and reporting. “In addition, I take the lead on stakeholder and external relations management, and act as an advocate for the centre and its mission,” he says. Another important part of his leadership role is “to ensure the centre is a beacon for attracting and developing talent at all levels from PhD students to senior principal investigators (PIs)” in order to “address the demand from industry for highly-skilled scientists and engineers, as well as to sustain the academic research ecosystem needed to supply these trainees and keep Ireland at the forefront of semiconductor research”. Apart from the development and implementation of the centre’s scientific and operational strategy, Townsend – whose personal research background is in optical fibre communications and sensing – gets some hands-on time at Rinn Semiconductors, too. “I am also an active PI in the centre, where my group focuses on developing new integrated semiconductor solutions for two main application areas: energy-efficient interconnect technologies for AI clusters in data centres, with the goal of constraining the overall power consumption of AI; and distributed acoustic sensing in optical fibre cables, where we target improved monitoring of undersea communication cables and offshore windfarm power cables to ensure resilience and protection.” Moving forward, Townsend says his Rinn centre is targeting progress in four main areas: research breakthroughs in semiconductor chiplet integration and packaging technologies; translation of these ideas into new manufacturable technologies and products; acting as a catalyst for talent development; and driving engaged research programmes with stakeholders for impact on policy and society. The latter, he hopes, could include “addressing the early stages of the talent supply problem by working with teachers and policymakers on how to encourage more school and university students, particularly women, to take up physics and engineering subjects that can lead to a career in the semiconductor industry”. Townsend notes, too, the potential of the overall network for fostering collaboration and shared progress across the seven cornerstone areas, and the prospect of creating “greater value and impact than could be achieved by a group of individual centres working in a less connected way”. “The wider Rinn Network plan adds the exciting possibility for centres to work together on complex problems that can only be addressed through an interdisciplinary approach. “I think the scale and ambition of the overall programme will also be a great attractor for external talent and a fantastic springboard for researchers already in Ireland to advance their careers. An especially important goal to him on an individual level, he adds, would be “the development of the next generation of exciting, talented researchers who will take Ireland on to even greater success in the future”. “I am very fortunate and proud to have had the opportunity to work in Ireland and to contribute at least somewhat to the development of the national research ecosystem. “On the personal side, my wife and I and our two now-grown-up daughters all live happily in and around Cork – and wouldn’t want to be anywhere else.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Intel shares drop after weak forecast over global PC slowdown

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
‘We see the PC slowdown continuing at least through the first half of next year,’ said Intel’s CFO. Intel shares dropped by nearly 24pc in pre-market trading today (30 October) after the chipmaker gave a weak fourth-quarter forecast over a slowed PC market. The company expects to bring in between $15bn and $16bn in revenue in the upcoming quarter. The figures are lower than reported analyst expectations for Q4 revenue of around $17.1bn. The US semiconductor giant announced a third-quarter revenue of $16.5bn – up 21pc over last year, in line with its expectations. Revenue from its client computing and physical AI division dropped by 5pc to $8.1bn while data centre and AI products gained 78pc to $7.3bn. CEO Lip-Bu Tan noted that the growth reflects “improving execution” and “meaningful progress expanding supply”, and said that demand for its CPUs remain “robust” despite “near-term headwinds across the PC industry”. Soaring memory prices – driven up by the continued unprecedented demand for AI chips – has had a notable negative impact across the consumer devices market, including personal computers. According to data from Counterpoint Research, global PC shipments fell 21pc in Q3 compared to last year. The report also forecasts a 27pc year-over-year drop in sales for the upcoming quarter. “Looking ahead, we see the PC slowdown continuing at least through the first half of next year,” noted Intel chief financial officer David Zinsner. “We remain optimistic about the opportunities across our broader portfolio, and we will continue making disciplined investments in capacity and working capital to increase supply and support future growth.” Just days ago, Intel announced plans to purchase Israeli quantum company Classiq to create a full-stack quantum computing platform. The company has been investing in the technology since 2015. In August, Intel completed a $23bn public equity offering to continue funding future growth opportunities. Intel shed about 1,200 employees between late June and September this year, its filings further showed. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell awarded UCD Ulysses Medal
2026-10-30

Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell awarded UCD Ulysses Medal

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Jocelyn Bell Burnell. Image: Chris Bellew, Fennell Photography The astrophysicist has a long legacy of scientific research, discovery and supporting the wider STEM community. Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell has been awarded the UCD Ulysses Medal for her outstanding scientific achievements and ongoing commitment to developing the next generation of researchers. The Ulysses Medal is awarded to those who have contributed something of immense importance on a global scale. Previous recipients include author Margaret Atwood, former Irish President Mary McAleese and the ‘Godfather of AI’, Prof Geoffrey Hinton. Raised in Northern Ireland, Burnell previously described her early STEM education as challenging. Despite her assurance that she wanted to study the stars surrounding our planet, her gender precluded her from engaging with STEM subjects in school, as they were aimed primarily at young men. She persevered, becoming one of only three young girls taking science in her secondary school, where she topped the class in physics and then continued on to the University of Glasgow and then Cambridge, where she undertook a PhD. It was at Cambridge where she worked on the development of a radio space telescope designed to explore the furthest reaches of space and where she discovered a “scruff” on one of the charts she was analysing. This was the moment she identified what would become known as a pulsating radio star, or pulsar. Despite her work however, Burnell was notably excluded when the discovery was recognised by the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1974. The prize was instead awarded to her PhD supervisor, Antony Hewish and a fellow Cambridge professor, Martin Ryle. Her exclusion stands as a prime example of how the achievements of women were so often disregarded in favour of their male co-workers and supervisors. In 2018, she donated £2.3m to start the Bell Burnell Graduate Scholarship Fund, a fund supporting students coming from underrepresented backgrounds. The money was part of the Special Breakthrough Prize in Fundamental Physics that she had earned for her previous discovery and five decades of leadership in the field. Prof Lorraine Hanlon , a full professor of astronomy at UCD, said Burnell had been “peerless in how she has deployed her brilliance, transforming her groundbreaking discovery into lifelong advocacy for curiosity-driven research, equity and global science diplomacy. “The achievements of all the Bell Burnell scholars stand as living proof of Dame Jocelyn’s enduring legacy in recognising and empowering the next generation of scientific leaders.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Stock Market Bloodbath Spikes Fears of AI Bubble Bursting

The entire US stock market looked like a house of Halloween horrors on Friday, driven by rapidly sinking tech stocks tied to the swelling AI bubble. The tech-heavy Nasdaq 100 — home of major AI stocks like Apple, Nvidia, Microsoft Corp and Amazon — was down 2.6 percent at the time of writing, well on track for a $1 trillion drop, as NBC News observed. Losses were pushed by Amazon, which shed about two percent when trading opened Friday morning. Though Amazon’s third quarter earnings report pointed to continued momentum in the company’s cloud computing unit, it wasn’t enough to offset deep-seated fears around the AI bubble. In particular, Amazon’s capital expenditures for 2026 ballooned to $220 billion, much of which is tied to AI. Friday’s rout comes after shares for Google’s parent company Alphabet — a major bellwether for the AI trade — plummeted over 12.6 percent on Thursday. It was the company’s worst single trading day ever, and the second-largest single day wipeout of any company’s stock in history, obliterating over $500 billion worth of market value. The motion is all stoking fears of a bursting AI bubble, as trillions of dollars remain tied up in just a few top AI companies, which have largely struggled to turn the tech into a commercially viable technology. Many are now asking whether this hyperscale approach — in which tech monopolies build massive, energy-intensive data centers to fuel AI demand — is a sustainable approach, compared to one which deploys efficient and lightweight models for a fraction of the cost. As for whether this is the end of the AI feeding frenzy, it may be too early to tell. For many investors at this point, the hyperscale approach simply has to work, as former federal consumer protection regulator Rohit Chopra told Politico in a recent interview. “Many in the market feel we have no choice but for it to work out,” Chopra explained. “But at some point, we will see whether those hypotheses about revenue and growth come together. Futurism
Man Granted Religious Exemption Against Using AI at Work
2026-10-30

Man Granted Religious Exemption Against Using AI at Work

Futurism | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
University of Kentucky administrator David Durman was granted a religious exemption from using AI at work, claiming that use of the tech violates his religious beliefs as a humanist. As Religion News Service reports, Durman maintains that outsourcing cognitive tasks to chatbots could interfere with his development as a “moral actor.” Assisted by the American Humanist Association (AHA), he applied for a religious exemption under Title VII, the federal civil rights law that forbids workplace discrimination and allows employees to apply for “religious accommodation,” and his request was accepted in September. “The process and work involved in thinking through situations, through my work, through my ethics, through the way I handle other people, is a key component in being able to develop myself as a moral person,” Durman told RNS. “And offloading that labor, regardless of whether it’s taxing or not, is undercutting my ability to be a moral actor.” Durman isn’t the first American to obtain this kind of AI exemption. This summer, a software engineer and Unitarian Universalist in North Carolina named Erin Maus was also granted a religious exemption from using AI at work, with Maus saying that ethical and environmental concerns raised by AI violated her religious beliefs. His approved exemption also comes as religious leaders around the world — most notably Pope Leo, who’s taken a stance against AI — grapple with what AI means for their faith. In his letter to the University of Kentucky’s Office of Equal Opportunity requesting the exemption, Durman wrote that “substituting automated machine processing for human presence, authentic listening, and personal responsibility in my work directly conflicts with my obligation to uphold human dignity and flourishing as primary.” Humanism isn’t an organized religion, but does qualify as a religion under federal statutes. The AHA told RNS that Durman, to its knowledge, is the first humanist to be granted an AI exemption. “This is the first of its kind at the AHA and for humanism generally to exempt those who see AI use as flying in the face of their freedom of conscience,” Alexandria Huiskens, an AHA attorney, told RNS. “For us, humanism is a comprehensive way of life,” she added. “It’s built on inherent worth and dignity, and everyone should have room to exercise this comprehensive way of life at work the same way Christians do, or Muslims do.” Durman’s reasoning is reminiscent of “cognitive surrender,” the term coined by Massachusetts Institution of Technology scientists that describes humans farming out their mental agency to AI tools. By refusing to outsource his critical thinking skills, Durman contends, he’ll be able to center his life and agency around humans. Futurism
Netflix Turns Horrific Social Experiment Into Reality TV Show
2026-10-29

Netflix Turns Horrific Social Experiment Into Reality TV Show

Futurism | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Netflix has recreated one of the most notorious and horrific social experiments in history: the Stanford prison experiment. The 1971 study has left an indelible legacy in psychology for perhaps all the wrong reasons. In it, Stanford University psychologist Philip Zimbardo had 24 male college students randomly assigned to play guards or inmates in a mock prison to see how power structures could affect the behavior of ordinary people. It was intended to last two weeks, but was shut down after just six days. From the very start, the students assigned as guards went on cruel power trips and repeatedly humiliated — if not psychologically tortured — the inmates, to the point that some of the prisoners suffered nervous breakdowns. The most infamous images show inmates forced to wear bags over their heads. Needless to say, the Stanford experiment continues to be controversial for its dubious ethics and the equally dubious rigor of its methodology. Debates still rage over what conclusions, if any, to draw from such a deeply flawed but undeniably compelling study. Netflix’s conclusion? That recreating the unsavory experiment would make for good television. The resulting six-part series, “The New Stanford Prison Experiment,” is less abjectly horrifying but still a tough watch, according to the Washington Post. “Nobody ends up with a bag on his head,” writes its critic Monica Hesse, and there’s “no workplace sadism.” But “what there is, again and again, are a minority of people who have come to an experiment about power and authority with apparently no set definition of power and authority.” Hesse described it as “maddening” to watch people choose to be punitive instead of judicious when there’s nothing forcing them to act that way. Her chagrin was deepened by the apparent pseudoscientific predilection of the experiment-runners, like the “psychologist who keeps introducing crazy variables and then murmuring, ‘Interesting!’ as if she stumbled upon the lunacy rather than planted it.” It sounds, then, that Netflix’s riff on the Stanford prison experiment is faithful to the original in that it’s fatally flawed. Zimbardo admitted in his 2007 book “The Lucifer Effect” how he acted as the prison’s “superintendent” and even coached the guards how to behave — details that critics say clearly compromise the objectivity of the study and biased it towards certain outcomes. There’s probably little about human behavior that the Stanford experiment has left to tell us, but it still clearly captures our imaginations. Frustrated Resident Blasts the Screeching Sound of a Data Center to a County Official During a Council Meeting, Gets Mocked

Futurism | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
A fiscal court meeting in Carroll County, Kentucky grew contentious when local residents started playing the sound of a data center to county officials. Spoiler: they didn’t seem to like it. As local news station WHAS11 reported last week, Carroll County is the planned location for two separate data center projects, each of which will likely be around 800 megawatts and will overtake hundreds of acres of Kentucky land. At that size, the data centers are considered hyperscalers. “I believe they talked about 800 megawatts, so it is a hyperscale unit,” Scott Nab, a magistrate on the fiscal court, told WHAS11 of one of the projects. “It’ll be multiple buildings. It’ll spread over 1,000 acres when completed.” As the news station notes, a data center of that size would consume the equivalent of 560,000 average homes. Nab is among the data centers’ proponents, arguing that data is the industry of the future. Local residents of the rural region, however, are pushing back. One of their strategies? Subject county officials to the horrible, 24/7 screech that large data centers emit. “We’re going to be making them listen to it, so they can get a dose of what they’re asking us to endure,” Laura Beth Austin, a local resident who traveled to a nearby data center to record the sound, told WHAS11. The data center Austin and an associate, April Elwood, visited was reportedly just five megawatts — making it comparatively meager in size to the ones being planned in Carroll County. In a video of the meeting shared by WHAS11, which went viral this week, Austin and an associate are seen interrupting a recent Carroll County fiscal court meeting with the recordings, leading to a tense exchange between Nab and concerned locals in the crowd. “You guys have made your point, okay,” Nab tells the local residents. “But we have to live with this now!” they urgently respond. “Oh, ya have to live with it?” Nab shoots back, smirking as he speaks. “Yes we do!” the locals reply, as other townies chime in. At one point, a security guard jumps in and warns everyone to act like “adults.” The exchange is one of many contentious clashes between those who oppose data centers in their communities, which have earned bipartisan hatred, and those advocating for them. With some exceptions, like Elon Musk’s noxious data center project in Memphis, many planned data centers are to be built in rural communities, and thus stand to drastically change the landscape and living conditions of those who live nearby. Data center proponents generally argue that the facilities will provide jobs and sustain the American lead in the global AI race. Opponents, meanwhile, urge that the infrastructure projects are associated with dangerous pollution, spiking energy costs, and loss of natural land. And then, of course, there’s the noise. “We don’t need a data center in a community, let alone two hyperscale data centers in a community of less than a population of 12,000 people,” Austin told WHAS11. Nab, however, is holding onto his guns, arguing data centers are an inevitability. “We have significant — we have other industries in here that have similar amounts of land that are being used for industrial purposes,” Nab told WHAS11. “At the end of the day, if this doesn’t come to Carroll County, it’s going somewhere.” More on data centers: Elon Musk’s Real Estate Goons Threaten Local Residents Not to Sue Over Deafening Data Center Noise

Theranos whistleblower Tyler Shultz warns that the AI boom is ‘a great opportunity to go deceive people’

REGISTER HERE. “You Can See Everything,” the documentary capturing disgraced Theranos founder Elizabeth Holmes’ final weeks before prison, is sparking national conversation as it becomes one of the highest-grossing documentaries of all time. The film, directed by Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim, intentionally refuses to characterize Holmes as a master manipulator or victim, instead highlighting her uncanny, almost hypnotic knack for making people pity her, even when they’re well aware of her crimes. Tyler Shultz didn’t need to see “You Can See Everything” to make up his mind about Holmes. Shultz, whose first job out of college was at Theranos, was a key whistleblower who exposed the company’s dangerously inaccurate blood testing devices, which made their way into Walgreens stores and delivered faulty results to real patients. (Theranos reached a peak valuation of around $10 billion despite its technology never actually working — the company only used its own machines for a fraction of tests, instead outsourcing others to third-party equipment.) There is little reason to keep talking about Holmes — her case is closed, and she’s in federal prison — but her con-artistry has fascinated the media (myself included) for the last decade. In fact, as Shultz says in an interview with The San Francisco Standard, “the only reason we’re talking about Elizabeth Holmes right now is because she wants us to be talking about Elizabeth Holmes right now.” But the cautionary tale of Theranos takes on a new significance as we enter the peak of the AI era, when companies that have never turned a profit are raising capital at trillion-dollar valuations. “I think the days of having a thirty-minute coffee with someone and sending them $100,000 should be over. Elizabeth took that model and scaled it to $100 million,” Shultz told SF Standard. His grandfather, former U.S. Secretary of State and Secretary of the Treasury George Shultz, was a notable investor and board member himself. “I believe that what [Holmes] did was raise the biggest angel round of funding of all time, but even at the smaller levels, I think investors need to recognize that when they put that type of money in someone’s hands, they are giving them tremendous power,” he added. Theranos’ funding often came from individual investors like the DeVos and Walton families, rather than venture firms. Theranos did not share scientific information or evidence that its machines worked with potential investors, citing concerns about revealing trade secrets, which warded off many top venture capitalists in Silicon Valley. At the time when Theranos’ fraud was uncovered, it was seen as a shrill wake-up call for investors that they need to prioritize due diligence. But in the AI era, Shultz worries that investors may forget these lessons. “A huge danger with AI is that I think it is really hard to do due diligence on it,” he said. “It’s really hard to tell the difference between what is something truly unique and new versus like, a ChatGPT wrapper… If you want to deceive people, I think this is a great opportunity to go deceive people.” The first question posed by “You Can See Everything” lies in the premise of the movie itself. Why would a mother of two young children choose to invite a film crew to follow her final days with her family before surrendering to serve a prison sentence of over eleven years? The film poses questions that seem inscrutable, but this one seems pretty clear. She wants people to pity her, because this pity could be more useful for her future endeavors than hate or mockery. In the film, we see Holmes and her partner, hotel heir Billy Evans, working on Haemanthus, a diagnostics company that they call “Theranos 2.0,” which has reportedly raised about $20 million in funding. “You Can See Everything” doesn’t make Holmes look good, but it also glosses over the more heinous crimes of Theranos, like giving dangerously faulty medical information to real patients, or even its targeted harassment of Shultz himself when he was attempting to sound the alarm about his former employer. In the cultural zeitgeist, Holmes remains more of a joke than a persona non grata. “I have this feeling of unease about what might come next,” Shultz said. “She’s certainly preparing and beginning her second act, and I don’t know exactly where it’s going to go.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. North Korean hackers blamed for stealing record $6.3B in Binance breach
2026-10-30

North Korean hackers blamed for stealing record $6.3B in Binance breach

TechCrunch | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. Hackers working for the North Korean regime have been blamed for a record cryptocurrency heist following a cyberattack at crypto exchange Binance. The hackers stole at least $6.3 billion in crypto from the company’s wallets, though about half of that amount was frozen as the stolen crypto traveled across various blockchains, according to security researchers. The breach is by far the largest known theft of crypto in history, though the incident has not been confirmed yet to be resolved, and the amount of crypto stolen may change. In a post on X, Binance said its initial investigation showed that the hackers broke into the company’s network after targeting a “compromised” Binance engineer’s laptop, which “allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions.” Binance said it has seen no evidence of a leak of its private keys, which would grant the hacker near-unfettered control over the company’s stores of crypto. The crypto exchange — the world’s largest — said it has now suspended all deposits and withdrawals while its investigation into the breach continues. TechCrunch reported on Thursday that the exchange had paused withdrawals across some of its blockchains. Binance said “user funds are safe,” but did not immediately provide proof of this claim. The company said it will “publish an updated proof of reserves once the remediation is complete.” Spokesperson Andie Cooper said in a statement shared with TechCrunch on Friday that some $3 billion has been frozen by industry partners. The spokesperson said Binance has “begun recovering affected assets for users and continues to work with law enforcement, issuers, blockchain intelligence firms and industry partners to trace, freeze and recover additional funds.” It’s unclear how the hack went down or under what circumstances the employee’s laptop was compromised, but it’s not uncommon for security incidents to stem from the use of compromised software on employee devices. Software is generally built from many components, including open source projects, which hackers regularly target, compromise and modify in order to steal data from devices that the affected software is installed on. Other times, malware bundled with regular-looking apps and games can allow hackers to steal usernames, passwords, and session tokens from infected computers, which may let them log in as if they were a legitimate employee. Binance will face questions about why the company’s security allowed a single employee’s laptop to access systems capable of controlling billions of dollars in funds. Spokespeople for Binance did not respond to an email with questions about the breach. TechCrunch has also asked the company who, if anyone, performed a security audit of Binance’s network and systems prior to the hack. Companies regularly ask third-party companies to stress-test their security to identify weaknesses that could be exploited by attackers, but it’s unclear if Binance sought such a review. Security researchers at TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and forensic company, attributed the breach to North Korean hackers, and called it the “largest hack in history.” Researchers describe the network of hackers, known in the cybersecurity industry as TraderTraitor, as a North Korean cell focused almost exclusively on stealing crypto from large companies and exchanges. This group was also blamed for the now-second-largest heist on record, a $1.4 billion theft from crypto exchange Bybit in 2025. According to TRM, the theft of Binance’s billions in crypto took about 20 minutes. Blockchain security firm Elliptic also attributed the Binance theft to North Korean hackers. Elliptic said some of the stolen funds ended up in the same wallet that was used in a previous cyberattack on crypto exchange Bitget earlier this year, in which hackers stole $351 million. The U.S. government and its allies have long warned about the threat from North Korea, which remains cut off from the U.S. and the global financial system for its ongoing development of its nuclear weapons program. Researchers say the breakaway country’s sole motivations are to steal as much crypto as possible in order to continue developing its sanctioned weapons of mass destruction. DoorDash said it’s only received ‘several hundred’ orders from AI agents

DoorDash said it’s only received ‘several hundred’ orders from AI agents

TechCrunch | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. There’s a big uproar about AI agents taking over commerce and orders, but that might not be the case just yet. Delivery company DoorDash revealed during its third-quarter earnings call that it has only seen “several hundred” orders from third-party agents like Muse, Instinct, Fo, and others. For comparison, the company said in its 2025 report it completes an average of 3 million deliveries daily. “I would say that this [using agents to order] is something that is still a very dynamic area. And my take on all things technology is you never want to fight gravity. But at the same time, you don’t want to be delusional,” DoorDash CEO Tony Xu said in an answer to an analyst’s questions about the topic. “What I mean by that is if you look at where AI agents are today, we’re seeing maybe several hundred orders in total from some of these third-party agents,” he said. Xu mentioned that users are still predominantly placing orders through DoorDash’s app. However, the company wants to learn more about agentic use cases and develop a healthy relationship with third-party agent makers. He added that, in the future, there could be an opportunity for it to advertise within these agents. DoorDash has its own agents, including a text-to-order agent it launched in September, a CLI agent introduced in July, and a chatbot called Ask DoorDash it launched in June, which allows users to place orders through prompts and photos. The company also released an MCP to enable ordering from other chatbots for corporate ordering. The space of agent-based ordering is evolving, as websites are still deciding whether to allow them to access their content or not. Amazon has been blocking such personal agents, but Walmart has laid out a red carpet for them in order to snag some business from its competitors. Can AI data centers be mass-produced like cars? This startup aims to find out
2026-10-30

Can AI data centers be mass-produced like cars? This startup aims to find out

TechCrunch | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. AI infrastructure company Runware is making news again, just months after unveiling the Sonic Inference Pod, a modular data center built in a factory and shipped to customers ready to install. Now the company has bigger plans. It told TechCrunch it wants to build a factory that assembles these pods on a production line, as if they were cars. The idea isn’t entirely new. Companies like Crusoe and Schneider Electric also sell prefabricated pods, but Runware says it can ship its pods faster. Flaviu Radulescu, co-founder and CEO of Runware, told TechCrunch that the company plans to scale production at its Romania-based factory to 30 megawatts of data center capacity per day by 2028, or nearly 1 gigawatt per month. That’s a whole lot of capacity: At that pace, Runware would produce roughly as much in a year as the entire U.S. was expected to add in data center power this year, about 10 gigawatts. But it also reflects an industrywide push to deploy data centers faster and more efficiently. Nvidia announced, for example, the Vera Rubin AI factories and the DSX POD earlier this year. Both are also meant to make it faster and easier for companies to build their own AI data centers. The difference with Runware, though, is that it says it has actually built a factory rather than just designed the infrastructure that would let others build one. Radulescu said Runware will gradually increase production starting next year and that it already has two factories — one it has used for a few years to build the pods, and a second it started building earlier this year. The second is expected to be finished within months and go online early next year. Runware will manufacture the modular data centers on an assembly line. Customers can provide their own site to assemble them, or Runware can find one for them. Radulescu said Runware aims to bring the pods online within 48 hours. That time covers installation and set-up time, not build time. A pod once took three weeks to build but it will soon take one day as it ramps up production, says the company. Runware will provide spare parts if components fail. Customers can also rent compute from Runware or supply their own GPUs and pay a monthly fee for the pods and operations. Power and internet are charged separately, he said. The pods can also be customized to customer preferences, he said. The pods have a standard design, but can be configured to meet customer requirements and the latest GPU chips. Chips can take a long time to arrive after they’re ordered, which works in Runware’s favor. “Our pod delivery times are also shorter than current GPU lead times, giving us time to adapt the infrastructure to the hardware being ordered,” he said. “For new GPU generations, our in-house hardware team can design, build, and test components itself, allowing us to adapt quickly.” Right now, these are all plans — execution is the test. The factory will need a lot of people, inventory, supply-chain logistics, and money. There are cautionary tales about reinventing manufacturing, notably the electric vehicle startup Arrival, once valued at over $13 billion, which struggled to mass-produce electric vehicles cost-effectively compared with traditional plants. Runware, which has raised more than $60 million to date, said it plans to raise a Series B, but for now it is finishing and launching the factory with the $50 million Series A it raised last December. “Our cost structure is very different,” Radulescu said, explaining how Runware seeks to avoid Arrival’s fate. “We do have headcount and materials cost,” he continued. “But our factory staff is cost-effective relative to the value of deploying compute for AI.” He also said that designing its own systems and buying widely available, affordable parts helps keep costs low. “We’ve brought in a highly experienced plant manager from the automotive industry to work alongside our chief of hardware and help us get that execution right.” Sourcing components could also be a challenge. Though Runware says it designs and builds its own cooling and other pod systems with standard components, some parts, such as pumps and fans, come from Germany, and it still depends on Nvidia for networking. Still, Runware claims it has received $1 billion in committed interest for its Sonic pods since it began offering them to outside customers a month ago, “representing over 20 megawatts, which we’re now working to deliver,” Radulescu said. Data centers in the U.S. and abroad have come under fire for how much energy they consume and how that affects the grid and other resources. When pressed, Radulescu said his company’s pods only need power and an internet connection. They’ll use a closed-loop liquid cooling system with air-cooled radiators to release heat, so they don’t really need a water connection. “We’re working with power providers on a site pipeline covering 397 megawatts, with potential to expand to 1.2 gigawatts,” he said, referring to its list of planned sites and adding that the company is deploying its modular data centers on sites that are much smaller than those used by hyperscalers like Amazon, Microsoft, and Google. The sites, he continued, typically already have power, so they shouldn’t further strain the grid or disrupt the community. The pods also run “completely silent up to temperatures of 86 degrees Fahrenheit. We use energy from renewable sources whenever possible,” he continued. “We are actively working on expanding our network of energy providers and sites. We’re aiming to have renewables as our main energy source.” Runware will bring its new factory online early next year, then expand to the U.S. and other regions worldwide. Modular systems are the future and will soon become “the standard for large AI deployment,” Radulescu said. “Customers should be able to plan a hyperscale data center around factory-built pods from the beginning and have it working in months instead of years,” he added. Himalayan Communities Are Relying on Centuries-Old Water Solutions In the Face of Climate Change
2026-10-30

Himalayan Communities Are Relying on Centuries-Old Water Solutions In the Face of Climate Change

TIME | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A man-made structure, called an ice stupa, rises up against the winter skies in India's Ladakh region. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The word Laya describes the natural rhythm that has carried the people of the Himalayas through monsoons and drought for centuries. Across the peaks and glaciers, the populated mid-hills and the foothills, communities have harmonized their calendars to the pulsing of water. Where annual rain falls in just four months, these master engineers funnel rooftop rainwater through handmade bamboo piping into gravel pits to recharge underground wells, maintaining them at the annual festivals. They adapt to the climate by unearthing ancient subterranean medieval stone spout networks for drinking, washing, and bathing. But there’s another Sanskrit word used in Hindi called Pralaya, passed down through oral tradition like Himalayan myth. It means the melting away of places that no longer exist. “Recent flooding in Nepal was a pralaya,” says hydrometeorologist and environmental scientist Ngamindra Dahal, chairperson of the Nepal Water Conservation Foundation (NWCF) and executive board member of the South Asian Meteorological Association. “We’ve never witnessed anything like it.” Himalayans have fallen out of sync with nature at no fault of their own. As earth’s largest swath of ice outside the poles thaws from the unprecedented rapid global warming, high- and mid-mountain villages across eight countries of the Hindu Kush and Himalayan mountain regions have entered a new world of extremes: too little and too much water. The Himalayas are losing glacier mass 65% faster than a decade ago. The impacts will be felt by 2 billion Asians across 12 major downstream river basins by 2050. And by 2100, it’s projected that 80% of glacier mass will be lost. The reality of dangerous cloudburst floods and water scarcity is, however, already being felt. On Aug. 26, a 55 million-ton, 93-m.p.h. ice-rock avalanche triggered by a glacier collapse in Nepal near the Tibet border killed more than 1,400 people, with thousands more still missing. Meanwhile, the Nepali government will drain four massive glacial lakes to reduce the danger of yet another catastrophic flood. The vulnerable, historically low-polluting country has been waiting for a response to its request for compensation from high-emitting nations for the damage created in part by this climate disaster. And on Oct. 22, Nepal became the first country approved for a provisional payout from the U.N.’s Loss and Damage Fund, securing over $21.6 million for recovery from the catastrophic floods. Meanwhile, with deepening uncertainty about the planet’s most basic natural resource, Himalayan people forge on charting their own solutions to save their homes by reviving ancient water systems, building artificial glaciers, and uncovering hidden flood risks. “The Himalaya is often described as Asia's water tower, but that metaphor is dangerously passive if it makes the mountains sound like a stable reservoir. What the laya-to-pralaya concept clarifies is that the Himalaya is a living, dynamic system—and that climate change is compressing geological time,” says Dahal. “What once happened across generations may now happen within one.” For centuries, perennial springs, or mool, have dictated how Himalayan communities live their lives. But that trusted relationship is breaking down. Still, resilient Nepali communities are adapting to the shifting environment of melting snow and ice and unpredictable monsoon rain by reclaiming historic infrastructure. To combat water shortages often from erratic rainfall shifts disrupted by climate change, they’re reviving an ancient network of underground terracotta canals that channel water to sunken stone spouts, some chiseled as early as the 6th century. Known as hiti or dhara, these water systems connect to ponds that absorb monsoon rain, recharging the groundwater table to keep spouts flowing during dry season. In places like the Kathmandu Valley, local governments are actively restoring and rerouting this massive ancient spring network, originally engineered by the Licchavi and Malla dynasties and now threatened by climate change and rapid urbanization. Through the NWCF and an intergovernmental organization protecting mountain environments and cultures called the International Center for Integrated Mountain Development, Dahal is replenishing water supplies by taking inventory of more recent drying springs to gauge a new baseline of water availability. "A community may have adequate water in its landscape but lack the investment to bring it to households,” says Dahal. “Conversely, a community may have taps everywhere but an increasingly unreliable source.” He’s teaching local communities how to revive and reroute their drying life sources based on changing water movement underground, by tracking seasonal flow rates, water quality, and geographic elevations. Once new water patterns are mapped, Dahal helps communities design localized water-sharing agreements and storage, and adapt household rainwater harvesting systems—in a grass-roots water governance model aligned with the Nepal government adaptation plan. “When a pipe breaks, you repair the pipe; when a tank collapses, you rebuild the tank,” says Dahal. “But when the spring itself stops flowing, repairing the infrastructure solves nothing.” Up in Nepal’s Limi Valley, families are confronting a different kind of uncertainty. Tashi Lhazom recalls being 13, studying at a Kathmandu boarding school, when she received a phone call from her remote high-alpine village. On the other line her mother simply said, “You better study hard because you have nothing to eat here anymore.” An avalanche had struck her 1,000 year-old ancestral valley—just two years after a massive glacial lake outburst flood (GLOF), a sudden violent release of water from a melting glacial lake—and had taken with hundreds of the yaks that were her nomadic herding family’s generational livelihood. This went against everything Limi Valley indigenous villagers had known about trusting the mountain Gods. “My people believed the Gods were angry with them for being greedy. That was their cultural rationalization,” explains Lhazom, now 26. When what villagers assumed was another catastrophic GLOF washed away bridges and a hydropower plant in the Limi Valley in May 2025, Lhazom was ready to do something about it. “I couldn’t let the community blame itself for something it was not responsible when I knew it was those emitting greenhouse gases who were partially to blame,” says Lhazom, one of the first from Limi Valley to earn a college degree and now an activist among a wave of Gen Zs fighting climate destruction in Nepal. She tracked down climate scientists and politicians, urging them to investigate the cause of the flood, until a field team finally agreed to come. After Lhazom directed them around closed flooded roads to local water sources, they trekked upstream, reaching glacial terrain at 17,000 ft. to learn the truth: It wasn’t a GLOF this time. Instead, thawing permafrost—the ice that acts as glue to bind mountain rock—was without warning, draining trapped small “thermokarst” lakes, releasing massive volumes of underground water down the slope. As a result of their investigation, that 2025 Limi Valley flood was reclassified as Nepal’s first-ever recorded thermokarst lake flood. After sharing drone footage, photos, and satellite images with the scientists who proved the flood mechanism, Lhazom became co-author of a September 2025 comment paper in the Communications Earth and Environment Journal alerting the world to this overlooked risk. It led to an acknowledgement and warning in a July 2026 scientific paper about the increased risk of thermokarst lake floods due to climate change—not just in the Himalayas, but across Peru, China, and other high-alpine mountain environments. And from it, a newly defined dangerous hazard category, called Small Alpine Lake Outbursts (SALOs), emerged. “Limi is fortunate enough to know they are in danger, but there are hundreds of ‘Limi Valleys’ across the Himalaya. There are still villages whose mountains have not been studied. They don't know if they are living in danger or not,” says Lhazom, whose 2024 film documenting the Limi Valley flooding, No Monastery No Village, was screened at Banff. But most importantly to Lhazom, she was able to convince her Indigenous people of the Limi Valley that the flooding was not their fault. Since finally putting them on the map, Lhazom has been working to help secure government funds to resettle the remaining families away from danger on nearby ancestral land. As Nepal’s politicians scramble after this year’s floods to reform the legal framework for disaster relocation, Lhazom is traveling high in the mountains to deliver relief for the victims among 33,000 now in need of permanent resettlement after being banned from rebuilding their homes along the permanently destabilized high-risk mountainside. “The frozen water banks we know are melting, and that means flooding that can sweep an entire village away within a fraction of a second. Our water sources are getting thinner and fewer and that is a grave concern,” says Lhazom. “But what’s most heartbreaking for these mountain people is knowing they can never go home.” Generational families have no choice but to accept the erasure of their ancient Indigenous settlements. “The mountain is more than land. It is our identity, our history. The water spirits we worship inspired our folk songs and Indigenous wisdom, and polluting means you are being disrespectful to the water spirits,” says Lhazom. “It’ll be a challenge to convince the people to live in safer places, but I don't want them to be in a place that could kill them.” Northwest of Lhazom’s home, in Ladakh, India, where –40°winds whip through a remote desert on an 11,500-ft. shelf in the shadow of the Himalaya’s highest northern peaks, the people rely on a single water source: melting glacial ice, now covered in soot from vehicles carrying adventure tourists acclimating in its capital of Leh. The region’s unbearably beautiful and dry glacial-dependent settlements could become the next Himalayan ghost villages—joining the 2,000 already abandoned from drying glaciers, water scarcity, drought, and unpredictable monsoons in places like Spiti and the Uttarakhand, India, and the Upper Mustang of Nepal. But here in the “Third Pole,” climate activist and mechanical engineer Sonam Wangchuk is blending modern technology with his traditional knowledge. In 2015, inspired by stories of his ancestors moving “parent” glaciers into a cave to make “baby” glaciers—based on the early work of “the Iceman of India”—Wangchuk developed the science to revive the ancient cultural practice of grafting glaciers. He grew new ice to reverse water scarcity in Ladakh. He ran a mile a mile-long pipe from a glacier spring down a steep slope; at the bottom in the shade the water shot straight into the air and, in the dead of winter, froze. Over a month, the ice grew into a multistory conical artificial glacier fountain he called an ice stupa after the sacred Buddhist dome-shaped shrines. Rather than wasting down the mountain, his invention preserves a critical water supply for an extra month through June when farmers need it most for planting. Word of Wangchuk’s ice storage traveled, and he built ice stupas in numerous Himalayan villages facing water scarcity, even allowing a community from Ladakh’s abandoned village of Kulum to return home. Beneath this elegant solution lurked a critical flaw. The pipes feeding the stupas were freezing. But working with longtime colleague Surya Balasubramanian, who earned his PhD in Switzerland in ice reservoir behavior, Wangchuk last year brought the ice stupas back to life through automation. By integrating solar-powered, computer-controlled sensor networks and motorized valves, the water flow could be turned off remotely to prevent the pipes from icing, ensuring 80% of the incoming spring water is frozen and stored. “We innovated a method to either spray water from fountains or drain the pipes using valves controlled by weather and pipeline sensors,” says Balasubramania, who in 2023 independently launched Acres of Ice to scale the ice stupas. Today, they’ve built or supported 160 ice stupas across Ladakh, Nepal, Mongolia, Kyrgyzstan, Chile, and Pakistan, each standing up to 12 stories and capable of storing 3.2 million gallons. But the fight may never be over for Wangchuk, who knows the temporary nature of his adaptation strategy won’t outlive the glaciers. He is now testing a new application: sourcing water for his stupa from a dangerous glacial lake to help prevent it from collapsing and sending a massive GLOF wave down the mountain—and using natural refrigeration to refreeze the lakes. With the growing threat of climate-induced glacier collapse, intensifying monsoons, and prolonged dry spells, the Hindu Kush-Himalaya region is nearing a tipping point. As global temperatures rise, the people who have learned to adapt to harsh conditions for centuries have little control over the destabilization of their ancestral homes. Still, they look to nature for a way forward through simple innovation to make every drop of water count. The question is: Will it be enough? “There’s very little that can be done at this point to protect against glaciers in vulnerable places in the Himalayas, so these communities suffer and pay the price for what the rest of the world does,” says Wangchuk. “The recent avalanche in Nepal is an alarm bell for the rest of the world to mend our ways on this planet.”

The Key Climate Issues to Watch In The Midterm Elections
2026-10-30

The Key Climate Issues to Watch In The Midterm Elections

TIME | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings People cast their ballots on the first day of early voting for the U.S. midterm elections, in New York, on Oct. 24, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings With the midterms approaching, the climate crisis doesn’t seem to be top of mind for most voters. In a recent poll, voters told Gallup that, among 11 issues, the economy, health care, and the war in Iran are the top of their concerns when it comes to this election season. That doesn’t mean climate isn’t on the ballot. The midterms come as the U.S. has faced a slew of climate rollbacks under the first two years of the Trump Administration, including withdrawing from the Paris Agreement, rolling back the landmark endangerment finding, and leasing public lands for oil and gas drilling. With Democrats being significantly more likely to view climate action as a priority, many in the climate movement are hopeful that if the Democrats take back some leadership in Congress, as it could prove essential in helping to prevent further rollbacks. “A new government could stop the bleeding,” says Malcolm Araos, assistant professor at the Wagner School of Public Service at New York University, whose work focuses on the politics of climate policy. “A new Congress, or new Senate can't reverse [many of the climate rollbacks], but they could investigate why [they were] repealed, and they could stop things like that from happening in the future.” All 435 seats in the House of Representatives will be determined on Tuesday, Nov. 3, while 35 Senate seats are up for election. Democrats need a net gain of four seats to take control of the Senate. With a low likelihood of federal climate policy under Trump, regardless of the outcome of the midterms, state races are where most environmental advocates are looking. That’s because state-level elections still leave open the potential for local climate action—especially if Democrats can manage a “trifecta” and win the governor’s mansion and a majority in both chambers, says Geoffrey Henderson, assistant professor of environmental policy and planning at the University of Michigan. “We've seen in recent history that when we have Democratic trifectas at the state level, that lends itself to an opportunity to pass pretty transformative climate policies,” he says. “It's often underrated how important state level races are, because there are states like Michigan that are not currently Democratic trifectas, but that could shift back in that direction…and that can really unlock major policy change.” Since President Trump took office, several states, including California, Virginia, and Colorado have pushed forward climate policies despite federal rollbacks. A number of climate measures are also on the ballot in several states. In Colorado, Amendment 82 will have voters deciding on the constitutional right to buy and sell natural gas—a measure that critics say, if passed, could challenge climate policies in the state by promoting fossil fuel reliance. On the other hand, in Michigan, a ballot measure hopes to limit utilities from influencing political campaigns. (Michigan utility companies have previously lobbied against community solar expansion.) Voters in Eugene, Ore., meanwhile, will be asked to decide whether to tax large corporate retailers to fund climate resilience projects. (The measure is modeled after a similar program passed in neighboring Portland in 2018.) These all serve as bellwether cases, says Henderson. “These ballot measures are in some ways testing theories of how each side is going to approach state level climate policy.” Two issues of concern for voters—the war in Iran and data center construction—could also prove an opportunity for climate-friendly policies like renewable energy investments. “These moments of crisis when energy is really really expensive [can] lead to these switches to renewable energy,” says Araos. As the war laid bare the precarity of fossil fuels, many communities around the world began turning to renewable energy for greater energy security. Voters in 10 states, including California, Kansas, and Ohio, will decide whether to curb data center development in local communities, a fight that’s been resonating with climate advocates. “Data centers have drawn a grassroots bipartisan opposition, and climate groups are very much aligned with that opposition because data centers are major energy users, and in many cases they're major water users,” says Henderson. “I think it is really a climate issue as well as local land use issue, an issue of costs and water availability, and there's an opportunity here for climate advocates to to be a part of a broader coalition.”

Is Running a Marathon Good for Your Heart?
2026-10-30

Is Running a Marathon Good for Your Heart?

TIME | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Every so often, after a marathon, headlines will pop up about a marathon runner collapsing and dying during or shortly after the race. It’s rare, but it happens: A 2025 study found records of 176 cardiac arrests during long-distance running events in the U.S. out of about 29 million people who finished the races. Deaths like these raise the question of whether such an intense physical feat as a marathon can possibly be dangerous, or at least unhealthy, for the heart. We asked scientists who study marathoners about long-distance races and heart health, plus what runners can do to keep themselves safe. Experts are pretty unequivocal: Yes. “Exercise is good for the heart,” says David Oxborough, professor of echocardiography and cardiovascular physiology at Liverpool John Moores University in the U.K. “We know that regular moderate-intensity exercise improves your cardiovascular risk, it reduces mortality, and it has real positive benefits.” Of course, training for a marathon—which can take months or years of steady increases in endurance—is different from the act of running the race itself, says Daniel Muniz, senior lecturer in exercise physiology at the University of Hertfordshire in the U.K. “Running a marathon is a big physiological stress. You're going to hurt after…and if you look at markers in your physiology, everything is going to be all over the place.” Markers of muscle damage and heart damage are elevated, at least for a little while. (Oxborough, who has studied some of these elevated markers, says that they come back down swiftly and don’t seem to be linked to long-term damage.) But the consistent training that running a marathon requires carries positive effects for physical and mental health, Muniz continues. “The benefits far outweigh the negatives. I think that's very fair to say.” Dying during a marathon is extremely uncommon, says Oxborough. What’s more, it doesn’t seem random. People who die during endurance running events tend to fall into two categories, often identified after the fact: either younger people who have undiagnosed genetic heart problems, or middle-aged men who have heart disease without any symptoms. “If you've got underlying disease, then there is a risk of sudden cardiac death. If you're under the age of 35, that is usually caused by an inherited cardiac disease, such as cardiomyopathy. As you go over 35, the main cause is more acquired disease, like coronary artery disease,” he says. “You should, absolutely,” says Oxborough. “We do pre-participation cardiac screening for young people in competitive sport. We have charities in the U.K. It's now becoming much more accepted in the U.S., and it wasn't before. This is a simple 12-lead ECG and a questionnaire to young people.” That’s enough to start the process of figuring out whether people might have an inherited disease, the experts say. If they do, it doesn’t mean they have to stop training. It just might shape how they decide to train and manage their risk. The problem with coronary artery disease is that many people have no symptoms in the early stages. If you’re thinking of running a marathon and you’re over 35 or 40, here’s what Oxborough recommends: “Go speak to your GP. Have a 12-lead ECG done. Have your blood done. Make sure your cholesterol levels are normal. Have your blood pressure taken, and if any of them show any sign that they may be a higher risk, then we recommend an exercise stress test. And if you're very lucky, if you're able to access it, a CT coronary angiogram,” he says. These are tests that will help establish your baseline and what is likely to be happening in your arteries before you really ramp up training. Not all of these are required, but “a baseline test of risk is really important, and that you can do at your GP,” he says. In principle, yes. “I am of the opinion that most people with training should be able to run a marathon. The important bit there is: with appropriate training for a number of months or or perhaps a year,” says Muniz. This is not a physical challenge where you can go out and achieve it tomorrow without practice. “But if you start training and you start running for 20 minutes, or run and walk, and then transition to run for 20 minutes, 30 minutes, 40 minutes, and then you do that for a number of months, I think most people should be able to eventually run a marathon,” he says.
