The Heartbreaking True Story of War and Football in The Mosquito Bowl
2026-10-30

The Heartbreaking True Story of War and Football in The Mosquito Bowl

TIME | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Tom Francis as Dave Schreiner and Ray Nicholson as Tony Butkovich in The Mosquito Bowl Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In the wet heat of a Guadalcanal afternoon on Christmas Eve 1944, two regiments of the 6th Marine Division met on a football field of packed earth and broken coral. They had come to settle a question argued over beer: Which of them had the better team? The island where the Pacific War’s first major American offensive was fought had been quiet for nearly two years, a staging ground of tents and supply depots where men trained and waited and swatted at the insects that gave the island’s Armed Forces Radio network its name. The game was a serious to-do: There were printed programs, numbered T-shirts, goalposts the Marines built themselves, and an announcer whose call went out over the Mosquito Network to men on ships and islands across the Pacific. At least 1,500 Marines lined the field, some with money on the outcome. Neither team scored. Three months later, the division sailed for Okinawa, an island on Japan’s doorstep, where 15 of the 65 men who played that day were killed. That game is the short reprieve at the heart of Peter Berg’s The Mosquito Bowl, in select theaters Oct. 30 and arriving Nov. 25 on Netflix. Directed by Berg, who wrote the screenplay with Mark L. Smith, the film adapts Pulitzer Prize-winning journalist Buzz Bissinger’s book and follows four of those players from the Saturday afternoons when the best of their lives still seemed to lie ahead, through weeks of training that could not prepare them for what came next, to one of the bloodiest battles of World War II. Nicholas Galitzine plays John McLaughry, alongside Tom Francis as Dave Schreiner, Bill Skarsgård as Bob Bauman, and Ray Nicholson as Tony Butkovich. Three of those men would die on Okinawa. The fourth would live to 90. The stadiums give way to boot camp, boot camp to the reef at Tarawa, Tarawa to the long crossing toward Okinawa, where the familiar idea of victory becomes a contest with no whistle, no clock, and survival as the only trophy. What the film is after, once the pageantry is stripped away, is that moment of recognition: young men who thought they had been sent to win learning what they have really been sent to do. Berg has made films (and a massively popular TV show) about football and films about war. Here the same young men must do both. “They got out there and very quickly found that it wasn’t a game,” Berg tells TIME. “They weren’t really qualified for it, but they were going to do their very best. The tragedy is the death of that innocence.” Director Pete Berg on the set of The Mosquito Bowl Netflix What the four men shared, after Japan attacked Pearl Harbor on Dec. 7, 1941, was an assumption. Their country had been attacked, and young men like them were expected to go to war. Schreiner, from Lancaster, Wisconsin, had twice been named an All-American in his career with the University of Wisconsin; in 1942 he was named the Big Ten’s most valuable player, and the Detroit Lions drafted him. The letters he left behind, now at the Wisconsin Historical Society, show a more private side of the star athlete. He was engaged to Odette Hendrickson, wrote to her and to his parents constantly and judged himself without mercy. “Tightly, tightly woven,” Bissinger says of the man. Francis found in him a care and a vulnerability that colored everything, and a mind that would not rest. “He overthinks everything, but then as soon as one of his brothers comes to him and isn’t quite there, he just can find a way to be like, ‘I’ve got you,’” the actor says. Schreiner had options. “Dave could have gotten out of it because of who he was,” Bissinger says. “He could have easily been a phys ed instructor somewhere at one of the bases in the United States, and he said, ‘No, I got to go. My friends are going. I’m just sitting around doing nothing, and I can’t live with that.’” The man who kept Schreiner loose was his teammate Bob Bauman, a tackle from Harvey, Ill. Bauman’s father died when he was a boy, and he grew up holding his family together. “Bob was more happy-go-lucky,” Bissinger says. When Schreiner tightened up, Bauman’s advice, as the author renders it, came down to a single line: “Dave, don’t overthink, Dave.” The two enlisted within months of each other and would serve in the same company. Butkovich was the son of Croatian immigrants who settled in a coal town in central Illinois. He played two seasons at Illinois before the Marines sent him to Purdue, where he rushed for 833 yards in seven games in 1943. The team went undefeated, but the Marines called him away before the season ended, and he cried in the locker room over having to go. The Cleveland Rams made him a first-round pick in 1944, and he kept up a pen-pal correspondence with a 9-year-old fan. “He was really on this high level,” says Nicholson of Butkovich’s stature as a football star. “But at the same time, he was a mama’s boy.” The oldest of the four, McLaughry was the only one already out of college. His father, Tuss McLaughry, coached at Brown, and John captained his father’s team in 1939 before playing a season for the New York Giants. He also painted. “Steady, solid, almost a Renaissance man,” Bissinger says. Galitzine was drawn to both McLaughry’s intellect and his artistic sensibility. He and Berg discussed McLaughry’s “ability to intellectualize things and how that kind of dissipates, given the trauma and the grotesque violence that he sees,” the actor recalls. What remains is a sense of duty more immediate than politics. “The loyalty you have really is not even to your nation necessarily, or to the men who send you to war, but it’s to your brothers who are in the foxhole next to you,” Galitzine says. By the autumn of 1944, the war had brought all four to Guadalcanal. Though the film has them all in the same regiment, in reality, Schreiner, Bauman, and McLaughry were officers in the 4th Marines, and Butkovich served in the 29th, the other regiment on the field that Christmas Eve. Nicholas Galitzine as John McLaughry Kane Skennar/Netflix Bissinger first learned of the Mosquito Bowl by chance, through a passing mention he found while working on another book. “It sounded ludicrous to me and totally unimaginable,” he says. A program survived, with both teams’ rosters, and then he learned that all but one of the men on them had since died. The lives behind those names emerged from what their families had kept: letters, report cards, pictures drawn in childhood. The game stemmed from an argument. The former college players in the 29th were sure they could beat the former college players in the 4th. The rosters held three All-Americans, seven former captains, and close to 20 men who had been drafted by NFL teams or would go on to play in the league. By December, the regiments had drilled for months, and the drilling had gone stale; men were getting antsy, Bissinger says, and antsy men “get this thousand-yard stare.” The division’s commander, Major General Lemuel Shepherd, was looking for a way to lift their spirits. “When morale flags, Marines get worried,” Bissinger explains, reconstructing the general’s reasoning. “They preoccupy themselves with death, and we need some diversion.” Shepherd approved the game, and the Marines worked hard to make it resemble the real thing. “This was about joy,” Bissinger says. “It was about doing something that they loved, that for many of them would be the last thing they would ever do that they loved.” Little of the game itself survives: the date, the printed program, a few photographs, and a final score of 0-0. The men wore T-shirts and cut-off dungarees. Nobody stuck to the intended mode of touch football for long, and the coral in the dirt drew blood. What matters about the Mosquito Bowl is not anything that happened on the field but that it happened at all, an afternoon in which the war paused and these men got to be what they had been raised to be. “The football of it all never really mattered. I think it wasn’t about who scored what, what tackle here, who had this many rushing yards,” Berg says. “It must have given them an opportunity in the middle of this descent into hell to stop, to get out of hell, to be free again, to be young again, and just play a game. That’s what kind of hooked me on Buzz’s book, and that’s what we tried to recreate.” In the film, the game plays as a respite, beautiful and painful at once: everything they get back is exactly what the war will take. Most of the plays are lost, and Berg is untroubled by it. “I don’t even know what the score of the game is in our movie. I truly don’t know,” he says. “You ask me who won. I don’t know. Was it a tie? I think so. Did they have fun? Yes.” There would be no rematch. On March 15, 1945, the division sailed from Guadalcanal, by way of the Navy anchorage at Ulithi, for Okinawa. Nicholas Galitzine as John McLaughry and Ray Nicholson as Tony Butkovich Kane Skennar/Netflix On the morning of April 1, 1945, Easter Sunday, the Americans came ashore on the western beaches of Okinawa, 350 miles south of mainland Japan, in the largest landing of the Pacific War. The defenders did not meet them at the beaches; they had dug into the ridges and limestone caves of the island’s southern third and waited. The 6th Marine Division swept north, cleared the island’s northern reaches in three weeks, then turned around and marched south. The rains came in late May, and for days they did not stop. The roads dissolved, supplies went forward on men’s backs, and the wounded came back the same way. The terraced hills were cut with caves and family tombs the Japanese had turned into firing positions, so that an advance of a few hundred yards could last an entire day of sealing holes with dynamite and flame. Civilians hid in the same caves the soldiers fought from, and many died in them too. By late June, more than 12,000 Americans were dead or missing and nearly three times as many wounded. The Japanese defenders, told not to surrender, mostly did not. The Okinawans are the ones no one can count; estimates of the civilian dead run from about 94,000 to more than 140,000. The Cornerstone of Peace at the island’s southern tip carries more than 240,000 names, soldiers and civilians from every side, and names are still being added. The worst of it came at a hill the Marines called Sugar Loaf. It stood only some 50 feet high, but it sat between the division and Naha, the island’s capital, and Japanese guns covered it from three directions. It changed hands again and again, and in the 10 days before the Marines finally held it, on May 18, more than 2,600 men from the division were killed or wounded. Three days before the hill fell, George Murphy, the former captain of Notre Dame, who had played in the Mosquito Bowl the previous Christmas Eve, was leading a platoon of the 29th Marines on its slopes. As Bissinger tells it, Murphy was ordered to make another assault and protested. “We don’t have enough hand grenades,” he said. He would fight for his country, but this was a death assignment. “The captain said, ‘I don’t give a sh-t. You got to go up there,’” Bissinger says. The Army’s official history of the campaign tells it slightly differently. In that version, it was Murphy who asked to fall back and his captain who refused: the hill was to be held, whatever the cost. Murphy brought his men down anyway, through the mortar smoke. He stopped for one of the wounded on the way, and a fragment killed him there. Only 11 of his platoon’s 60 men were left. Bissinger, who refused to soften the book’s combat, puts it plainly: “War is not about glory. War is not about heroism. War is about death.” Butkovich had been dead for a month by then. He was killed on April 18, during the division’s push through the north of the island. He had been on Okinawa less than three weeks. He was 24, the first of the four to die. Bauman and Schreiner were still paired up. On the morning of June 4, the 4th Marines landed from the sea on the Oroku Peninsula, southwest of Naha. Bauman had been wounded in April and returned to the front. On June 6, his captain went forward with a scouting party, and Company A of the 4th Marines was Bauman’s to lead. He did not lead it long: a Japanese machine gun on the high ground nearby opened fire, and Bauman was killed. Schreiner, a platoon leader in the same company, had two more weeks to live. There is no agreed-upon account of Schreiner’s final patrol. By June 20, the Japanese defense had collapsed into the island’s southern tip, and the Marines were clearing the last pockets of resistance there. Schreiner was mortally wounded on patrol. Accounts collected by Terry Frei, a chronicler of Schreiner’s Wisconsin team, differ on how: some cite grenade fragments, others a sniper or Japanese soldiers who appeared to surrender before opening fire. He died the following day, June 21, as the battle was drawing to a close. Japan announced its surrender less than two months later. McLaughry, whose platoon of self-propelled guns was supporting Schreiner’s battalion that day, was told at the time that a sniper had shot his friend, and later heard that the bullet had lodged in his spine. McLaughry fought through all of it and lived. He came home to his wife, raised three children, led the football programs at three colleges, the last of them Brown, and kept painting, but the war stayed with him. Tom Francis as Dave Schreiner Kane Skennar/Netflix Bissinger never met any of the four, though his father, a rifleman in the 4th Marines, fought on Okinawa and never talked about it. “Can you make guys come alive you’ve never met, and never will meet?” he says. He is candid about how he chose them to tell this story: “I picked those people because they had the most readily accessible material.” The McLaughrys kept everything, down to letters John wrote at 4. Schreiner had a niece. Butkovich had a cousin, still living when Bissinger was writing. “Tony had really nothing, so I compiled as much as I could,” he says. “But I felt he had to be in there, particularly since he was the best of the football players.” Their stories reach only as far as their families, and the war, allowed. The book found its way to the screen through family. Berg and Bissinger are cousins, and The Mosquito Bowl is the second of Bissinger’s books Berg has adapted, after Friday Night Lights. The book follows a wide cast of Marines through several battles; a two-hour film could not, and Berg took his cuts to its author. “I presented my kind of outline to Buzz. I’m like, how would you feel if I did this?” he recalls. “He thought about it, and he said, ‘Do it.’” The film takes dramatic license in places, including a grudge between Schreiner and Butkovich and a battle at Tarawa, the bloody November 1943 assault on a Pacific atoll, where none of the four fought; Marines stranded on a reef under fire was the first image Berg knew the film had to contain. “There were some areas in which Pete had to create his own narrative, and that was going to veer from the book. But that doesn’t bother me, because the essence was there,” Bissinger says. Berg found his own way in on the ground. He walked Okinawa’s battlefields with Marines stationed there, and came away ready to write. On set, Berg gave up some of the control a director usually keeps. “I like not being in complete control and allowing something to grow out of that,” he says. Filming in Australia, that meant a director who kept noticing terrain no one had checked. Wildlife handlers cleared the ground before the actors lay in it, Galitzine says, because the local snakes could kill. Berg would look past the cleared area to some unvetted cliff and announce that the actors should climb it. “We just did it,” Galitzine says. Crawling through the Australian bush made the four actors feel as though they were on an adventure together. “It really was an exercise in trust, in a lot of ways.” Berg had never shot anything like the reef landing. The production put stuntmen, then actors, then camera operators into heavy Australian surf. “You can drown in that surf,” Berg says. “They call that ‘washing machine.’ You’re in the ‘washing machine.’ Think about doing that while you’re being shot at.” Elsewhere, a sand quarry near the coast stood in for the battlefield below Shuri Castle, the Japanese command post at the heart of the island’s defenses. Production designer Philip Ivey scattered scorched books, broken dishes, and clothing across it, the wreckage of ordinary life. The resulting film is intimate and enormous at once, the camera close enough to see fear, with cutaways from the ground to the planet itself that set the men’s suffering inside a scale too large to take in. In the editing room, the question became how much football the film needed. Early versions spent considerably more time on the game. The finished film instead begins with McLaughry after his experience in combat, before going back to the men’s football years. “The football is there to serve a purpose, and that’s to illustrate that these men were living it up. They were at a good spot in their lives,” Berg says. “So I felt like we needed just enough football to tell that story, and then move on.” The actors trained for much more than made the final cut. Before cameras rolled, they spent weeks in training that was part football practice, part Marine boot camp, each actor drilled at a position, enabling the production to make limited use of stunt doubles. Ray Nicholson as Tony Butkovich Kane Skennar/Netflix Each of the four actors went looking for the man he was playing, and each found him somewhere different. Francis found his in a joke. The Mosquito Bowl was his first film, and early in the shoot he went to Skarsgård with a stage actor’s questions: Where do I look, what do I do, how do I make it all happen? Skarsgård’s answer was, “You’re overthinking, Schreiner.” Francis heard the character in it. “I was like, this is the man,” he recalls. Skarsgård leaned on the one fact that set Bauman and Schreiner apart from the others. They played beside each other at Wisconsin, joined the Marines a few months apart, and went to war together. “Tom can always come to me and lean on me, and I could always come to lean on him, both as actors, but also as our characters,” Skarsgård says. “The way that they worked was that Bob could pull Dave out.” Nicholson found his in letters. He read Butkovich as a performer whose confidence was real and whose tenderness ran underneath it. “Once they go through this experience, that version of Tony Butkovich is forever gone,” he says. A five-page handwritten letter Nicholson sent Berg, one the director calls among the “most beautiful” he has ever received, showed him the passion he was casting for. Butkovich’s letters to a young fan, meanwhile, gave the actor a way into the man. Nicholson wrote letters of his own to his father, actor Jack Nicholson, who would have been about that boy’s age during the war. “How do you be someone’s hero?” he asks. “What is that like?” Purdue also sent him restored film of Butkovich with the ball. By chance it came on the anniversary of Butkovich’s death, and Nicholson watched it by himself with the lights off, feeling as if the two of them had somehow met. Skarsgård went to the place itself. While researching the role, he spent three days on Okinawa with Marine guides, and at the end he asked them to take him to where Bauman was shot. It is in Naha now, a residential neighborhood with a playground and a convenience store. “I’m standing where he took his last breath,” Skarsgård recalls, describing the “immense amount of responsibility” he felt. He was struck by the unlikely connection between his own life and Bauman’s. “Here I am, a Swedish actor, 80 years after this happened, and I’m thinking about this man, and I’m connected with this man, and I feel just so much gratitude towards him.” Galitzine carried a different weight, the survivor’s, and it came into sharper focus toward the end of filming. The production shot the last scene for Nicholson, Francis, and Skarsgård on a boat, and when it was done, they wrapped and he did not, because McLaughry’s story continued. “There was just this underlying sense of dread, for a lot of the movie, and guilt,” he says. He had spent months alongside the three actors, “knowing that they were going to have to say goodbye in a way that I didn’t.” Nicholas Galitzine in The Mosquito Bowl Kane Skennar/Netflix Late in The Mosquito Bowl, in a scene Berg wrote, Schreiner talks with McLaughry on a secluded beach about having children and what a man might leave behind. “The contrast of being in one of the most beautiful places I’ve ever been, and then thinking about Dave going through that thought process, it was almost like an out-of-body experience,” Francis says. Galitzine, playing a version of McLaughry beginning to unravel, says that in the scene Schreiner helps “crystallize the reason why they’re doing what they’re doing,” and that Francis’s portrayal gave him “something larger than the chaos and the brutality.” Schreiner never had those children. Bissinger keeps a photograph of him on his wall, and when he looks at it, he reaches for a word he acknowledges is probably not the right one: waste. “Here was a life that really should have been lived,” he says. “He should have had kids. He should have been married. He should have had a career. He should have had laughter. He should have had glory. He should have had disappointment. All the things that we have in life, and he’s never going to get that.” The film frames the four men’s lives with a warning and a number: the Herbert Hoover line it opens on, about old men declaring wars the young must fight, and the loss it closes on, 75 million dead worldwide. Berg wants the audience to leave carrying both. “That wasn’t that long ago,” he says. “We live in a very violent planet today, and I really hope that never happens again. I don’t think a movie can change that. But taking a personal responsibility for each and every human being to think about how we can prevent people from having to go kill each other. If we can, let’s do it.” Against that death toll, four men may seem like nothing. Yet the enormity of the war begins to register through the three lives it cut short and the one it forever changed. Of the dead, traces remain: Schreiner’s letters, Bauman’s snapshots, and restored footage of Butkovich running with the ball. The war left a fuller record of these men than ordinary lives would have, had they simply been allowed to go on living them uninterrupted. Had Schreiner come home, his handwriting might never have left Lancaster. In the few photographs of the game, young men in T-shirts stand on the makeshift field, the jungle a dark line behind them. For one afternoon on Christmas Eve 1944, nothing was decided. Nobody lost. McLaughry grew old with the years his friend never had. Then, in 1993, nearly half a century after Okinawa, he wrote out what he could remember of the day Schreiner was hit, a man in his seventies setting down what a young officer had seen, heard, and been told. Much of what he knew had reached him secondhand, and he doubted the version that had settled into the record. He returned to it all the same, because he was the last of the four men who could, and because somewhere on a field of coral in the flat afternoon light, with the ball sailing through the air, the other three were still 23, and in that borrowed moment, always would be.

Cybersecurity Is Economic Security
2026-10-30

Cybersecurity Is Economic Security

TIME | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Today, a locally owned coffee roaster in Colombia can reach customers in Germany and Japan, accepting payments in euros, yen, and pesos. Meanwhile, a daughter in Connecticut sends money to her parents in Morocco, and it arrives in their account in a matter of seconds. It’s easy to forget that these everyday moments, so central to our lives, were not possible until relatively recently. Moments like these remind me of how our connected digital economy—and the technology that powers it—has opened more opportunity for more people in more places than ever before. It’s nothing short of revolutionary. At the same time, this revolution created new risks. Connections that unlock growth can also create shared vulnerabilities that hackers, fraudsters, and scammers are quick to exploit. In 2024, cybercrime losses were equivalent to the third-largest economy in the world measured in GDP, behind only the U.S. and China. By 2029, its value is projected to reach $15.6 trillion. Add to that the rapid evolution of AI models, and the stakes are rapidly being raised on both sides of the cybersecurity equation. While bad actors use AI to commit more convincing scams and scale attacks, defenders are putting AI to work to detect unusual activity faster, identify vulnerabilities, and prevent fraud before it happens. Now, agentic commerce is beginning to drive a growing share of business transactions. That means cybersecurity requires further levels of verification to ensure agents are only acting as they are permissioned and empowered to do. Our economy has long depended on cybersecurity. As technology evolves, protecting it requires a broader, shared commitment. Cybersecurity can no longer be treated as an issue only for the IT department. It is an economic priority for all of us, from government leaders to CEOs to individual citizens. We all have a role to play. It starts by strengthening the systems that power the secure exchange of value, so they are resilient, intelligent, and inclusive for all. The need is especially acute for small businesses like the coffee roaster in Colombia. Small businesses are the building blocks of economies worldwide, driving 70% of employment and accounting for half of global GDP. Yet, they are also often the least equipped and resourced to withstand a cyberattack. The damage from a cyberattack is rarely limited to just the targeted small business. The broader economy feels the impact too, including the employees, suppliers, customers, and communities that depend on them. Recent Mastercard research found that only 37% of small business owners currently use cybersecurity tools, but 89% say they want to adopt more digital tools. The only question is whether our ability to protect small businesses and the broader online environment will stay ahead of evolving cyber threats. It’s our collective job to make sure it does. No business or single organization can secure the digital economy alone. Banks, technology companies, platforms, governments, and industry groups each bring different strengths—from sharing intelligence and setting standards to building more secure products and helping businesses act early. Groups like the Alliance for Critical Infrastructure and the Cyber Readiness Institute maximize that cooperation and close gaps. Only by working together can we help protect the connections that small businesses run on. Investing in cybersecurity for businesses and communities of all sizes isn’t altruism; it’s a potent business opportunity to drive economic growth. When strong protections are easier to access, more entrepreneurs can participate in the digital economy, sell across borders, and adopt new technologies with confidence. Strong cybersecurity becomes an economic advantage, attracting investment, supporting growth, and kicking off a virtuous cycle that further strengthens defenses. Everyone benefits. We have to get this right. Underinvesting in cybersecurity, or restricting who benefits from those investments, is a recipe for stunting future economic growth and innovation. But when we lead with cybersecurity, we protect the trust that keeps the digital economy running. When we make it harder for bad actors to exploit vulnerabilities, we create the confidence people and businesses everywhere need to keep creating, connecting, and growing. That is a powerful return we all share. Success on this issue should not be measured by how well the best-resourced institutions protect themselves, but by how effectively the ecosystem protects every participant. When strong protections reach businesses of every size, we strengthen trust, resilience, and growth for all. The digital economy may have started as a revolution, but today it is the keystone of economies everywhere. It depends on technology being safe, reliable, easy to use, and above all, trusted. Cybersecurity is the key to unlocking our collective next chapter of digital growth. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

John Turturro Gives a Career-Best Performance in The Only Living Pickpocket in New York
2026-10-30

John Turturro Gives a Career-Best Performance in The Only Living Pickpocket in New York

TIME | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Turturro as Harry in The Only Living Pickpocket in New York Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings To paraphrase the old-school comedian Rodney Dangerfield, a pickpocket in 2020s New York gets no respect. Almost no one carries much cash anymore, and credit cards are useless unless you’re willing to commit fraud. What’s a poor guy with crackerjack observational skills and exceedingly dexterous fingers supposed to do when he can’t even pay for his diner breakfast with his morning’s earnings? That’s where John Turturro’s Harry Lehman finds himself in The Only Living Pickpocket in New York, writer-director Noah Segan’s charming, nimble, and affecting drama about a guy who has seen his city, and his world, change drastically during his lifetime. For Harry, a typical workday involves boarding the subway and identifying the easy targets. In his soft, elegant tweed coat—a garment he might have bought 20 or 30 years ago, maybe at Barneys, when the dough was really flowing—he swirls through New York, his vision attuned to checked-out, earbud-wearing Wall Street dudes, or women who allow their purses to dangle precariously. He sees what others miss, though he’s living in a world where no one pays any attention to their environment. It’s a lonely life. But there’s a bright spot in Harry’s world: Though his wife, Rosie (Karina Arroyave), has been stricken with an unnamed ailment and can neither move nor speak, his days revolve around her. He cares for her tenderly, as best he can, talking to her, laughing with her. At one point he puts Odyssey’s 1977 sequins-and-stardust disco hit “Native New Yorker” on the stereo and dances for both of them, in a kind of disco-salsa shuffle, his arms whirling as if the breeze itself were his dance partner. Turturro and Arroyave Courtesy of Sony Pictures Classics Rosie’s care costs money, which means Harry needs to hustle harder than ever. Watches can still bring in the bucks, as long as they’re not fake. And to unload those, Harry has a close pal and longtime co-conspirator, the wily, wisecracking pawnshop owner Ben (Steve Buscemi). Ben will also take stolen phones, though they’re troublesome: they need to go to a guy in Chinatown, Kelvin Han Yee’s Frank, who knows how to scrub them, after which they’re packed into a container and shipped off to god knows where. When Harry boosts a USB crypto wallet from a brazen young criminal and spoiled rich kid, Dylan (Will Price), Ben has no idea what to do with it—plugging it into his circa-1995 desktop personal computer sends the machine into a tailspin. So he sends Harry downtown to see Frank—whose front is a herb shop, though it seems less like a front than a side hustle—and that’s where Harry’s troubles really begin. The Only Living Pickpocket is part drama, part comedy, part thriller. It’s also a movie about a man reckoning with his past: We may warm to the sight of Harry caring so lovingly for Rosie, but there’s much more to his story, as we learn. And you can’t always tell where the movie is headed; its beats are wriggly, unpredictable. Until recently, writer-director Segan has worked mostly as an actor, often with director Rian Johnson (he appeared in Brick, Looper, and the Knives Out movies), and he has directed one previous feature, the 2022 horror movie Blood Relatives. The pickpocketty lightness of touch he shows is remarkable for a fledgling director. It doesn’t hurt that he’s got Turturro as a star. In the early part of his career, as a favorite of Spike Lee and the Coen Brothers, Turturro, with his tightly coiled, jack-in-the-box energy, always looked like he might blow at any moment. But he’s become much subtler, and sexier, over the years. in the underappreciated 2013 comedy Fading Gigolo, which he also wrote and directed, he played a foxy florist who becomes a classy male escort, servicing sultry hotties (Sharon Stone, Sofia Vergara) before falling for a lonely Hasidic widow (Vanessa Paradis). Suddenly, he was a leading man, and a hot one. Giancarlo Esposito, who plays a detective opposite Turturro's pickpocket Courtesy of Sony Pictures Classics Turturro still has some of his old combustibility; you occasionally catch an air of menace behind that snaggle-tooth smile. But he now has more softness, more range, and the performance he gives in The Only Living Pickpocket in New York is one of his finest, as well as one of the best of the year. In the first section of the film, you believe Harry is just a guy whose glory days have passed: He belongs to a dream New York, that of the 1970s and ‘80s, of strobe-lit dance clubs and independent cinemas seemingly on every block. (Cinematographer Sam Levy, who also shot Frances Ha and Lady Bird, is in tune with the old New York nestled within the current one; he captures the city, from the Bronx to Chinatown, in all its shabby-shiny glory.) At one point, Harry suggests to Rosie that the two hunker down for the evening and watch an old movie, maybe something with Barbara Stanwyck; he comes from a generation that was used to looking toward its past as well as its future. The city wasn’t a safe place in Harry’s younger days—but we’re talking about something other than physical safety, a kind of creative vitality that, if you were attuned to it, could rub off on you like glitter. Harry still has some of that sparkle, but it’s tempered by melancholy, the aftermath of little mistakes and big ones he’s made along the way. Turturro captures all of it—and shows how Harry intends to do right by those he’s harmed—with the kind of lived-in ease you earn, if you’re lucky, over the course of a career. Whirling through the city in that tweed coat, he’s like Fred Astaire breezing through an obviously fake white-cardboard Venice or descending a staircase from nowhere. The grit of the city is his glamour, its noise his music. It’s all his for a song, or a watch deftly separated from its owner’s wrist. His city is a tough place and, as Odyssey sang, he should know the score by now. But at least he knows the dance steps, because as much as New York may change, those never do.

ELI5: Why does Wikipedia care so much about notability? I get the privacy angle. Nobody wants a random Wikipedia page about their neighbor. It's entirely possible that someone is well known in a certain circle but that circle isn't in the public zeitgeist, but if you join that circle you would know of that person. I mean, I can guess if you have a public persona then at a minimum that should be worthy of something - if your neighbor is like everyone else who keeps to themselves then yeah, they don't get a wikipedia page. But if you're a public performer and have done something in public you are notable for, then having that would be worthy of inclusion. If nothing more than just your public facing persona. Like Xav's page would have his parents listed (both are public notable personas), and details on his music, but I would expect the rest of it to not be documented because that would likely be private information. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Donald Trump Sure Is Corrupt. If Only There Were a Constitutional Remedy for That.
2026-10-30

Donald Trump Sure Is Corrupt. If Only There Were a Constitutional Remedy for That.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
This is the fifth in a series of articles, “Try, Try Again?,” drawn from my new book—Great and Dangerous Offences: The Case For (and Against) Impeaching Trump a Third Time—due out in early 2027. The most common ground for impeachment throughout Anglo-American history has been corruption, the misuse of the status and powers of office for private gain, either for oneself or for one’s family, friends, or political allies. Both Parliament and the American Framers abhorred official corruption and made it impeachable because it undercuts the basic contract between rulers and the ruled—that the rulers will not place their private interests, whether in wealth or increased power, over the interests and well-being of the nation. Donald Trump has broken this covenant by repeatedly, corruptly accepting payments and gifts from foreign powers, thereby violating the foreign emoluments clause and betraying the national interests of the United States. This conduct is a clear impeachable offense. Corruption can take many forms, from simple bribery to complex networks of exchanges of money, commercial concessions, and political favors. An especially insidious form of corruption, one especially to be feared from a monarch or president, is the sale of the nation’s interests to a foreign power. The American Framers repeatedly mentioned corruption as being, next to treason, the most necessary ground for presidential impeachment. George Mason spoke of the “corruptibility” of presidents as a primary justification for impeachment. Gouverneur Morris specified “corruption” as one of the “few” offenses for which he would favor impeachment. Among the definitions of impeachable conduct provisionally approved early in the Constitutional Convention were “treason, bribery, or corruption” and then “treason or bribery.” “Bribery,” a specific form of corruption requiring a quid pro quo exchange of favors for something of value, is part of the final constitutional definition of impeachable conduct. Late in the convention, the delegates adopted the additional phrase “or other high Crimes and Misdemeanors” in important part to expand the reach of impeachable corruption beyond technical bribery. The Framers’ fear of corruption is also evident in the Constitution’s emoluments clauses. The domestic emoluments clause bars presidents from taking anything beyond their established salaries from either Congress or the states. The foreign emoluments clause prohibits all federal officials from accepting gifts of valuables, office, or title from any foreign country without express congressional consent. Critically, unlike bribery as traditionally defined in the criminal law, the prohibition on emoluments is not conditional on any corrupt bargain by the public official. Likewise, emoluments are barred regardless of whether the official takes or refrains from any act whatever. The Framers understood that, even for the most virtuous, receipt of favors creates a predisposition to do favors in return. Once that disposition exists, it is only a tiny step to corrupt action. Therefore, mere acceptance of an emolument, without any accompanying promise or reciprocal official act, violates the Constitution. Violation of the foreign emoluments clause is impeachable corruption. At the Philadelphia convention, Morris several times insisted on the need for impeachment in the case of a president in the pay of a foreign power. At the Virginia ratifying convention, Edmund Randolph said explicitly that “there is another provision against the danger … of the President receiving emoluments from foreign powers. If discovered, he may be impeached.” During Trump’s first term, foreign governments or entities closely affiliated with foreign governments paid large sums to his hospitality businesses for lodging, food, entertainment, meeting services, and the like. Although the foreign emoluments clause permits receipt of emoluments from foreign kings, princes, or states with the “Consent of Congress,” Trump never sought the required consent. In his second term, Trump’s pursuit of prohibited emoluments has been far more egregious, in both its brazenness and the amounts involved. However, it is useful to think about his first-term behavior in order to frame the issues surrounding his second-term conduct. From the beginning, Trump’s business arrangements presented the troublesome question of whether domestic or foreign government payments to a president avoid the prohibition on emoluments so long as they take the form of a commercial exchange. Trump supporters argued during his first term that an arm’s-length business transaction is not a “present” or “emolument.” To this Trump added the more general plaint that a businessman should not have to surrender his business (or even abandon its active management) to be president. Both contentions are flawed. In the founding period, the word emolument was used to mean not only salary but far more commonly profit, gain, advantage, or benefit. Even an arm’s-length commercial transaction implies mutual benefit. The seller provides a good or service and receives in return value exceeding the cost to him of the good or service—a profit. When the federal or state government or a foreign power buys from a president’s business, it confers on him a profit, gain, advantage, or benefit. It does not matter for emoluments purposes that there is no corrupt intention on either side. The point of the emoluments prohibition is to eliminate the need to prove such things. It is the conferring of the benefit that is prohibited, and the Constitution is emphatic in banning foreign emoluments “of any kind whatever.” Likewise, the contention that a president should not have to alter his profit-making activities to enter public office is wrong. The Framers wrote the salary and emoluments provisions into the Constitution to ensure not only that the wealthy could afford to serve the country but also that the poor and those of middle station would not become wealthy (or the already-wealthy wealthier) by entering public office. Trump’s position is that wealthy people like himself should be able to occupy the presidency while surrendering no private advantages, and indeed should be entitled to leverage public office to create greater personal wealth. In short, he claims a right to engage in the precise form of corrupt self-enrichment that has since the 14th century been the quintessential impeachable offense. Moreover, the Constitution does not ban foreign emoluments absolutely; it merely requires that Congress consent to them. A businessman president who wants to both serve and continue his foreign commercial connections simply has to ask for permission, and in doing so, provide the people’s representatives sufficient information to assure them that giving permission does not impinge on larger national interests. Trump has never asked. During either of his terms. And Congress has never given permission. The most notorious gift to Trump from a foreign power was Qatar’s 2025 transfer of a luxuriously appointed Boeing 747-8 jet worth $400 million. The announced terms of the gift were that the United States would upgrade the plane for use as Trump’s presidential aircraft, known as Air Force One, but that at the end of his term, it would pass to the ownership of the Trump presidential library. In the summer of 2026, Trump seemingly began to waver on whether the Qatari plane would go to his library, or whether he might try to decommission a different presidential plane and transfer that one. The question remains unresolved. The terms of the gift as originally announced plainly violated the foreign emoluments clause. It was a gift from a foreign government to Donald Trump, to be enjoyed by him while president and to pass into the ownership of his presidential library thereafter. Congress never approved either the original gift or the proviso that it would later become the property of an entity controlled by Trump. Even if Trump ultimately changes his mind about taking the plane with him when he leaves office, the congressionally unapproved gift nonetheless violates the Constitution. The distinguishing features of the Qatari airplane are its size, comfort, and the luxury of its interior furnishings, points Trump has gushed over. Foreign gifts made for the personal pleasure or enjoyment of the president during office do not escape the emoluments clause if someone else gets to enjoy them thereafter. If, for example, a foreign country gave a president an entirely new wardrobe of tailored suits and casual wear, that would be a prohibited emolument, even if the president promised that, upon leaving the White House, he would donate the whole lot to the Smithsonian or Goodwill. Of greater ongoing concern than the Qatari airplane is the flood of commercial agreements the Trump Organization, controlled by Trump but nominally operated by his sons Eric and Donald Jr., has entered into with foreign entities. For example, only weeks before the plane gift in May 2025, the Trump Organization announced its participation in development of a $5.5 billion luxury golf resort in Doha, Qatar. Trump would doubtless argue that the resort deal is a purely private business transaction between developers in Qatar and his family business, thus not qualifying it as an emolument from a foreign state. But the Qatari development company with which the Trumps are partnering is reported to be Qatari Diar, which is owned by the Qatari government. Moreover, Qatar is an absolute monarchy ruled by a hereditary emir without whose approval no decision by a government-owned entity to do business with a U.S. president’s family could possibly occur. In 1993, the Justice Department’s Office of Legal Counsel concluded that “business corporations owned or controlled by foreign governments will fall within the [Foreign Emoluments] Clause,” a result it reaffirmed in 2009. Likewise, any contention that money received by the Trump Organization is not an emolument to Trump because his sons run the company is a threadbare fiction. So far as can be determined, Trump owns all of or controlling interests in all the entities that make up the Trump Organization. Money paid to it is money paid to him. The golf resort deal is, therefore, a prohibited foreign emolument. Worse, the Qataris’ multiple gifts to President Trump are not merely technical violations of the foreign emoluments clause, but are a classic instance of why the Framers put that clause in the Constitution to begin with. Qatar has huge stakes in its relationship with the U.S. government. U.S. trade in goods and services with Qatar exceeds $10 billion annually. More importantly, as graphically demonstrated during the ongoing Iran–U.S. war, the United States is the principal security guarantor of Qatar. Indeed, in September 2025, only four months after Qatar gave Trump an airplane and his family business a major real estate concession, President Trump issued an executive order declaring that “the United States shall regard any armed attack on the territory, sovereignty, or critical infrastructure of the State of Qatar as a threat to the peace and security of the United States,” and stating that in the event of an attack, the United States would defend Qatar. What we have here is not a plane gift made as a friendly intergovernmental gesture between traditional allies or a normal, arm’s-length real estate deal that just happens to involve Trump’s family business, but what normal people would see as bribes paid by a foreign government to the president of the United States to induce him to take official actions favoring Qatar. And bribery is an impeachable offense. Moreover, the Qatar golf resort deal is not the only one of its kind. According to one source, as of July 2026, 24 Trump-branded real estate projects were underway in foreign countries. Not all of the contractual counterparties to these agreements have the direct government connection of Qatar’s Qatari Diar. But some do, as was true in December 2024, a month after Trump’s second election, when the Trumps secured participation in a real estate development supported by Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman. In addition, LIV Golf, a professional golf league financed by the Saudi sovereign wealth fund, paid the Trump family an undisclosed amount to host an April 2025 tournament at the Trump National Doral resort in Florida. Like Qatar, Saudi Arabia depends heavily on the United States for security support. Moreover, in the same period that the Saudis were paying to rent a Trump golf course and Trump businesses were moving into Saudi real estate with Prince bin Salman’s blessing, Trump’s administration was negotiating an agreement (on which Trump is now waffling) that would permit the Saudis to expand their use of nuclear power. The appearance of influence-buying is obvious. The Trump family’s cryptocurrency speculations have also involved repeated egregious violations of the foreign emoluments clause. In sum, firms backed by the government of Abu Dhabi and headed by a member of its royal family paid half a billion dollars for a 49 percent stake in the Trump crypto company World Liberty Financial, half apparently paid before Trump’s 2025 inauguration and half after. Thereafter, an Abu Dhabi–backed group invested $2 billion in crypto company Binance by purchasing World Liberty “stablecoins,” giving World Liberty $2 billion in deposits to invest. Like Saudi Arabia and Qatar, Abu Dhabi and the other members of the United Arab Emirates are deeply economically enmeshed with the United States and heavily dependent on America for national security protection. It would seem entirely accurate to characterize these investments in Trump’s crypto wealth as literal protection payments. And not unfair to suspect that the millions flowing into Trump’s personal coffers have been repaid in the coin of American lives and treasure expended defending the Emirates in Trump’s foolish Iran war. The bottom line is that Trump has repeatedly violated the letter of the foreign emoluments clause, thereby vastly enriching himself and his family businesses, while openly directing American foreign policy to favor his foreign benefactors. There can be no question that the Framers of the American Constitution would be united in finding this conduct both shameful and undeniably impeachable. It’s Democrats’ Dream Senate Scenario. The Polls Say It’s Not Impossible.
2026-10-29

It’s Democrats’ Dream Senate Scenario. The Polls Say It’s Not Impossible.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Winning a U.S. Senate majority is often a game of hedging bets. Every so often, though, a political party dares to dream. As we explained last month, Democrats’ most realistic path to retaking the upper chamber on Tuesday runs through a handful of purple and red states. But as Donald Trump’s approval ratings sink, that map appears to be expanding. Polls now show that Democrats’ preferred Senate candidates are competitive in several states Trump won by double digits. All the usual caveats apply; if the polls are off, Democrats’ strength could turn out to be a mirage. But much ink has been spilled on states like Texas, Ohio, and North Carolina, where Democrats have struggled in recent cycles but appear to be in better shape this year. So instead let’s lavish some attention on the states where Democrats didn’t seem competitive even a few months ago, but which now seem to be in play. Can a mild-mannered pastor win a state that Trump swept by 16 points in 2024? If Adam Hamilton does, he’ll become another example of how strong candidates can help Democrats defy the odds in the Trump era. Hamilton, a former Republican who founded a Methodist megachurch, has embraced moderate positions and out-raised his opponent, Republican Sen. Roger Marshall. Marshall’s own scandals may be helping Hamilton, too. Last month, the New York Times revealed that Marshall, a former OB-GYN, had sued hundreds of patients over unpaid bills, including new mothers. Hamilton wouldn’t be the only Democrat who’s managed to win Kansas of late, either; the state has had a Democratic governor since 2019. Dan Osborn is the best chance Democrats have had in decades of unseating a GOP senator in this Trump +20 farm state—perhaps because Osborn isn’t really a Democrat. A mechanic, former union leader, and Navy veteran, Osborn is running as an independent with the backing of the state’s Democratic Party. There have been few nonpartisan polls of Nebraska, so it’s hard to know exactly where the race stands. But most surveys point to a very close race between Osborn and incumbent Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts. Still, the outcome may hinge on whether enough voters buy Osborn’s independent pitch. He unsuccessfully ran for Senate in 2024, and leaked audio recently captured him calling Trump’s border wall “a symbol of racism” and remarking that rural Trump voters’ lives “probably suck.” If Democratic pediatrician Annie Andrews flips the Palmetto State blue next week, a twist of fate may help explain why. After Republican Sen. Lindsey Graham died suddenly in July, his sister Darline replaced him in the Senate and as a candidate for reelection. But as a political neophyte, Darline has made some notable gaffes (“I’m not that informed on national security,” she said during one debate) and polls now show a close matchup. This week, a New York Times/Siena University survey found the race tied. Andrews, who previously lost a 2022 House race, has framed her candidacy around the cost of healthcare and recently earned support from one of South Carolina’s former Republican governors. Yet she’s also had to address past positions that aren’t popular in her home state, including opposing a bill to ban transgender athletes from girls’ sports. In a recent ad, Andrews said she opposes letting “biological men” compete alongside women. The Sunshine State hasn’t always been as hostile to Democrats as the other states I just mentioned, but it has slipped further and further rightward since Trump won it in 2016. Angie Nixon, a state lawmaker and democratic socialist who won the Democratic primary in an upset, will test whether progressive energy can buck the trend against a Republican incumbent, Sen. Ashley Moody, who has labeled Nixon a “card-carrying socialist.” So far, though, that’s looking like a big if; this week’s Times/Siena poll found Moody ahead by 6 points. The home state of the president of the Confederacy might seem like an unlikely place for a Democratic upset, and it’s certainly among the party’s more far-fetched Senate reach seats this year. But Mississippi is also the state with the highest share of Black residents, and Republicans are trying to wipe away its only majority-Black congressional district. Those facts have fueled Democratic hopes that Scott Colom, a Black district attorney, can oust Republican Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith. So far, the evidence is mixed. Few legitimate pollsters have surveyed Mississippi (a fake firm recently claimed to have found a close race), and Hyde-Smith appears to be leading. To sum up: It’s pretty unlikely that all five of these races will swing Democrats’ way, and it’s possible that none of them will. But if they’re even remotely close, it will bode well for the party in the races it almost certainly has to win to take control of the Senate. After a tour around the country, what could be more relaxing than my colleagues’ suggestions about how to unwind this evening? Take a You Can See Everything Rorschach test: Everyone in the Elizabeth Holmes documentary is playing a character, but only one performer is utterly convincing. Isaac Butler makes the case that the film holds a mirror up to its audience by focusing on the nature of performance and artifice. Bless this “Mes”: Jennifer Lawrence is an Oscar-winning actress, but her new role is a bit of a head-scratcher. Heather Schwedel explains why the A-lister is making a pivot to selling luxury hair care. Whip up some homemade treats: Back in the day, trick-or-treaters used to receive homemade sweets on Halloween. The idea of letting children consume goodies prepared by a stranger might seem unthinkable now, but Chason Gordon argues that we’ve lost something by relying solely on store-bought candy. He proposes a delicious solution. Make sense of the midterms with Slate: Speaking of tricks and treats, watch this livestream recording from Slate’s politics team, who broke down all the spooky thrills and chills we can expect on Election Day. And mark your calendars for Slate’s postelection live event next Wednesday at noon Eastern, when you can join What Next host Mary Harris and Slate senior politics reporter Jim Newell for a debriefing on the midterm results. Come for my colleagues’ smart insights about the midterms, stay for their killer senses of humor. Till tomorrow! Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Why There’s a Real Risk of a Replay of the 2020 Election Come Tuesday
2026-10-29

Why There’s a Real Risk of a Replay of the 2020 Election Come Tuesday

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
This is Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Click here to receive it in your inbox each week. The midterm elections are now underway across the country and as voters decide who should hold the levers of power in Washington, all eyes are on President Donald Trump. He’s spent the last decade baselessly questioning the integrity of U.S. elections—despite plenty of evidence finding they are safe and secure—with things coming to a violent head on Jan. 6, 2021. This year, as more and more polling is suggesting Democrats will sweep Republicans, even in solidly red districts, it raises the question: Will the president accept the results of critical House and Senate races, or won’t he? “I will, if they’re honest elections,” Trump ominously responded when asked this very question on Tuesday. Since Trump began his second term, he’s been laying the groundwork to try to nationalize U.S. elections and go after “voter fraud” through executive action and lawsuits against states, though most of these efforts have been struck down in court. Yet, the Justice Department has not given up. In fact, in recent weeks the agency filed at least two more dubious election lawsuits against California and Alaska, plus a series of cases against immigrants for alleged noncitizen voting. The legal challenges suggest the administration is planning ahead this time around, planting lawsuits that might create a foothold in the courts for the GOP to seize on if the midterms don’t end up going their way. To Charles Stewart III, a political scientist and professor at MIT who has spent decades studying American congressional politics, elections, and political development, the current situation created by Trump is a test of America’s democracy. “Not primarily because of fundamental shortcomings in American election administration, but because political actors associated with the Administration will attempt to delegitimize or overturn its work,” he wrote in a newly published paper, “The Day After: Threats and Guardrails in the 2026 Election.” Stewart believes that Trump’s efforts to thwart this year’s midterms should be viewed in three distinct phases: Before Election Day, during the vote counting and certification process, and when the new Congress convenes on Jan. 3, 2027. In practice, this looks like Trump beginning to seed baseless claims that losing Republicans can make after Election Day: mail ballots are suspect, voter rolls are contaminated with noncitizens, slow ballot counts are evidence of fraud, and any Democratic victories in close races shouldn’t be accepted. After Election Day, the lawsuits begin, harkening back to the days and weeks following Trump’s 2020 loss to Joe Biden, and finally things might culminate at the Capitol, where lawmakers could be pressured not to certify election winners. I spoke with Stewart to understand how this framework can help us see important red flags in the hours immediately following Election Day, plus the chances that Trump incites yet another angry mob to take over the Capitol. And is there any silver lining to hold on to? Here’s our conversation, lightly edited and condensed for clarity. Shirin Ali: In your paper, you drill down on the president’s efforts to undermine the midterms into three flashpoints: Before Election Day, during the vote counting and certification process, and when the new Congress convenes. Can you explain what each of these are? Charles Stewart III: The ideas behind those flashpoints come from what we’ve seen the Trump administration working on in the second term: two executive orders, activities at the Justice Department and Homeland Security, actions that are beginning to nibble around the edges of the election administration process. Particularly before Election Day, putting pressures on mail voting, highlighted by the executive order from this past March that basically directed the U.S. Postal Service to create a new series of procedures for handling mail voting, marrying that together with directives to DHS to create these lists of citizens. The second flashpoint has to do with pressures on election administration, especially election administrators and the institutions themselves. There, I think the pressures go beyond even what the administration has been doing. Certainly, a lot of the activity of the administration has been focused on the possibility that if election officials don’t do what the administration wants, they will not only bring civil complaints against them, but they may very well bring criminal complaints against them. Then after Election Day, that’s where we’re thinking about getting the final count in. We shift into the formal process of canvassing the vote, certifying it, and making sure that we’ve named the winners. I’m thinking broadly about a potential replay of what happened after the 2020 election, where the Trump campaign lodged a number of lawsuits in battleground states like Wisconsin and Pennsylvania. I’m still anticipating, even more now, that coming true. The final flashpoint is what happens when the 120th Congress convenes on Jan. 3, 2027, particularly the House. And there the question is whether there will be efforts to bar the seating of senators or members of the House who’ve been elected, specifically Democrats who would be the pivotal members of the two chambers that would flip control of the chamber to the Democrats. There’s another obvious parallel with 2020, when there were efforts to deny the certification of the presidential election. How do the Justice Department’s lawsuits against California and Alaska fit into this? Those are kind of jumping the gun on the timeline that I had suspected, because they’re really almost preempting post-election challenges and making them pre-election challenges. About a week ago I was saying, “Well, they’ve filed this lawsuit against California, it’s getting kind of close to the election. If there’s just California, it’s kind of a weak rule in terms of applying formal pressure on the states.” Then came Alaska, and this one makes me wonder what else the DOJ is going to do in the next week to bring early lawsuits against states and kind of do a shot across the bow. The California lawsuit is the sort of thing that we that we’ve long expected the Trump administration to do, and in fact in the first administration, there were rumors that they were trying to put together a national voter database and merge it with immigration records to begin to go after states for having noncitizens on the rolls. This has been a long dream of the administration and people associated with the administration, and it’s taken them basically a decade in order to act on it. It doesn’t look like they’ve done a particularly sophisticated job, both in terms of the actual job of database matching or on the legal side. The Alaska case seems to be the most frivolous election challenge that I can imagine, and in fact it’s kind of orthogonal. It’s unrelated to the things I had anticipated, because the lawsuit they’re bringing is the sort of lawsuit that you would expect a candidate to bring. Really, it’s up to Sen. Sullivan to bring these challenges under Alaska law and then potentially to bring a contest to the U.S. Senate. I only have to believe that the Alaska case is the classic case of pleasing an audience of one; Donald Trump wanted it and he got it. There’s just no way in the world this is going to go forward. It’s sort of like going after James Comey or going after the members of Congress who reminded service members they didn’t have to follow illegal orders. It’s really performative, to please the boss, and hard to take seriously, except the degree to which it’s predictive of what we’re likely to see after the election: the federal government putting the thumb on the scales of candidate-oriented post-election litigation. Next week, immediately after Election Day is over, what are you going to be watching for? I think there will be two things. One will be legitimate, and the other will be illegitimate. The legitimate thing is that we may very well see more losing candidates contest their elections and begin taking legal actions to try to save evidence, within the normal contest laws. Some of it may very well be performative to garner support from the administration or local political actors, but those are the ill-fated sorts of things that there’s a legal framework for doing. Plus there’s a legal framework for judges throwing these cases out, right? And that’s the way it should happen. What I think we need to be prepared for, and I don’t think it’s going to be widespread, are candidates supported by the administration going into court to attempt to impound voting machines and ballots and to remove them from where they’re normally housed. Basically, a repeat of the Fulton County event a year ago where the FBI removed ballots. Those are the things I think we should anticipate, and I think that the Department of Justice may very well take matters into their own hands that are quasi-independent of the candidates themselves. For instance, the Alaska lawsuit was just something that the DOJ did because the president wanted it. That’s a disturbing thing that I think we need to be prepared for after the election because we’ve already seen it happen before the election. That is the DOJ just going into places they don’t like and filing lawsuits. In your paper, you wrote, “The greatest danger is not that even a few challenges will succeed; it is that a small number of delayed certifications could matter greatly if control of either chamber is in doubt.” What did you mean by that? If you think about Jan. 6, 2021, in Congress and leading up to that, there was a whole lot of noise in the political system about whether the vice president could refuse to accept electors and whether Congress could impose their own view on what state electors they should accept, and all those sorts of things. In retrospect, traditional constitutional election lawyers had it right all along in terms of the law. The correct person was elected president, but the political ramifications of that whole episode and Jan. 6 itself were just catastrophic for American politics and certainly transformative in many ways. The law held, the right guy was elected president, but antidemocratic elements were energized and we had a riot at the Capitol. A social movement has arisen in the country that makes millions of dollars based on stoking a view that elections in America are illegitimate. It’s an extreme view, and a minority view even within the Republican Party at that level of extremity, but it’s really been politically consequential over the last six years. I think we could be in an Act 2 of that, and that’s what I’m getting at here. Imagine that this was a decade ago and we’re coming into a Congress where the Democrats were going to take over the chamber and have about a 20-seat lead. You wouldn’t have imagined anything major to come out of any contest, any seat. There might have been a couple of contests and it might have been highly politicized, but they would have been handled in the regular order. Imagine right now if Democrats have that same margin of success. I think we have to assume there will be at least a few members of the House on the Republican side who will make a lot of hay around refusing their being seated, refusing to allow them to vote for the speaker, pressuring the clerk not to accept their certificates of election, those sorts of things. All of those would be illegitimate. The rules of the House are very clear about the clerk of the House accepting the properly given certificates of election for members of the House for the purpose of the roll-call vote at the beginning and electing the speaker. There is a very prescribed way of handling contests in the House of Representatives, and there is no legitimate, legal way for the current Congress to be the judge of those elections. Now, one of the things I think will stoke the political fires, though, is the investigations into any contest that any member would want to bring in the 119th Congress. There will be pressures in the current Congress for House Speaker Mike Johnson to highly politicize those investigations, use them as opportunities to shine a spotlight on California and other states that are not cooperating with the administration over noncitizens on the rolls, for instance. Or on states with high levels of mail voting. It’s going to unleash this political theater in hopes of blowing up regular procedures that are very clear, much more clear than the Electoral Count Act was in 2020, into something that looks like a rejection of this election. And the closer it is, the higher the stakes and the higher the temperature. Despite having serious concerns about what the Trump administration will do in the event Republicans lose control of Congress, you’re optimistic that the 2026 midterms will be resilient enough to withstand a Trump bashing. Why? It starts with the decentralized nature of election administration. When I’ve been asked about whether the Trump administration is going to be successful in overthrowing this election, I’ve generally responded with two words: Feckless—they’ve not been very good at what they’ve done; and capacity—there are just not enough lawyers left in the Department of Justice, or enough immigration agents, there’s not enough of anything to take over the American electoral system. If it’s a matter of mobilizing the federal government, there are more local election officials running elections than there’s the capacity of the Trump administration to go after enough of them. That’s decentralization. The second thing is that we continue to see evidence that the facts-based institutions of our electoral system continue to hold. That includes state and local election officials who are nothing if not careful about following the law. And there are the county boards of elections; we’ve seen general election boards stand by the canvassed results. There have been several states that have passed laws over the last few years reinforcing the notion that the certifications of elections at the local level are ministerial and that the proper place for conducting contests, if something is seen to be amiss, is in the court system, not in the election boards. Finally, I think we continue to see the courts holding to what the law is, holding to the precedents that have been established in the past about what do you do when someone contests the results of an election. There’s established law there, and trial courts have, on the whole, held, and when they haven’t, they’ve been admonished or overturned on appeal. It would be nice if we didn’t have to say, “Well, at the end of the day, we’re relying on the Alaska Supreme Court.” It would be much better if it didn’t have to get that far, but we do still have a robust and independent judiciary that has been willing to stand up to nonsense. We hope you learned a thing or two from this edition of Executive Dysfunction, and if you enjoyed reading it, please consider supporting our legal journalism by becoming a Slate Plus member! If Democrats gain control of the House and Senate, this will be one of the first issues they will need to grapple with, and Frank Bowman, law professor and former federal and state prosecutor, tells Dahlia that Trump’s actions are definitely impeachable. However, Congress is likely to face obstacles that may just be insurmountable. In the Amicus bonus episode, Dahlia is joined by Madiba Dennie of Balls and Strikes to discuss Supreme Court Justice Elena Kagan’s biting remarks about originalism, plus a flash of positive court news that includes a rejection of the Trump administration’s ability to harm transgender inmates in an emergency. States that have struggled to enforce strict abortion laws have found a new avenue to force their agenda, and it focuses on controlling speech. Mary Ziegler, law professor at the University of California, Davis, writes in Slate about a new frontier around reproductive rights that’s brewing for after the midterms. Ohio Republican candidate for governor Vivek Ramaswamy’s decision to run an ad accusing his Democratic opponent Amy Acton of lying about sexual abuse she endured at the hands of her stepfather gets at a dark and damning trend: We don’t believe children. Dale Margolin Cecka, associate professor of law at Albany Law School, explains in Slate the statistics around child sexual abuse and how Ramaswamy turned Acton’s vulnerability into a campaign tactic.
