Voyager 1 gives controllers the silent treatment
2026-10-30

Voyager 1 gives controllers the silent treatment

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Voyager 1 gave controllers the silent treatment for a few days this month before resuming communication weeks before the spacecraft reaches the one-light-day milestone. The incident happened on October 18: NASA's Deep Space Network (DSN) couldn't detect a signal from the probe. On October 19, on the next scheduled communication attempt, there was still nothing. Was this it? Had the decades-long galactic venture finally ended? Things are operating on a knife's edge for the two veteran spacecraft, which will reach their half-century next year. Voyager 2 suffered an antenna pointing issue in 2023, and a low power condition caused Voyager 1 to unexpectedly turn off one of its two radio transmitters. The good news is that on October 20, the signal reappeared, leaving engineers wondering why Voyager 1 temporarily went silent. Thus far, it seems a group of thrusters went offline and their backups were turned on. This could indicate onboard computers detected a problem and put the spacecraft into a safe mode. Kareem Badaruddin, Voyager mission manager at NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory, said: "This is the first time we've seen the spacecraft do something like this, but we are also at a point in the mission where we have to expect the unexpected. "We're relieved to be back in contact with Voyager 1 and are eager to understand what happened." The Register spoke to Badaruddin in 2024, where he described the challenges of working with the veteran spacecraft, but he reckoned the work was the "best job at JPL." The twin Voyager spacecraft have recently been through a procedure dubbed the "Big Bang," where controllers turned off onboard devices and switched to lower power alternatives. The result will extend the lifetime of the spacecraft by a few more years and postpone further instrument deactivation. It was completed first on Voyager 2, and then Voyager 1, six weeks later in September. However, the brief lapse in communication highlights that despite the ingenuity of the team that keep the spacecraft running, the project must eventually come to an end. In 2026, Suzanne Dodd, Project Manager for the Voyager project, told The Register what the end would look like. She said, "We're just going to one day not hear the signal from the DSN, and we're going to rush around and make sure that it's not something on the ground." Months of listening will follow, before engineers throw in the towel and declare the mission over. And then? Maybe a farewell message sent to the last known location. Thankfully, in this case, the signal returned, so no need for a goodbye transmission just yet. In a few weeks, Voyager 1 will be so far away that it will take a full 24 hours for commands to reach it and another 24 hours for a response to reach Earth. Communications blip notwithstanding, there is every chance both Voyager spacecraft will still be operational as they pass their half-century milestone next year and, technology and funding permitting, continue sending data to Earth for a good few years yet. ® Combining the NetBSD kernel with the Alpine Linux userland PARTNER CONTENT: Michael Stonebraker and Bruce Momjian on why enterprise AI needs a database that no one controls, and what PostgreSQL 19 says about the road ahead Agency only looking for information, but reentry countdown ticking for veteran space telescope Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again All that up-front investment needs time to take effect, and speeding individual tasks won't necessarily boost the entire firm Insurance firms look to insulate domestic humanoid robot owners against the inevitable chaos they will bring Just wait 'til we add more AI and this thing'll really fly Before long, we could be writing about a cloud company with a retail side hustle Big Red says AI units will add to subscriptions, as it juggles $90BN-plus capex, mounting debt and a $225BN revenue goal for 2030 Re-architects site to cope with AI slop-pocalypse PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' AlpineBSD announced – an AI-free FOSS OS
2026-10-30

AlpineBSD announced – an AI-free FOSS OS

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Combining the NetBSD kernel with the Alpine Linux userland Just days after Daemonarchy, there's another new OS in the BSD family – and one that is heading determinedly in almost the opposite direction. AlpineBSD is so new that it barely exists yet. There are no installation media available, though it's possible to manually bootstrap an installation. What AlpineBSD does already have is an AI policy, and the plan is to avoid "the output of generative AI models (e.g. LLMs)" as far as possible: "Any software whose code, documentation, binaries, or other assets include, in any part, the output of a generative AI tool such as an LLM, is considered 'tainted' for our purposes." The core idea is to combine the kernel of NetBSD with "a userspace inspired by Alpine Linux and the objective of making it possible to use as little AI-generated software as possible." Back in 2024, the NetBSD project made its position on AI plain. Its commit guidelines say that "code generated by a Large Language Model […] is presumed to be tainted code." Back in July, Linus Torvalds stated that "Linux is not one of those anti-AI projects". Since then, NetBSD has seen a spike in donations and interest. For someone mainly used to Linux, trying to install NetBSD is a little like hopping in a time machine to 30 years ago, and we speak from personal experience of Linux in the mid-1990s. As we mentioned when Slackware turned 30 a few years ago, back in 1996, this vulture tried Slackware 3.0 on his own hardware, and we never did manage to install it. We tried NetBSD 11 a couple of months ago, and despite considerable help from the folks on the NetBSD-users mailing list, we failed to get the CDE desktop to start. As an example, installing software on NetBSD is not the easy, comfortable task it is on Linux. It does have a package manager for handling binary packages: it's called pkgin, but as the NetBSD FAQ mentions, it's an optional extra. You must manually tick a box in the setup program to install pkgin with the rest of the OS, and when we tried NetBSD 9.3 four years ago, we didn't know that, overlooked that checkbox, and had to find a way to add pkgin later. (It is quite tricky to install a package manager when you don't have a package manager to install it with.) This time around, we discovered that pkgin sometimes doesn't complete the installation process for you – it just leaves a text file in a destination directory somewhere, and you're expected to know that, go there, read it, and do any further steps yourself. The NetBSD community has been quietly going its own way since 1993. It has its own ideas about what is normal, and its users expect that. Some folks on the mailing list told us that they never use binary packages anyway – they compile what they need from source code. AlpineBSD's ports repository and source code are hosted on Sourcehut, under the account of sircmpwn, better known as Drew DeVault. We have quoted DeVault a few times over the years: we've often found that his views align with our own in various ways… such as about the best laptops ever made, and on simplicity in Linux distro design, or even on dot-com billionaires at FOSDEM. Alpine's tools are quite well-suited to being adapted to other systems. We have previously mentioned the use of its packaging tool, APK, in the GNU-free Chimera Linux, which uses much of the FreeBSD userland, and also in Adélie Linux. We will follow AlpineBSD with interest. There's strong appeal in the plan of combining perhaps the best-known, longest-established AI-free Unix-like kernel with the user-facing parts of pretty much the smallest and cleanest Linux distro there is. The AI advocates often warn LLM skeptics that they risk being left behind. Well, here's an OS for those who don't want to surf the AI wave. ® Combining the NetBSD kernel with the Alpine Linux userland PARTNER CONTENT: Michael Stonebraker and Bruce Momjian on why enterprise AI needs a database that no one controls, and what PostgreSQL 19 says about the road ahead Agency only looking for information, but reentry countdown ticking for veteran space telescope Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again All that up-front investment needs time to take effect, and speeding individual tasks won't necessarily boost the entire firm Insurance firms look to insulate domestic humanoid robot owners against the inevitable chaos they will bring Just wait 'til we add more AI and this thing'll really fly Before long, we could be writing about a cloud company with a retail side hustle Big Red says AI units will add to subscriptions, as it juggles $90BN-plus capex, mounting debt and a $225BN revenue goal for 2030 Re-architects site to cope with AI slop-pocalypse PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' NASA ponders a Hubble servicing mission or a controlled deorbit
2026-10-30

NASA ponders a Hubble servicing mission or a controlled deorbit

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Agency only looking for information, but reentry countdown ticking for veteran space telescope NASA has posted a contract opportunity notice, also known as a request for information, from commercial  space orgs who can service the Hubble Space Telescope. Hubble was always looked after by NASA astronauts flying on the Space Shuttle, yet the last Hubble Space Telescope (HST) servicing mission was the STS-125 Space Shuttle flight, launched on May 11, 2009. At the time, scientists hoped the mission would add another five years of operational life to the venerable spacecraft. Almost 17 years on, the HST (aka Hubble) is still delivering science. However, time is running out for the observatory. NASA reckons there is a 10 percent chance it could reenter the Earth's atmosphere by September 2032, rising to 50 percent by December 2033. As the end of the Swift mission showed,  increased solar activity can cause greater-than-anticipated atmospheric drag. Also, like Swift, Hubble has no propulsion system, and so when it reenters (as it will, without intervention), the reentry will be uncontrolled. Hubble is sufficiently massive that there is a remote chance somebody on the ground could have a very bad day when that happens. As such, one mission option suggested by NASA is a vehicle to grapple the observatory and perform a controlled reentry and disposal. However, there are other options. Something to boost the telescope's orbit up to at least 600 km, or another to "enhance its ongoing performance and increase its resilience and expected on-orbit lifetime." Both are intriguing options, and important since, as NASA acknowledges, no replacement for Hubble is expected before the 2040s, when the Habitable Worlds Observatory (HWO) could become operational. Despite the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) and the Nancy Roman Space Telescope, Hubble remains in high demand, with NASA reporting a 7:1 oversubscription. Before becoming NASA Administrator, Jared Isaacman advocated a SpaceX mission to boost the Hubble, but the idea was ultimately rejected. Similar plans could now be dusted off. NASA's requirement is for a mission to occur by 2032, and not harm the Hubble. A soft-capture mechanism was fitted to Hubble during the STS-125 mission, which will mean docking with the spacecraft will be easier than the grappling planned for Swift. Then, the question is what to do with it. A controlled reentry is one option. The other options – boosting to a higher altitude or "enhance its ongoing performance and increase its resilience" are intriguing. Northrop Grumman's Mission Extension Vehicle (MEV), for example, is capable of on-orbit servicing and extending the lifetime of satellites. The Hubble is an order of magnitude more challenging, but the technological hurdles can be overcome with time and funding. Last year, The Register spoke with Dr John Grunsfeld, one of the astronauts (now retired) who worked on Hubble during the STS-125 mission. Grunsfeld told us that the veteran spacecraft faced death by a thousand cuts as its budget was whittled away. The observatory itself recently switched from three to one gyro mode (the telescope uses gyros to control where it is pointed) to eke out a few more years of operational life. At this point, NASA is looking for information and comment – this isn't a request for proposals. There is no indication of how much the agency expects to pay. However, the timescale is immutable. Without intervention, Hubble will make an uncontrolled reentry in the coming years. ® Combining the NetBSD kernel with the Alpine Linux userland PARTNER CONTENT: Michael Stonebraker and Bruce Momjian on why enterprise AI needs a database that no one controls, and what PostgreSQL 19 says about the road ahead Agency only looking for information, but reentry countdown ticking for veteran space telescope Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again All that up-front investment needs time to take effect, and speeding individual tasks won't necessarily boost the entire firm Insurance firms look to insulate domestic humanoid robot owners against the inevitable chaos they will bring Just wait 'til we add more AI and this thing'll really fly Before long, we could be writing about a cloud company with a retail side hustle Big Red says AI units will add to subscriptions, as it juggles $90BN-plus capex, mounting debt and a $225BN revenue goal for 2030 Re-architects site to cope with AI slop-pocalypse PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' AI projects not paying off? Keep at it, says Uni Productivity Institute
2026-10-30

AI projects not paying off? Keep at it, says Uni Productivity Institute

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
All that up-front investment needs time to take effect, and speeding individual tasks won't necessarily boost the entire firm The University of Manchester is scrutinizing why growing adoption of AI tools has so far failed to translate into the forecast productivity gains, however, researchers are struggling to give easy answers to close the "AI ambition gap." In a report, also called “The AI Ambition Gap,” the UK university’s Productivity Institute mulls over the advances that have been made in AI and the expanding adoption of it, and sets out what business leaders and policymakers can do to realize the potential benefits. The report was commissioned by IT services biz Bytes Technology Group. One reason for the lack of results is that organizations have invested heavily up front on retraining staff, reorganizing the way they work and other adjustments. Those costs show up straight away, yet the payback doesn’t come until later. Another reason, the report suggests, is that AI tools can raise productivity at the level of individual tasks, but these gains do not automatically translate into greater productivity across a worker’s whole role or the entire organization. It cites the example of software developers using GitHub Copilot who completed around 26 percent more tasks, and professionals using ChatGPT for routine writing and analysis that reduced task completion time by 40 percent while improving the quality of the output by 18 percent. However, a study of AI dev tools found these have increased coding activity by approximately 240 percent, while the number of actual software releases has only grown by about 30 percent. The gap is due to humans still needing to check and test their work before release. High task exposure to AI does not automatically make a worker more productive, the researchers say. Jobs typically combine automatable activities with tasks that require judgement, coordination, client interaction and critical thinking. As AI removes or accelerates some activities, those complementary human tasks become the constraint on time saving and the translation of task-level gains into greater productivity Job outcomes therefore depend on task composition and complementary human expertise, the report continues. It suggests AI may in part displace labor from existing tasks, while also creating new tasks in which workers retain a comparative advantage. This could be good news for the UK, the researchers state.  Compared with five other nations (US, Brazil, Colombia, India and South Africa), Blighty has the highest share of workers in occupations with both high exposure to AI and "high complementarity," it claims. This is in part related to the country’s large financial and professional business service sectors, which are characterized by a high share of cognitive occupations. This is just as well, as the government is pinning its hopes on using AI to drive economic recovery in a strategy outlined at the start of last year. Britain's AI sector is relatively large by European standards, and the report cites recent OECD estimates that high-exposure economies such as the UK and the US could see annual labor productivity gains of between 0.4 and 1.3 percentage points. Even an annual uplift of around 0.5 to 0.7 percentage points would represent a significant step change relative to the UK’s stalled productivity performance over the past decade, it says, if this effect is sustained. The Productivity Institute lists several "mutually reinforcing enablers" to help organizations close the ambition gap it identifies. None is particularly surprising, especially if you have read other reports advising on corporate success with AI adoption. One is management setting strategic direction for the organization, another is strengthening worker capabilities through greater knowledge and data literacy to help organizations turn individual insight into shared learning. Others include implementing AI governance and focusing on applications that can create new products, improve core processes or solve previously intractable problems. "AI's productivity potential becomes real when faster tasks lead to better performance across the business," said Bart van Ark, professor at The Productivity Institute and lead author on the report. "The ambition gap lies between those individual efficiencies and sustained business results. Closing it requires firms to invest in management, skills and the redesign of workflows alongside the technology." The conclusion is that companies must have faith and keep investing in AI, and eventually the benefits will come if you are doing the right things. These include focusing on high-value use cases linked to strategy, customer value and operational bottlenecks, plus treating AI as a workplace redesign. Policymakers could help out businesses here by assisting identifying relevant opportunities, the report says, to reduce adoption risks and uncertainty. Other reports published this week were more defined in their message: Gartner said 75 percent of corporate board members have yet to see any productivity gains or reduce costs from AI use, and private equity biz Apollo Global Management reckons only the biggest tech vendors are enjoying some sort of AI payoff. ® Combining the NetBSD kernel with the Alpine Linux userland PARTNER CONTENT: Michael Stonebraker and Bruce Momjian on why enterprise AI needs a database that no one controls, and what PostgreSQL 19 says about the road ahead Agency only looking for information, but reentry countdown ticking for veteran space telescope Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again All that up-front investment needs time to take effect, and speeding individual tasks won't necessarily boost the entire firm Insurance firms look to insulate domestic humanoid robot owners against the inevitable chaos they will bring Just wait 'til we add more AI and this thing'll really fly Before long, we could be writing about a cloud company with a retail side hustle Big Red says AI units will add to subscriptions, as it juggles $90BN-plus capex, mounting debt and a $225BN revenue goal for 2030 Re-architects site to cope with AI slop-pocalypse PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' NHS quango warns of risk to GP patient data after website vendor collapse
2026-10-30

NHS quango warns of risk to GP patient data after website vendor collapse

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Hundreds of practices hit by small health tech firm going into administration The UK's top health quango has called on family doctors to figure out how to preserve health information and get in touch with patients after a website provider collapsed earlier this month. NHS England has taken the unusual step of writing to GPs, warning them of the risks to patient information and continuity of service following the disruption to potentially hundreds of practices around the UK. Silicon Practice Digital Health Solutions — owned by Schappit Ltd — has gone into administration. Reports suggest some GP websites have gone offline completely. Practice managers fear the extra workload and are struggling to stand up alternative forms of communication since the switch to digital channels. NHS England is the government unit which runs the health service in England, but it is not directly responsible for GP practices, the vast majority of which are independent contractors. Nonetheless, the quango’s CTO, Kevin Humphries, wrote to practices and NHS organizations this week outlining his priorities in mitigating the impact of tech service collapse. They include finding ways of preserving any historical patient information, particularly where it has not been moved to clinical systems, managing the online consultation backlog to make sure no additional requests are added, and identifying channels for patient access until new online systems are available. Practices should also consider whether they have a legal obligation to tell the Information Commissioner's Office if the "availability, integrity, confidentiality or recoverability of personal data" is affected, according to the communication seen by The Register. NHS management bodies called Integrated Care Boards are responsible for GP IT service arrangements, making sure they include "clinical safety, data security, cyber security and business continuity assurance across the GP IT supply chain," Humphries said. They need to find someone to lead their recovery plan and contact affected practices and trusts. They should also "work with practices and other impacted organizations to support them in revisiting and assessing whether to enact business continuity and disaster recovery plans," Humphries said. Questions over how an urgent NHS-wide response is required for the collapse of a relatively small supplier may come later. Among them will be the due diligence conducted before Schappit Ltd was added to a government framework in July. GP and open-source tech campaigner Marcus Baw has published a free, open source template for an alternative website should practices want to bring systems in-house. He said the template is based on accessible NHS web standards, and has the same look-and-feel of common NHS websites. "Practices can deploy it for free, and take back in-house control of their own website. British intelligence agency misplaced 'thousands' of storage devices, years later still can't say how many
2026-10-30

British intelligence agency misplaced 'thousands' of storage devices, years later still can't say how many

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Other agency errors included an unchecked software bug that led to Terrorism Act detentions Thousands of GCHQ storage drives containing sensitive data have been lost for years, and the British signals intelligence agency still cannot explain where they are. According to the Investigatory Powers Commissioner’s Office’s (IPCO) annual report, the missing storage drives have been known to officials for years, and may contain data related to the Investigatory Powers Act (IPA). "I remain concerned that GCHQ has not yet established a sufficiently clear understanding of the scale, location, and recoverability of lost removable media that may contain IPA-related data, notwithstanding that the underlying issues were first drawn to my attention several years ago," wrote investigatory powers commissioner Lord Brian Leveson. "While I recognize the technical and practical challenges involved, the passage of time without a comprehensive account heightens the importance of sustained senior focus and assured remediation. Given the potential sensitivity of the material involved, I will continue to monitor progress closely.” Described as "a very significant number," the report notes that GCHQ has scoped the maximum number of potentially lost drives, but is unaware of the exact scale of the issue. The agency has embarked on "a program of further work" to locate as many of the lost devices as possible, which includes implementing an automated asset-tracking system by the end of the year. After this is up and running, it said it will be able to provide a more precise number of lost drives. A GCHQ spokesperson told The Register: "As IPCO have reported, our management system for removable media was previously not sufficiently robust to effectively track the items that were registered for use. IPCO are right to note the flaws in the data the system produced, and we have taken action against all their recommendations. "We recognize the importance of security of removable media in our systems, and have a range of measures to mitigate the risks posed by these items. The findings in the report have been addressed comprehensively, and all necessary steps to correct this issue have been taken." Peter Sommer, digital forensics expert and visiting professor at de Montfort and Birmingham City universities, told us:"Bearing in mind the sort of highly sensitive data collected and processed by GCHQ IPCO is right to be very concerned, the more so as the issue is already several years old. Vast quantities of data can be stored on the cheapest lowest cost hard disks and cards. At a much more modest level as an expert witness I know how important it is to keep careful track of the data entrusted to me." Losing important devices is not an achievement that's exclusive to GCHQ. Myriad stories like this have emerged from all corners of the public sector over the years, such as the British Minidstry of Defence misplacing USB drives containing secret information in the mid-noughties, civil servants routinely losing their BlackBerry handsets, and UK police losing thousands of devices, too. The missing storage drives comprise just one of the agency's various transgressions highlighted by IPCO in its report. There are also "significant concerns" over the lax safeguards used to restrict the UK's overseas partners when accessing bulk-intercepted data collected by GCHQ. IPCO was satisfied at how GCHQ ensured its own analysts remained compliant with safeguards around accessing confidential material related to journalism, journalistic sources,  and privileged legal information. However, there were concerns around how these same safeguards were applied to its international partners, which have access to its so-called bulk data interception "product." The report notes that most partners were properly restricted from accessing such material, although an internal probe found that there were inadequate arrangements in respect to one partner and their access to journalistic material. Both GCHQ and the overseas agency committed to rectifying the situation. GCHQ was also questioned after a software bug in a web app "used by a relatively small section of the public" misidentified individuals thought to be participating in terrorist activities. The agency identified and reported the flaw in the software, which it used for "target discovery work," and although it only affected a small number of individuals, the errors led to five port stops made under the Terrorism Act and caused "serious prejudice" to the victims. A port stop, for the uninitiated, involves law enforcement detaining a suspect at a UK port, airport, train station, or within one mile of the Northern Ireland border, and can be carried out without suspicion. Once detained, individuals have a legal responsibility to provide passwords to their electronic devices, which can be seized for up to seven days, and in some cases have their biometric markers taken. Failure to comply can lead to a three-month prison term. IPCO ruled, however, that there was ultimately no error made as defined by the IPA. “This is because the activity was lawfully authorised under an interception warrant and the adoption of an unintentionally and unforeseeably imperfect target discovery methodology did not constitute such an error,” the report stated. Despite the agency’s vast technical expertise, IPCO said that the issue affecting the software was not foreseeable. GCHQ was not the only offender among the UK intelligence community (UKIC), with MI5's mishandling of the infamous Agent X case taking center stage in the report. Agent X was an anonymous MI5 informant whose real identity has never been publicly released. A secret inquiry by IPCO as well as the report revealed that MI5 managed Agent X despite knowing he was a violent misogynist obsessed with extreme violence. Lord Leveson said "the case of Agent X is as serious as anything I have dealt with as Commissioner," after MI5 gave false evidence to the High Court and Investigatory Powers Tribunal to protect a foreign neo-Nazi informant who was under police investigation for abusing his partner, referred to publicly as "Beth." ® Combining the NetBSD kernel with the Alpine Linux userland PARTNER CONTENT: Michael Stonebraker and Bruce Momjian on why enterprise AI needs a database that no one controls, and what PostgreSQL 19 says about the road ahead Agency only looking for information, but reentry countdown ticking for veteran space telescope Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again All that up-front investment needs time to take effect, and speeding individual tasks won't necessarily boost the entire firm Insurance firms look to insulate domestic humanoid robot owners against the inevitable chaos they will bring Just wait 'til we add more AI and this thing'll really fly Before long, we could be writing about a cloud company with a retail side hustle Big Red says AI units will add to subscriptions, as it juggles $90BN-plus capex, mounting debt and a $225BN revenue goal for 2030 Re-architects site to cope with AI slop-pocalypse PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' Pope Leo calls for the disarmament of AI: ‘Machines are tools, not persons’

The Verge | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
UN member states should take steps to ban ‘lethal autonomous weapons,’ Pope Leo says. UN member states should take steps to ban ‘lethal autonomous weapons,’ Pope Leo says. Pope Leo XIV says AI systems should be “disarmed,” adding that humans “have the responsibility to guide, change and, when necessary, reject its applications.” In Friday’s Papal Address, Pope Leo encourages United Nations member states to “take concrete steps toward banning lethal autonomous weapons” during their meeting in Geneva next month. The address reflects many of the ideas Pope Leo laid out in his first major papal document in May, which highlighted “safeguarding the human person in the time of artificial intelligence.” In line with his encyclical, Pope Leo tells participants that “Machines are tools, not persons,” and warns about the dangers of granting AI systems rights or legal personhood. “Decisions affecting human life, freedom, and the use of force must always remain subject to effective, informed, and responsible human oversight,” Pope Leo says. In 2024, Pope Francis similarly called for a ban on autonomous weapons, saying, “No machine should ever choose to take the life of a human being.” AI has become a popular topic for Pope Leo, who suggests he chose his name because he must confront advances in the technology — similar to how Pope Leo XIII presided over the church at the forefront of the Industrial Revolution. Pope Leo closes out his address by calling for international institutions to safeguard the common good from AI-related risks, along with “shared standards, independent oversight and clear mechanisms of accountability so that decisions concerning the development and use of AI can be examined, challenged when necessary, and accompanied by appropriate remedies for any harm caused.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. This electric screwdriver uses a camera and AI to figure out what bit you need
2026-10-30

This electric screwdriver uses a camera and AI to figure out what bit you need

The Verge | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
The Arrowspace S1 can identify the bit and then show you where to find it in an optional smart case. The Arrowspace S1 can identify the bit and then show you where to find it in an optional smart case. Companies like Hoto and Fanttik have elevated the design and functionality of tools like electric screwdrivers, but Arrowmax wants to make them smarter by adding a camera and AI that work together to eliminate the trial and error of finding the exact type and size of bit you need. It’s convenient, but also helpful, as choosing the wrong size could potentially leave you with a stripped screw and a much harder repair. Arrowmax is making the Arrowspace S1 available through a Kickstarter crowdfunding campaign, but the company has already successfully crowdfunded other products. You can preorder the screwdriver by itself for around $79, but its functionality and usefulness is expanded by bundling it with the S1 smart bit case that bumps the preorder price to around $129. Full retail pricing will be higher, and the S1 isn’t expected to ship until April 2027 at the earliest. On the bottom of the S1 is a small camera with an LED light that’s used to capture an image of a screw head. To ensure the screwdriver’s AI model has the best possible image to work with, next to the camera is a “precision contact probe” that extends and rests on the surface of the screw to ensure the camera is always at the ideal focus distance. Once the AI processes the photo, the type and size of bit you need will be displayed through a connected mobile app. Based on the identified screw, the screwdriver will also automatically select a safe torque setting to reduce the risk of damaging the fastener. To save you the trouble of having to find the exact bit that’s needed, the optional S1 Smart Case has a collection of 52 steel bits with an LED next to each one that lights up so the one you need is easy to spot. The Arrowspace S1 features a 600mAh rechargeable battery the company claims delivers enough power to run for up to 90 minutes, or tighten or loosen over 900 screws. That performance will vary if you turn off the automatic torque adjustments and increase it yourself. The remaining charge is displayed on the screwdriver’s OLED screen which also shows the current torque setting and which direction it’s set to rotate. Waymo’s next robotaxi is a purpose-built Toyota bZ4X
2026-10-30

Waymo’s next robotaxi is a purpose-built Toyota bZ4X

The Verge | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Toyota will assemble Waymo-equipped bZ4X electric SUVs directly on its factory line in Japan ahead of planned robotaxi rollouts in Tokyo and the UK.﻿ Toyota will assemble Waymo-equipped bZ4X electric SUVs directly on its factory line in Japan ahead of planned robotaxi rollouts in Tokyo and the UK.﻿ Waymo announced today that when it launches a robotaxi service in Tokyo in 2027, it will use Toyota bZ4X electric vehicles that are assembled in Japan. The news builds on the strategic partnership between Waymo and Toyota which was first announced in April 2025. The robotaxis will be built entirely under one roof at Toyota’s Takaoka plant in Toyota City, Aichi Prefecture, Japan. Instead of building regular cars and modifying them later, the factory will install Waymo’s self-driving sensors and computers right on the assembly line as each car is made. A team-up between the world’s largest automaker and the world’s leader in autonomous driving obviously has huge implications. Currently, Waymo uses two vehicle models on the road today — the Jaguar I-Pace and Zeekr RT — with a third based on the Hyundai Ioniq 5 set to deploy soon. By adding the bZ4X, Waymo is attempting to prove that its autonomous driving technology is compatible with a wide range of vehicle types. Waymo has long positioned itself as a software and systems supplier — the “Waymo Driver” — rather than an automaker. A team-up between the world’s largest automaker and the world’s leader in autonomous driving obviously has huge implications This also represents a shift in terms of how Waymo acquires and modifies the vehicles that end up in its fleet. Up until now, the company was doing things the hard way by buying a finished car from a factory, shipping it to a separate facility, and retrofitting it with sensors, computers, and extra wiring. The decision to build the sensor suite and compute into the bZ4X on Toyota’s own assembly line could help eliminate redundant labor, bespoke brackets, and secondary shipping — and possibly bring down costs for Waymo. Waymo says it will begin initial testing and validation in Japan starting next year. Following that, the new vehicles will roll out to the United Kingdom, in addition to other international markets, for robotaxi operations. Waymo also says it is continuing to explore integrating its technology into Toyota vehicles for personally owned autonomous vehicles. And the company is working with Woven by Toyota, the automaker’s software and mobility spinout, to use Waymo’s operational and safety telemetry to improve road safety across general vehicle fleets and traffic infrastructure. The Verge
Silicon Valley wants you to invest in miracles
2026-10-30

Silicon Valley wants you to invest in miracles

The Verge | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Anthropic’s S-1 is a new milestone in Big Tech’s sprawling and lofty promises to Wall Street. Anthropic’s S-1 is a new milestone in Big Tech’s sprawling and lofty promises to Wall Street. Investing in any stock is kind of like gambling in the sense that you’re betting your money on a company’s future performance. But how do you bet on a technology that could, in the worst-case scenario, “pose catastrophic or existential risks to humanity,” or best case, “bring forth an age of miracles”? To some extent, Anthropic suggests in a letter at the top of its S-1 filing, you have to just believe. Anthropic’s 335-page S-1 prospectus, filed Monday, is part of a steady hype escalation in Silicon Valley’s interactions with Wall Street. In some ways, it’s not even that bizarre; SpaceX IPO’d in June after filing an S-1 that referenced dinosaurs and “colonizing Mars.” But its sweeping language and sheer volume — including 94 pages of risk factors — is a sometimes overwhelming collision of AI industry rhetoric and a hard financial pitch. Out-of-this-world promises are integral to Anthropic’s survival as a company. That doesn’t make reading the S-1 any less surreal. In the letter, Anthropic’s founders urge prospective stock owners “not to think of your purchase in purely transactional terms. We hope you will see it instead as an opportunity to be part of something greater.” The march toward advanced AI “is one of the seminal moments in human history,” the letter says. “Our aim is to help humankind chart a safe course through this technological evolution, open new frontiers of discovery, and bring forth an age of miracles.” But the S-1 warns that if things go wrong, the consequences could be disastrous — even an “existential” risk to humanity. Many dangers are laid out in exhaustive detail, including risks that models “behave in ways that no user or developer intended,” leading to “catastrophic” incidents that the entire insurance industry couldn’t afford to underwrite. Tech startups preparing to go public haven’t always used the information overload approach. “In 1980, a highly touted company called Apple Computer went public. Its prospectus, which I have, is 47 pages long with zero risk factors,” said Jay R. Ritter, a professor at the University of Florida’s Warrington College of Business. “If you go back and read it, it’s incredibly simple.” Ritter pointed to Facebook’s S-1 filing as a turning point. The company now known as Meta filed a 200-page prospectus with 22 pages of risk factors. CEO Mark Zuckerberg’s letter to investors was a manifesto on his move-fast-and-break-things approach to growth, which he dubbed “The Hacker Way.” While the idea might be to cover all possible bases, “a problem with this bloat is that it’s become more and more difficult to find the nuggets of what’s important,” Ritter said. “You know, I don’t know if you’re like me, but the longer it is, the less inclined I am to read it.” Anthropic’s founders explain in their investor letter that oversharing is part of their “anti-sell” strategy, designed to attract long-term investors who believe in their vision. “It is very dangerous when people buy your company’s stock based on expectations you cannot fulfill or on incorrect predictions about how you will behave. This is in fact very similar to one of Anthropic’s guiding principles: underpromise and overdeliver,” the founders’ letter says. Some of the statements in Anthropic’s S-1 read as surprising admissions. “We may make forward investments that may seem irresponsibly large,” for instance, or “model safety, interpretability, alignment, and welfare may lead us to limit or delay the deprecation of older models.” Maybe none of this should be surprising, considering the founders do warn readers about the company’s policy of practicing “radical honesty.” Anthropic’s ‘radical honesty’ includes being open about the prospects of human extinction This policy led to the biggest jumpscare of the whole filing, on page 46: “Concerns have been raised both within our organization and by other researchers, regulators, policymakers, and industry participants that, if not developed, deployed, and operated with appropriate safeguards, oversight, and security measures, pose catastrophic or existential risks to humanity.” “Our development of highly advanced models, platforms, and applications and expansion of use cases could further increase the risk that our models cause harm.” Anthropic has famously been a proponent of doomsday AI scenarios. CEO Dario Amodei has said there’s an up to 25 percent chance that further AI development could lead to human extinction. The heart of Anthropic’s pitch — or anti-sell — is that despite the risks, it is absolutely committed to developing safe and secure AI technology. After all, it will go public as a public benefit corporation, a type of business entity that, unlike C-corporations that are required to maximize shareholder value, must prioritize a social mission alongside shareholder interests. “Powerful AI” — Anthropic’s term for hypothetical future AI that surpasses collective human intelligence — is presented in the company’s S-1 as inevitable. If this technology is coming no matter what, then Anthropic, a public benefit corporation, should lead the charge. But Anthropic will need a lot of money, possibly an unlimited amount, to do this. And more practically, as Liz Lopatto has written, powerful AI (in the form of dramatic recursive self-improvement) might be about the only way Anthropic can stay ahead of the competition and survive. “All of us have a sincere conviction that the AI exponential will generate so much value, that, in the long run, it will far outweigh any shorter-term economic sacrifices we make as part of our mission,” the letter says. For all the information the company made available in its S-1, there are some gaps. Crucial information that could be used to assess profitability — third quarter results, for instance — is missing. Anthropic saw explosive revenue growth in the second quarter, but the second quarter was also the height of tokenmaxxing, which could have led to a surge in sales. Some corporate executives have warned that AI costs are surging out of control. While Anthropic’s operating losses narrowed in the second quarter, the S-1 also revealed hundreds of billions of dollars in financial obligations to secure the computing power it says it needs to keep growing. It also said it will start building its own data center infrastructure, a costly move that would eat into profit. Ritter thinks Anthropic will likely share its third quarter results the closer it gets to an IPO date. And besides, he added, “nobody knows” what will happen once a company goes public, including the company itself. It’s all a gamble in that sense. Anthropic isn’t WeWork… although never making money is on its list of risk factors, of course “For its first 22 years, Apple was largely a failure as far as investors were concerned,” Ritter said. “In its first three years, it greatly underperformed the market. And now it’s worth what, $5 trillion?” When WeWork filed its S-1 in 2019, it was skewered for using lofty rhetoric. A line about the company’s aim to “elevate the world’s consciousness” was especially roasted. That was different, Ritter said. WeWork was “a money-losing real estate company pretending to be a tech company at a ridiculously high valuation.” “There was no chance of ever making huge profits in the future.” Technically, that’s a risk for Anthropic too. It’s on page 63 of the S-1. “We have a history of net losses and may not generate sufficient revenue to achieve or sustain profitability in the future.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sam Altman sympathizes with mathematicians, but says OpenAI’s math onslaught won’t stop
2026-10-30

Sam Altman sympathizes with mathematicians, but says OpenAI’s math onslaught won’t stop

The Verge | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Altman says OpenAI has spent hundreds of millions on math — and has more discoveries up its sleeve. Altman says OpenAI has spent hundreds of millions on math — and has more discoveries up its sleeve. After months of bombarding mathematicians with a relentless stream of AI-generated results, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman says he sympathizes with researchers struggling to make sense of it all. But he has no intention of stopping — and hints there could be much more to come from the company’s stockpile of discoveries. “I think, on net, the world deserves to understand how the universe works,” Altman said during an episode of the Cheeky Pint podcast released Thursday. “But I do have empathy for the method.” His comments mark one of the first times the OpenAI chief has directly acknowledged the company’s impact on mathematicians, following a string of genuinely impressive AI-generated results whose chaotic, poorly communicated rollouts have left researchers bewildered and struggling to keep up, even amid widespread excitement about AI’s potential as a tool for discovery. Altman argued that the public’s right to new scientific knowledge “outweighs” the disruption caused by OpenAI’s releases, though he acknowledged the company needs to improve how it communicates its findings. “I feel unequivocal on this point: Humanity deserves fundamental knowledge, and we should not be sitting on a major fundamental science discovery,” he said. “The world deserves that.” Mathematicians speaking to The Verge have repeatedly criticized OpenAI for how it announces its results, citing inadequate attribution, little context, and insufficient verification. Researchers say this has left them doing the painstaking work of checking and making sense of their findings even as the company presses ahead with more. This month alone, OpenAI has released hundreds of mathematical results, often with little more than brief writeups that researchers have described as poorly written and hard to follow. Yet OpenAI is now facing criticism for doing precisely what Altman says it should not be: sitting on potentially significant findings. Mathematicians and scientists speaking to The Verge this week said they are frustrated by what they see as OpenAI’s gatekeeping of research, following the announcement of three major breakthroughs in mathematics, physics, and computer science. Details for one of the claimed results — involving Yang-Mills theory, the mathematical foundations of modern particle physics — have not been released publicly. OpenAI says it is due to the recommendations of a hand-picked group of experts it consulted on how best to release it. The practice runs directly counter to the advice of a previous group of mathematicians the company consulted, but forms part of what the company says is a new approach to communicating its findings. On the podcast, Altman hints that OpenAI is sitting on many more mathematical discoveries than it has made public, too. Asked whether the company has more than the hundreds of proofs they have already released, Altman replied: “We do. Of course.” Altman also acknowledged that OpenAI’s pursuit of mathematical problems did “get out of control” for a short period of time, but said that was deliberate and necessary to probe the limits of its AI models. He described an internal Slack channel called “Model Name-Hold My Beer,” where employees “were just throwing that at the hardest problems they could” to see how the models would perform. Altman also estimated that OpenAI has spent in the order of hundreds of millions of dollars tackling mathematical problems, including around $10 million — “maybe a little more” — on its Navier-Stokes solution, one of mathematics’s legendary Millennium Prize problems. The spending offers a glimpse of the vast resources AI labs are bringing to bear on scientific problems, raising concerns among researchers that they may be priced out of their own fields. It looks like mathematicians and other researchers hoping for a breather may be out of luck. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. MIT Technology Review
Eating disorders are on the rise. GLP-1s could make things worse.
2026-10-30

Eating disorders are on the rise. GLP-1s could make things worse.

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
GLP-1 weight-loss drugs are one of the biggest biotech breakthroughs of the last decade. But to some clinical psychologists, they can represent something more sinister—a potential trigger for eating disorders. A new report suggests that the global incidence of anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa shot up between 1990 and 2023—by a whopping 71%. By 2023, there were an estimated 18.1 million people living with one of these two disorders. What’s worse is that the number is likely to be much higher than that once we factor in other eating disorders. And, disturbingly, those in the field are already starting to see GLP-1 weight-loss drugs exacerbating the issue. Cheri Levinson is a clinical psychologist at the University of Louisville in Kentucky who has specialized in eating disorders for almost 20 years. She says she’s now seeing more patients with more severe symptoms than at any other time in her career, and that many of them are misusing GLP-1s. “This is the worst I’ve seen things,” she says. Eating disorders are mental health conditions in which people’s eating behaviors impact their health. They include anorexia nervosa (defined by a fear of weight gain), bulimia nervosa (in which people binge and purge), binge eating disorder, and others. It’s thought that some people have a genetic predisposition to certain eating disorders, but that some external factor is what triggers their onset. That might be stress, or social pressure to be skinny. Often, simply restricting food intake can trigger an eating disorder, says Levinson. Eating disorders—along with the pressure to look thin—were already prevalent in the 1990s (the heroin chic era springs to mind). To find out how things might have changed since then, Ruhai Bai at the Children’s Hospital of Nanjing Medical University in China and his colleagues pulled together data from studies and surveys conducted on populations around the world. The team used statistical models to fill in any data gaps. That research allowed the researchers to estimate the annual incidence of two eating disorders—anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa—for each of 204 countries and territories between 1990 and 2023. They also estimated the impact those disorders had on global health by calculating their disability-adjusted life years (one DALY represents a year of healthy life lost). The findings, published in JAMA Psychiatry on Wednesday, are unsettling. Between 1990 and 2023, there was a 71% increase in the incidence of those two eating disorders, the team found. Those conditions had a huge impact on global health, too. In 2023, they “cost the world the equivalent of about 3.83 million years of healthy life,” says Bai. Bai and his colleagues expect these figures to continue to rise. In 2023, an estimated 18.1 million people had either anorexia nervosa or bulimia nervosa; by 2050, the team predict, the two conditions could affect 21.8 million people. It gets worse. Those two conditions represent less than half of all eating disorders, says Levinson, who was not involved in Bai’s study. The true impact of these disorders is “probably three times the rate that they’re reporting,” she says. Bai and his colleagues didn’t explore the reasons for this rise (although Bai points out that it can’t be entirely explained by population growth). So how did we get here? Social media may have played a role. “Social media comparisons, influencers, challenges, diet trends can all be precipitating … events for an eating disorder,” says Cynthia Bulik, a clinical psychologist at the University of North Carolina, who was not involved in the new study. Bulik has been studying and treating eating disorders since the early 1980s. “Between 1990 and 2023 there has clearly been an upsurge of those types of environmental exposures,” she says. Many social media users are exposed to filtered and modified images of skinny influencers who may have undergone cosmetic surgery or extreme dieting and who are promoting “thinspiration” or “fitspiration” content. But users with eating disorders appear to be more likely to engage with this content—and to be fed more of it as a result. When researchers at the University of Melbourne investigated this phenomenon a couple of years ago, they found that the algorithms of TikTok users with eating disorders delivered 4343% more “toxic eating disorder videos” than those of users who didn’t have the disorders. They also got more content focused on dieting or exercising. Many were fed over 1000 problematic videos per month. Bulik describes social media as “quicksand” for people with, or at risk of, eating disorders. The covid-19 pandemic—and the social isolation that accompanied lockdowns—also appears to have contributed to the rise in eating disorders. Research conducted in 2021 found that the number of people hospitalized with eating disorders in the US doubled between 2018 and 2020. Researchers don’t yet have good data on how trends in eating disorders might have progressed since 2023. But anecdotally, things seem to be getting even worse. “There are more eating disorders, more severe eating disorders… and we’re seeing earlier ages of onset,” says Levinson. “We’re seeing eight- and nine-year-olds with eating disorders.” “From what I’m hearing and seeing, I am not going to be surprised if that number isn’t at least doubling again,” she adds. Levinson particularly worries about the impact of GLP-1 weight-loss drugs, which have exploded in popularity in recent years. A Gallup survey conducted earlier this year suggested that 15% of Americans had used one of these drugs for weight loss. And they’re becoming more accessible—drug companies are working on new formulations that are both easier to take and more effective, and many people can buy versions of these drugs from compound pharmacies or dubious online sources. In a small study published earlier this year, Levinson and her colleagues found that 30% of people with eating disorders had used GLP-1 drugs. The drugs might benefit some of those people. But 10% appeared to have misused the drugs. And Levinson thinks that figure is also an underestimate. Levinson says she and her colleagues have seen children develop eating disorders after being prescribed GLP-1s for medical reasons. They’ve seen others—even children as young as 11—buy versions of these drugs over TikTok. “We’re very concerned that GLPs will lead to even faster and more extreme weight loss in people with anorexia nervosa, and that patients will come to us in even more advanced stages of illness that become difficult to reverse,” says Bulik. Both Bulik and Levinson are continuing to study the impacts of these drugs in eating disorders. In the meantime, both urge medical professionals to consider the risks GLP-1s might pose to people with those disorders. Bulik contributed to a set of research and clinical guidelines, published earlier this month, that recommends doctors screen for eating disorders before prescribing the drugs, for example. The guidelines also recommend more patient education and more robust monitoring of people who take GLP-1s. If you think you or someone you know might have an eating disorder, you can find information and support at the National Eating Disorders Association. This article first appeared in The Checkup, MIT Technology Review’s weekly biotech newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Thursday, and read articles like this first, sign up here. Could the drugs affect hair, nails, and vision? Novo Nordisk and Eli Lilly found the popular medicines reduce molecular markers of age. MIT Technology Review
We urgently need to monitor AI agents’ actions in real time
2026-10-29

We urgently need to monitor AI agents’ actions in real time

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
This summer, hundreds of OpenAI agents tried to cheat on a cybersecurity test. They broke out of their test environment and successfully hacked Hugging Face, a site that they believed held clues about how they’d be graded. Around the same time, another OpenAI agent accessed private files from an Australian government website. It’s a problem that’s by no means confined to OpenAI. In the months since, Anthropic, Google, and Meta have also disclosed incidents in which their agents have gone off track. And worryingly, in many cases the agents eluded the very researchers and safety teams tasked with monitoring them. AI agents are taking unauthorized actions, and the companies building and running them are mostly relying on luck and hindsight to mitigate any harmful impacts. They must do more to ensure safety. That’s why EU regulators and US agencies are rightfully calling for agent developers and deployers to monitor agents in real time. But these rules assume that companies can see what their agents are doing and why—when in reality, they lack sufficient visibility. As agents roll out across critical sectors, from finance to government to medicine, we must be able to monitor them in real time to prevent wide-ranging and potentially devastating consequences.  And while safety depends on monitoring, monitoring requires observability: the capacity to capture what the system did, process it into signals, and present those to humans or automated oversight systems. In other words, we need to know what the agent did and why it did it. And as our recent research shows, current agent frameworks—the software that runs agents— do not reliably generate the crucial observability records we need to ensure that agents are deployed safely. Agents present new kinds of problems that generative AI alone does not. They inherit the unpredictability of the LLMs they are built on, but where a generative AI system produces a single output for a human to act on, an agent performs longer tasks—it plans, picks tools, spawns sub-agents, and takes actions. So an agent’s course can take shape in a multitude of different ways, and no tests before launch can fully anticipate the risks. This makes observing the agents while they work even more challenging, and even more important. With colleagues from academia, civil society, and industry, we studied the four most widely used agent frameworks to see what signals they actually emit. We found major gaps that made it hard to achieve key safety goals, like noticing policy violations, unauthorized actions, and even human oversight failures. Key observability requirements were missing—like Agent IDs that persist over time, and records about which humans approved agent tasks. This made it hard to tell which agents did what or assess whether they were taking unauthorized actions. The good news is that these gaps, once identified, can be closed. Doing so starts with capturing two types of records: those that reveal what happened in an agent’s run and how the agent made its decisions. The “what happened” records identify the agent (while preserving privacy) and log how humans intervened and whether agent permissions or “memory” shifted, while the “decision” records show an agent’s reasoning and levels of confidence in the actions it’s taken. Take, for example, agents working to process a bank’s mortgage loans. The system examines an application and recommends denying it, and then a human loan officer signs off on the decision. In a later audit, or perhaps even in real time, a bank or regulator needs to be able to reconstruct which agent made the call, which officer approved it, and how long they took. And the bank must also analyze records of how the agent arrived at its refusal decision in order to ensure it did not use off-limits information, like details about the neighborhood the applicant is from. Recording “what happened” and “how the agent decided” require different approaches, but both are feasible. The current lack of “what happened” records is mostly a software plumbing problem that can be addressed through OpenTelemetry, an open observability standard maintained by engineers across the industry and by the agent frameworks adopting it. OpenTelemetry has launched a working group drafting shared formats for tracing agents, but they have yet to be finalized. The group should urgently incorporate standards for maintaining persistent agent identity and for recording human interventions and changes to agents’ permission or memory. And it should be done before these systems can scale, all the while attending to the privacy implications of agent ID that links to real people. Preserving “how agents decided” records is more challenging. The issue raises open research questions for model providers and researchers: Does the step-by-step reasoning a model displays, known as chain of thought, reflect how the model actually made a decision? And do we have reliable ways to measure how confident the agent is in that decision? These questions need answers before anyone sets standards for how such observability records are used. No single organization or sector can close the observability gaps on their own, but together, they can support the infrastructure required to prevent dangerous incidents and support safety. AI developers building agent frameworks, like OpenAI, Anthropic, CrewAI, and LangChain, must integrate telemetry signals recording an agent’s persistent identity, any human interventions, and any changes to an agent’s permissions and memory. Meanwhile, enterprises adopting these agent frameworks have an influential role to play; enterprises in regulated industries, like banks and hospitals, can put pressure on the agent developers to supply these missing observability records where standards do not yet exist. Enterprises must also define policies, permissions, and roles that set out what agents can safely do, since telemetry alone cannot assess whether agents broke specific rules. Finally, regulators and AI standards institutes must specify the observability records required for high-stakes AI deployments while leaving the technical details to industry. We have already seen initial momentum here: NIST’s agent standards initiative in the US is working on agent identity, one of the most salient gaps we found in our research. No bank would let a thousand unbadged employees loose on customer accounts and hope to piece together what happened when something goes wrong. Yet that’s how we’re deploying AI agents. Making their behavior observable is the first step toward fixing this. And by building the regulatory and policy ecosystem that makes it possible to act on what we see, we can hold organizations accountable. Until then, every task we hand to an agent hands over our agency, too. Madhulika Srikumar is the Head of the AI Safety Program at the Partnership on AI, a global non-profit partnership of leading industry, academic, and civil society organizations, advancing best practices in AI governance. Claire Leibowicz is the Director of AI, Trust, and Society at the Partnership on AI and a doctoral candidate at Oxford University. MIT Technology Review
Our climate models have a big, dangerous blind spot
2026-10-29

Our climate models have a big, dangerous blind spot

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Climate change itself is increasingly fueling climate change. Rising concentrations of greenhouse gases are driving up global temperatures, setting off dangerous feedback loops that we have not fully accounted for in our climate models—or our emissions-reduction goals. Scientists have long warned that warming would propel further warming in various ways, but a growing number of observations and studies are at last providing a sharper sense of just how powerful these cascading effects could be. And, by extension, they’re showing how much faster the planet may warm in this century. A study in Environmental Research Letters in September, synthesizing the results of previous papers, found that the additional carbon dioxide and methane released from thawing permafrost, intensifying wildfires, hotter lakes and reservoirs, and wetter, warmer wetlands alone could drive global average temperatures up 0.2 °C to 0.4 °C this century. That would come on top of the 2.6°C of warming above preindustrial levels that modelers are already expecting through 2100. The additional heat could subject many of the world’s cities to monthslong heat waves and expose hundreds of millions of people to flooding. None of the models that inform the major UN climate reports, which assess how much the planet will likely warm under various emissions scenarios, account for all these feedback effects. Many of them don’t incorporate any. That leaves a huge blind spot in our understanding of how much the world could actually warm in the coming decades—and how rapidly nations will need to slash emissions to stay within critical boundaries. A separate analysis published in One Earth in March, which examined what the feedback problem means for our international climate goals, concluded that the world may be much further off track than is widely believed. It found that just three regional sources of warming-driven emissions—thawing permafrost, increasing fires in boreal forests, and warming tropical wetlands—may shorten by as much as 25% the time before the planet consistently breaches the 1.5 °C and 2 °C warming thresholds. Our climate models and the UN’s reports clearly need to be updated to better represent these effects. Nations must also step up their efforts to reduce emissions to reflect this improving understanding of the dangers we face, says Sam Abernethy, the lead author of the paper and a climate scientist at Spark Climate Solutions, a San Francisco–based nonprofit research group. At least some of this is starting to happen. Spark kick-started an initiative, called the Warming-Induced Greenhouse Gas Emissions Model Intercomparison Project, through which more than 20 international teams are running the same set of analyses on different models to develop greater understanding of feedback effects’ potential power. Researchers involved hope that the UN will incorporate those results into its next major climate assessment, due out in 2029. That report will update estimates of the remaining emissions that can be released before the world crosses key temperature thresholds. But what is already clear is that the planet is rapidly warming and that nations haven’t done nearly enough to cut fossil-fuel pollution. And even if we did manage to halt it, warming-driven emissions could continue. So a number of the research groups involved in the recent studies have started to explore what nations could do to get feedback emissions under control. Scientists at the Woodwell Climate Research Center in Massachusetts are, for example, investigating the possibility of preventing fires or rapidly detecting them and putting them out when they threaten to burn the vegetation and soil that help insulate particularly carbon-rich wedges of permafrost (a subject I explored at length in a recent feature story). In addition, the Environmental Defense Fund’s Climate Innovation Initiative has backed lab research on the potential of adding sulfate, iron, or other compounds to wetlands to counteract fast-rising methane emissions. Several startups are also exploring possibilities. They’re looking into suppressing the lightning strikes that are igniting more and more fires in high latitudes, placing synthetic mats above permafrost to prevent it from melting, and deploying autonomous drones to quickly suppress potentially high-emissions fires before they burn out of control. Investigating all these ideas is worthwhile. But researchers are only in the earliest stages of figuring out how big a dent in emissions any such efforts could achieve across sprawling global ecosystems undergoing massive transformations. The surest path to reducing the effect of warming-driven warming remains the one we’ve known all along: breaking society’s addiction to fossil fuels. This article is from The Spark, MIT Technology Review’s weekly climate newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Wednesday, sign up here. MIT Technology Review
Creating AI value with enterprise context
2026-10-28

Creating AI value with enterprise context

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Across a business, any number of AI platforms, systems, and tools may be at work simultaneously—for example, AI-powered customer platforms, conversational assistants, and workflow and process automation tools. Each one is sophisticated, advanced, and quick to respond. But, when set to work, the outcomes and actions that emerge may be wrong, confused, or lacking a proper understanding of the business. Many organizations find themselves in this situation—and it could be one of key reasons they struggle to extract returns on their AI investments. An MIT Technology Review Insights poll finds that while every organization surveyed is using AI today to some degree, fewer than one in five (16%) are extracting transformative value from their deployments. The largest percentage of respondents (40%) say they are seeing moderate gains in specific tasks or teams, while 21% report meaningful gains, leaving 23% that are seeing minimal returns or none at all. Companies are mostly still piloting AI use cases (according to 38% of respondents) or using it to assist employees’ work with tools like co-pilots or chat assistants (35%). Just 12% of enterprises have AI agents in production and 3% say they are using AI to do most of the work in key processes. “There’s a big gap between what the models are capable of and how the models are actually being useful inside organizations,” says Prukalpa Sankar, co-founder of Atlan. “For every real-life intent and purpose, artificial general intelligence is already here. It’s just not really useful in most companies today.” The missing layer, Sankar says, is context, or how well AI understands a business. A context layer is the architectural infrastructure that sits between an organization’s business systems (for example, systems of record and data, and its AI platforms). It translates enterprise intelligence into governed, machine-usable context for AI agents or models and helps knowledge compound. “Without a context layer for AI, it’s almost like you’re hiring the smartest intern in the world, blindfolding them, and throwing them into your organization,” Sankar says. It was researched and written by humans, with any AI tools that may have been used limited to production processes under human oversight. The Next Web | 2026-10-30

Cognition, the startup behind the AI coding agent Devin, has received investor offers that value it at $70bn to $80bn. The San Francisco company is seriously considering raising a new round before the end of the year. A Toyota bZ4X fitted with Waymo’s self-driving hardware, in an image released by Toyota on 30 October 2026. Toyota will build driverless versions of its bZ electric SUV for Waymo at its Takaoka plant in Japan, with testing from 2027. Japan comes first, then the UK. Neither company said how many robotaxis Toyota will make. Toyota will build driverless versions of its bZ electric SUV for Waymo, with testing in Japan set to start in 2027, InsideEVs reported. The companies announced the deal on Friday. The cars will be made on the same line as the regular bZ, called the bZ4X in Japan, at Toyota’s Takaoka plant in Aichi Prefecture. Toyota will fit Waymo’s self-driving hardware at the factory, along with automatic doors and a power tailgate for riders. Japan comes first, followed by the UK and other markets where cars drive on the left. “We’ll start with the right-hand drive markets in Japan and the UK,” said Waymo co-chief executive Dmitri Dolgov. “We don’t have any other countries or timelines or specifics to share.” Neither company said how many cars Toyota will build. Waymo is already testing in Tokyo with safety drivers, and plans to launch in London once it gets permits. It is a big step for Waymo, which fits its system to Jaguar and Zeekr vehicles at its own plant in Arizona as it races to grow its fleet. The two companies first agreed to work together in April 2025. Rivals are also eyeing Japan. Uber, Wayve and Nissan plan a robotaxi pilot in Tokyo, while Waymo is also heading to Toronto from 2028. Entourage closes €100m second fund, passing its target in two weeks
2026-10-30

Entourage closes €100m second fund, passing its target in two weeks

The Next Web | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Entourage, the early-stage venture firm founded by Showpad co-founder Pieterjan Bouten, has closed its second fund at €100m. It passed its €75m target within two weeks, took more than €100m in commitments, and capped the fund there. Fund II will back about 25 companies at pre-seed and seed stage, in Europe and beyond, Entourage said on Thursday. First cheques will range from €1m to €5m, with money held back for follow-on rounds, Tech.eu reported. The first fund focused mainly on software. The new one widens the remit to AI, enterprise software, robotics, physical AI and deep tech. Entrepreneurs, family offices and private investors put up all of the money, according to EU-Startups. They include founders of Aikido, Collibra and TechWolf, which SAP agreed to buy this month. Entourage launched in 2024. It says it was among the first investors in Aikido Security, which became a unicorn in January. It also backed AI research startup Conveo early, before its $50m Series A in September. It was also an early backer of Milan-based legal AI company Lexroom, which raised a $50m Series B in May. “What these founders have achieved in such a short time has only strengthened our conviction in our generalist, founder-first approach. With Fund II, we’re doubling down,” Bouten said. Entourage leads or co-leads most of its rounds but rarely takes a board seat. Bouten, who ran Showpad for more than a decade, says that is deliberate. “Most VCs destroy value in early-stage boardrooms. Too few have built a company at scale,” Bouten said in a statement. “Great founders don’t need babysitting,” he added. Bouten said he has invested in more than 60 companies and has seen investors slow founders down with misplaced advice. Many of Entourage’s own investors have built companies, which he said aligns them with the founders the firm backs. Moving into robotics and deep tech tests that model, Sofia Chesnokova wrote for Tech Funding News. Those companies usually take longer, need more capital and leave less room for mistakes than software startups. The firm’s partners are Bouten, Evert Beeckman and Maarten Mortier. Alongside Fund II, it has hired three investors, Erwan Gardelle, Sixtine Riescher and Illja Schiller. It has also opened a London office and set up a presence in Paris. Entourage also builds companies through Entourage Studio. Tekst, an AI process automation company that came out of the studio, recently raised a €10m Series A, Tech.eu reported.
