EU plans to let Frontex monitor social media and flag posts for removal
2026-10-30

EU plans to let Frontex monitor social media and flag posts for removal

The Next Web | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
The European Commission wants Frontex, the EU’s border agency, to monitor public social media posts about migration. It would also flag illegal content to platforms. MEPs and digital rights groups say the plan puts online speech under a border agency’s watch. The Commission proposed the change on Tuesday as part of a wider overhaul of Frontex. EU tech chief Henna Virkkunen told reporters that Frontex would get an online monitoring and response centre for public social media content, Eliza Gkritsi reported for Politico Europe. The centre would watch for news relevant to border management. It would also support action against illegal content linked to border security risks. Frontex would also act as a trusted flagger under the Digital Services Act, the Commission said in its press material. Platforms must deal quickly with content reported by a trusted flagger. National regulators grant the status after checking an applicant’s expertise and independence. Virkkunen later clarified that Frontex would have to apply. She said she expected regulators to approve it because of its “special expertise”. The agency could also ask national authorities to issue removal orders. The rest of the overhaul is about deportations. Frontex’s standing corps would grow to 30,000 officers. The agency would also support returns from one non-EU country to another, The Brussels Times reported. The Commission also wants to double Frontex’s budget over the next seven years, according to Politico. In July, thousands of migrants crossed into Ceuta, the Spanish exclave, within a few hours. Social media played a central role in that crisis, Politico reported. Italian social democrat MEP Brando Benifei told Politico it was legitimate to remove online adverts for people smuggling. “But a broad social media surveillance mandate goes much further, placing online speech under a border agency’s watch,” he said. A 2019 tender with similar monitoring powers was revoked after privacy criticism. Benifei said the Commission now proposes “copying ICE’s invasive online tracking”, a reference to the US immigration agency. That agency’s staff details surfaced this week in a Homeland Security leak. “The DSA’s trusted flagger program was never conceived for police forces to circumvent due process when assessing the legality of people’s online expression,” said Jan Penfrat of the digital rights group EDRi, according to Politico. The criticism crosses party lines. Polish right-wing MEP Piotr Müller told Politico the mandate must target smugglers, not opinions about migration. Fabrice Leggeri, a former Frontex director now sitting for France’s far-right National Rally, called it an attempt at censorship. Irish centre-right MEP Regina Doherty told Politico that border authorities need tools against smugglers. Some use social media to promote dangerous crossings, she said. She said the priority is a clear legal framework. German centre-right MEP Andreas Schwab said the EU should use the DSA rather than add new tools. Commission spokesperson Guillaume Mercier told Politico that any monitoring would stay limited to border and migration management. The DSA already lets regulators fine platforms that fail to act on illegal content. The proposal now goes to the European Parliament and EU governments in the Council for negotiation. It adds to a long-running EU tension between safety and privacy laws. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription. STMicroelectronics forecasts $4.15bn quarter as AI data centres lift demand
2026-10-30

STMicroelectronics forecasts $4.15bn quarter as AI data centres lift demand

The Next Web | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
STMicroelectronics’ site in Agrate Brianza, Italy. STMicroelectronics expects fourth-quarter revenue of $4.15bn, up about 10.5% on the third quarter and above analysts’ forecasts. The Franco-Italian chipmaker says AI data centres and satellite makers are adding to a wider recovery in chip demand. Third-quarter revenue rose 17.9% to $3.76bn, ST said on Thursday in its earnings statement. Operating income excluding one-off charges nearly doubled to $429m. Analysts polled by LSEG had expected $400.5m, Nathan Vifflin and Fabrizio La Rocca reported for Reuters. “In an environment where demand remains well above supply, we continue to expand capacity aligned with ST’s strategic roadmap,” chief executive Jean-Marc Chery said in the statement. The midpoint of the forecast puts full-year revenue at about $14.5bn, up 22.8%. ST names AI data centres and low-Earth-orbit satellite links as its own growth drivers. Chery told analysts that ST now expects about $1.3bn in data centre revenue this year, Reuters reported. In July, it had forecast more than $1bn. He said silicon photonics chips, which use light to move data between servers, drove most of the demand. ST kept its 2027 goal of well above $2bn. The earnings statement itself did not carry the new figure. Writing early on Thursday, Citi analyst Pavan Daswani said there was no update to the data centre outlook, Bloomberg reported. Revenue in ST’s RF and optical communications unit rose 46.2% to $504m. Journalists asked Chery whether heavy AI spending can last. He said the data centre business depends on the biggest cloud companies, not the smaller neoclouds. The business is “much more related to the top five hyperscalers than the neo-cloud business”, Chery said, according to Reuters. Gross margin rose to 37.3% from 33.2% a year earlier. A better product mix and less idle factory capacity helped. ST expects about 39% in the fourth quarter. Supply is tight and input costs are rising, so ST is raising prices on some products, finance chief Lorenzo Grandi said, Reuters reported. Chery said ST now plans equipment, materials and chemicals three to four years ahead. A push for “sovereign” supply chains free of China, Taiwan or the US makes that planning more complex, he said. Chery told investors he has accepted a supervisory board proposal to stay on as chief executive for another year, Christina Kyriasoglou reported for Bloomberg. Shareholders will vote on it at the 2027 annual meeting. In February, the board hired a headhunter to find his successor, according to Corriere della Sera, as cited by Bloomberg. In a separate announcement, ST named Remi El-Ouazzane chief technology officer and Marco Cassis chief strategic officer. Both changes take effect on 1 January 2027. ST shares barely moved in Paris on Thursday morning, Bloomberg reported. The results follow a strong week for chipmakers riding AI demand. Samsung posted record profit on Thursday, and SK Hynix’s profit jumped on Wednesday. Rival Infineon committed €1.1bn to an Austrian plant on Tuesday. Ofcom names four age checks for the UK’s under-16 social media ban
2026-10-30

Ofcom names four age checks for the UK’s under-16 social media ban

The Next Web | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Ofcom has named four age checks it says can reliably tell whether UK social media users are 16 or over. They will underpin the UK social media ban for under-16s due next year, and many adults will have to pass them too. The regulator published its assessment on Thursday. The ban is due to start in March. It will cover ten of the biggest platforms, including Instagram, TikTok and Snapchat, Zoe Kleinman reported for the BBC. The government will decide which checks to require by the end of the year. It announced the ban on under-16s in June. Photo ID matching compares a document such as a passport with a live image taken during the check. Bank checks use the age a bank holds on record. About 90% of UK 16 and 17-year-olds have a bank account, Ofcom estimates, The Register reported. Digital ID services verify a person once, then confirm their age to other services. Facial age estimation has a margin of error of a few years. Ofcom suggests platforms back it up with a second method. Three checks that work for adults did not make the list: credit cards, mobile network checks and email-based checks. Each one only proves a user is 18. Ofcom also judged self-declaration, contract terms and parental consent ineffective, TechRadar reported. All four methods need some form of photo ID. Ofcom estimates that about 94% of 16 and 17-year-olds have one, such as a passport or a driving licence. Those without one would need to get one. Ofcom will not prescribe a single method. Chief executive Melanie Dawes told the BBC that countries which set strict rules for porn sites found they did not work. “Here are things that can work, they must preserve privacy, they must protect people’s data, but it’s on you to put in place what you think works for you,” Dawes told the BBC. Platforms may infer some adults’ ages from their past activity. But Ofcom said “many” adults will have to verify, according to The Register. The regulator told the BBC it has no firm figure, but expects a “large number of accounts”. Meta told the BBC it wants checks done once on the phone, through the app stores run by Apple and Google. Users would then not have to prove their age on every platform. Ofcom said that would not free the platforms from running their own checks. Ofcom accepts that some children will get round the checks. It plans easier reporting of suspected underage users, age inference models, and 12 months of monitoring once the ban starts, The Register reported. Australia’s ban, in force since December 2025, failed early age checks in testing. “This ban will shape the future of children’s lives online, so it’s important to get it right,” Dawes said in a statement. Silkie Carlo, director of Big Brother Watch, called the plan “a national ID system by the back door”, according to The Register. “The plan will kill anonymity online,” Carlo said. Pieter Arntz of Malwarebytes told TechRadar that photo ID matching creates “another attractive target for criminals”. He pointed to a breach at a Discord support provider that exposed about 70,000 ID images. Child safety groups welcomed the guidance, the BBC reported. The NSPCC said tech firms must be held to the standards. Andy Burrows, chief executive of the Molly Rose Foundation, said Ofcom had a “dire track record” of enforcing the Online Safety Act. Other European countries are moving the same way. Greece passed its under-15 ban last week, and the EU has its own age verification app. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.
